Composites RGB 221 (day), 123, 321, 125, or one channel; the node shows the latest part, **Save PNG** saves the whole pass; `img` (every 2 s, when wired) and `lines`.

The generator's LRPT mode carries a real MSU-MR stream: a test picture (a different pattern per channel) encoded into segments, packets and M_PDUs; it comes back with a mean error under 2 levels (QF 80).

Presets: *Meteor-M LRPT: Image (Generator)*, *Meteor-M LRPT: Image (USB SDR, 137 MHz)*.

## Radiosondes (RS41)

**RS41 Radiosonde** (Decoders, worker) receives Vaisala RS41 weather balloons at 400–406 MHz. It follows zilog80's `rs41mod` ([rs1729/RS](https://github.com/rs1729/RS), GPLv3), the decoder behind radiosonde_auto_rx.
- **Input:** raw IQ at any rate (decimation, ±10 kHz channel, FM discriminator), or a real stream that is already FM audio, e.g. a recording from a scanner.
- **Frames:** GFSK 4800 Bd. The 64-bit header is found by normalized correlation over fractional bit windows, in either polarity; bit timing is tracked on transitions.
- **Decoding:** 64-byte XOR mask, RS(255,231)×2 (GF 0x11D) for 320- and 518-byte frames, then CRC-16 blocks: status (frame number, serial, battery, one of 51 calibration pieces), PTU, GPS week/time, ECEF position and velocity.
- **Output:** records per frame with a position (`id`/`label` = serial, `lat`, `lon`, `alt` m, `climb`, `vh` m/s, `heading`, `sats`, `frame`, `batt`, `temp` °C and `rh` % once the calibration pieces have come in, time from GPS) with a balloon icon for the map. The node lists the sondes it hears.

Checked on real data: the two sample frames from rs1729/RS (RS corrects exactly the two bytes it should, serials, positions), and a 6 s recording of RS41 L1830070 (the recording is not in the repository). Every frame matches `rs41mod`'s output to its last printed digit, temperature included.

Presets: *Radiosonde RS41: Map (Generator)*, *Radiosonde RS41: Map (USB SDR, 400–406 MHz)*.

## MPT 1327

**MPT 1327 Decoder** (Decoders, worker) reads the control channel of analogue trunked systems (MPT 1327, common in UK, Eastern Europe and Asia).

- **Input:** IQ (any rate, decimated to ~24 kS/s inside; put *IQ Frequency Shift* before it if the channel is off-centre, ±11 kHz is passed) or an already demodulated FM audio.
- **Chain:** FM discriminator → slow DC block → FFSK 1200 Bd (1 = 1200 Hz, 0 = 1800 Hz, tone energies in a one-bit window) at 8 bit-clock phases → sync word (`0xC4D7` control, `0x3B28` traffic, one error allowed; an inverted signal is recognised by the CRC) → 64-bit codewords: CRC-15 (polynomial `0x6815`, xorout 1) and overall even parity. Data codewords are taken only right after a valid one.
- **Output:** records `{chan, kind: address|data, pfix, ident, raw, text}`; the node lists the last codewords, counts and the polarity.
- **Not done:** the message layer (ALOHA, GTC, ACK…) is not interpreted — only PFIX/IDENT of address codewords and the raw 48 bits are shown. The generator sends test codewords with valid CRC, not real system messages.

Presets: *MPT 1327: Control Channel (Generator)*, *MPT 1327: Control Channel (USB SDR)*.

## ISM 433

**ISM 433 Decoder** (Decoders, worker) decodes short telegrams of the 433.92 MHz band (also 315 / 868 / 915 MHz): weather sensors, door and gate remotes, doorbells. It is a base for adding protocols, not a replacement of a catalogue like `rtl_433` — for that use [ISM Scanner](#ism-scanner-rtl_433), which carries rtl_433 itself.

- **Input:** IQ at any rate (decimated to ~250 kS/s inside; put *IQ Frequency Shift* before it if the signal is off-centre, keep it off the DC spike of the SDR).
- **Slicer:** channel filter (*channel width*) → envelope for OOK, frequency for 2-FSK (the level is the middle of the seen frequency span). The threshold follows the peak and the noise floor (needs *min. signal over noise*), with hysteresis; runs shorter than 30 µs are merged with the neighbours. A packet ends after a silence longer than *end of packet* (default 12 ms).
- **Analyzer:** the pulse train (mark, space, … in µs) is clustered by duration. A rare long space with a regular step splits the packet into repeats (rows). Then the encoding is chosen: **PWM** (two mark widths, constant period), **PPM** (two space widths), **MAN** (Manchester, widths are multiples of T, 01 = 1 / 10 = 0, phase per row) or **PCM** (the same lattice, raw levels). If the durations do not fall into a few clusters (noisy FSK, NRZ without gaps), the bit period T is found from the periodicity of the run lengths and the bits come out as PCM; runs longer than 12 T end a segment.
- **Protocols:** `EV1527 / PT2262` (PWM, 20-bit address + 4 data bits, tri-state view of PT2262) and `Nexus` (TFA / Digitech-type clones: id, channel, battery, temperature, humidity). The most frequent row among the repeats wins (`agree` of `nrows`). A protocol is a function `an → fields | null` in `ISM_PROTOS` (`modules/ism-kernels.js`): it gets the rows of bits and the timings.
- **Unknown packets** (*log unidentified packets*) are logged too: encoding, bit count, hex, short / long / gap in µs. Use them to find out the timings of a device and write its parser.
- **Output:** records `{t, src:'ISM', mod: OOK|FSK, proto, kind: sensor|remote|unknown, id, text, freq, foff, rssi, snr, enc, pulses, short, long, gap, …fields}`; identical packets within *skip identical packets* are dropped (by the stream time, so it works on recordings too). Rec Log saves CSV, *Rec: Unique by Key* (key `id`) keeps one line per device.
- **Not done:** the protocols were checked against the generator and hand-made vectors, not against real devices — check a new one with your own captures. Only mark-first, single-frequency packets are handled; a trailing run of zeros in FSK is lost (it looks like silence); no CRC-based protocols yet (Oregon, LaCrosse, Fine Offset…); OOK+FSK mode gives duplicates and false unknown packets.

Presets: *ISM 433: Sensors and Remotes (Generator)*, *ISM 433: Sensors and Remotes (USB SDR)*.

### ISM Scanner (rtl_433)

**ISM Scanner (rtl_433)** (Decoders, worker) takes the whole band of the receiver at once and decodes everything [rtl_433](https://github.com/merbanan/rtl_433) knows: about 380 decoders (weather stations and thermometers, TPMS, door / window / motion / smoke sensors, doorbells, remotes and car keys, energy and water meters, …). It is the real rtl_433 — pulse detector (OOK and FSK), all pulse slicers and all device decoders — compiled to WebAssembly (`vendor/rtl433.wasm`, `tools/rtl433/build.sh`; see `tools/rtl433/README.md`), not a rewrite, so the fields are the rtl_433 ones (`temperature_C`, `humidity`, `battery_ok`, `channel`, …).

- **Whole band, in parallel:** RTL-SDR at 2.4 MS/s → a polyphase filter bank (the one of *IQ Channelizer*, WASM SIMD) with **all channels at once** — no slot picking, a sensor burst is shorter than any selection interval. With *channel overlap 4* (default) there are 32 channels 75 kHz apart with a ±100 kHz flat band, 300 kS/s each, so a signal always lies whole in one channel (and weaker in its neighbours); *overlap 2* has 16 channels and half the CPU, but a signal between two channels is split. Every channel has its own rtl_433 detector (own noise and level estimates), so several sensors can talk at the same moment on different frequencies. Quiet channels are not fed to the detector.
- **Levels:** *gain* auto keeps the noise floor at about 300 of 32767 and the strongest signal below 0.7 of full scale (it follows slowly: a step in the middle of a packet would cut it); a fixed gain in dB is possible. The pulse level floor of rtl_433 follows the noise: *min. signal over noise* (20 dB by default; lower finds weaker sensors and gives more false packets); after a packet the floor of that channel follows the level of the packet for 3 s (sensors repeat packets). The centre channel (DC spike of the dongle) is high-passed, or skipped (*centre channel*); an upstream *IQ DC Block* helps.
- **Queue:** decoded events and unidentified packets go into a queue that is worked off with a time budget per tick (6 ms); when it overflows the oldest unidentified packets are dropped first (`lost` in the readout). The same packet seen in two neighbouring channels, or repeated, is dropped within *skip identical packets*.
- **Decoders:** *default* runs the ones rtl_433 runs by default; *all* adds those it keeps off (more false positives); *only these decoders* takes names (substring) or rtl_433 protocol numbers, e.g. `nexus, 19`.
- **Unidentified packets** (*log unidentified packets*): packets no decoder recognized go to the general analyzer of *ISM 433 Decoder* (clusters of durations → PWM / PPM / Manchester / PCM, repeats, bits). The record has `hex`, `bitlen`, `enc`, `short` / `long` / `gap` in µs — feed `rec` to **HEX Structure** (`hex` field) or collect packets of one device in a Table and give them to **HEX Field Finder** to find the fields; that is the way to add support for something rtl_433 does not know.
- **Output:** `rec` — `{t, src:'ISM', mod: OOK|FSK, proto: model, kind: sensor|device|unknown, id: "model:id/channel" (key for Rec: Unique by Key), dev: the rtl_433 id, text, freq, rssi, snr, noise, …rtl_433 fields}`; `spec` — power of every channel (busy ones light up in the analyzer); `active` — busy channels.
- **Checked on:** the single-channel path against the recordings of [rtl_433_tests](https://github.com/merbanan/rtl_433_tests) (`node tools/test-rt433.mjs <path>/tests`): 7503 of 7734 expected events (97 %, 383 decoders; the rest are mostly recordings that need rtl_433's own options or flex configs). The scanner (`node tools/test-ismscan.mjs <path>/tests`) with those recordings interpolated to 2 MS/s and shifted to different offsets in the band: about 80 % of the 40 recordings are decoded one by one and 5 of 6 when mixed together — these are synthetic mixtures, not real wideband captures, so treat the percentages as a rough guide. Real sensors usually repeat a packet several times, which helps more than the single recordings do.
- **Cost:** about 75 % of one core at 2.4 MS/s with overlap 4 (measured in Node on a 4-core container; the polyphase part is WASM SIMD, ~270 ms/s for 32 detectors), overlap 2 and a lower sample rate cost less. The module is 700 KB and is loaded on first use; without it the node says so, the other ISM node keeps working.
- **Licence and use:** rtl_433 is GPL-2.0-or-later, so the WebAssembly module is a GPL part of the project (source: github.com/merbanan/rtl_433, commit 80f7bcc). Receive only. Decoding other people's sensors (neighbours' weather stations, vehicle TPMS ids, alarm sensors) can touch privacy law depending on the country; nothing here transmits or replays.

Presets: *ISM 433: Wideband Scanner, all rtl_433 decoders (Generator)*, *… (USB SDR)*.

## LoRa

**LoRa Decoder** (Decoders, worker) and **LoRa Modulator** (Protocols) implement the physical layer of Semtech's LoRa from open descriptions of it (gr-lora_sdr, LoRaPHY): whitening (LFSR x⁸+x⁶+x⁵+x⁴+1), nibbles low first, Hamming 4/5…4/8, diagonal interleaving, Gray code, cyclic shifts of a base chirp; payload CRC-16 (CCITT over the payload without its last two bytes, XOR with them). The first 8 symbols always carry SF−2 bits at 4/8 (the header), the rest SF bits (SF−2 with low data rate optimization).

- **Decoder input:** IQ at any rate not below the bandwidth, 2× the bandwidth or more is better (put *IQ Frequency Shift* before it to bring the channel to the center). Parameters: spreading factor 7…12, bandwidth 7.8 … 500 kHz, sync word (hex, `any` takes all; LoRaWAN `34`, private `12`, Meshtastic `2B`), explicit / implicit header (then length, coding rate and CRC are set by hand), low data rate optimization (auto: symbol longer than 16 ms — it is not signalled in the header, so it must match the sender), preamble threshold, inverted IQ (LoRaWAN downlinks).
- **How it receives:** channel filter wider than ±BW/2 (a carrier offset moves a chirp edge out of the band) → resampling to 2·BW → de-chirp with a reference chirp and an FFT of 2^SF points. A preamble is the same FFT peak in several consecutive windows. The frame boundary and the carrier offset come from a pair of peaks, up-chirp (preamble) and down-chirp (SFD); the amplitude of the down-chirp over the windows removes the M/2 ambiguity, then the boundary is refined to a fraction of a sample. The windows of the symbols are cut from the fractional position, a PI loop on the phase step between the two halves of a chirp (before and after the frequency wrap) follows the sample clock error. The peak offset cannot be used for this: its shift depends on the symbol value.
- **Output:** records `{t, src:'LoRa', kind:'frame', sf, bw, cr, crc, crcOk, hdr, len, sync, snr, cfo, errs, fixed, text, hex}` (`errs` — Hamming words not corrected, `fixed` — corrected ones; `cfo` in Hz) and the text of frames with a good CRC on `text`. *log frames with CRC errors too* keeps broken ones, marked `crcOk:false`.
- **Modulator:** a frame goes out on a rising edge of `go`, the *Send* button, a change of the `text` input (*auto*) or every *repeat every* seconds; the message may contain `{n}` (frame number), format text (UTF-8) or hex. Sample rate, center frequency, offset, clock error (ppm), level, noise and inverted IQ are set for tests. The `tx` output is 1 while a frame is on the air (HackRF TX takes it). The block is a main-thread block; the decoder runs in the island worker.
- **Checked:** encoder ↔ decoder on all SF, coding rates, header modes and lengths; the receiver on synthesized IQ with a carrier offset up to ±0.25·BW, clock error up to ±30 ppm, noise down to about −5 dB SNR at SF7 and −12 dB at SF12 (our own noise figures, with a wide filter that lets some extra noise in), at 1…8 times the bandwidth (`node tools/test-kernels.mjs`).
- **Not checked:** the receiver and the transmitter were tested only against each other, not against real chips or captures. The bit order of the Hamming code, the interleaver, the header checksum and the CRC byte order follow descriptions from memory of open projects: if a real frame does not decode (or a real chip ignores ours) the first suspects are these. Not done: SF5 / SF6, frequency hopping, LoRaWAN decryption / MIC, several channels at once, a second frame overlapping the first, sensitivity at the level of the chips. The SNR in records is an estimate from the FFT peak and saturates at about 10–30 dB.

Presets: *LoRa: Chirp Link (Loopback)*, *LoRa: Receive (USB SDR, 868 MHz)*, *LoRa: Send a Message (HackRF TX)*.

## AIS

**AIS Decoder** (Decoders, worker) receives the Automatic Identification System of ships on the two marine VHF channels: **A 161.975 MHz** and **B 162.025 MHz** (25 kHz, GMSK 9600 Bd, ±2.4 kHz). A 1 MS/s SDR around 162.000 MHz sees both; pick one channel with *IQ Frequency Shift* (±25 kHz from the centre).

- **Input:** IQ at any rate (decimated to ≥48 kS/s inside; ±15 kHz are passed) or an already demodulated FM audio of at least 48 kS/s.
- **Chain:** FM discriminator → mean of the middle half of every symbol against the mean over the 40 symbols around it (follows the transmitter's frequency error, no DC tracking needed) at 8 bit-clock phases → NRZI → HDLC (flag `0x7E`, zero after five ones) → CRC-16 X.25 (residue `0xF0B8`). A frame is accepted by the CRC only; the same frame from the neighbouring clock phases is counted once. Bytes go over the air LSB first while message fields go MSB first, so the bit order is reversed inside each byte (a frame whose first six bits only make sense without the reversal is read as it came).
- **Messages:** 1 / 2 / 3 (class A position: status, speed, course, heading), 4 / 11 (base station), 5 (static: name, call sign, IMO, type, size, draught, destination, ETA), 9 (SAR aircraft), 18 / 19 (class B), 21 (aid to navigation), 24 A / B (class B static). Other types are counted and give a `!AIVDM` sentence, only the MMSI is read.
- **Vessels:** kept by MMSI (*forget a vessel after*, min): names, call signs and dimensions from static reports are joined to the next position of the same vessel, so the map shows `DEMO CARGO` instead of a number once its type 5 / 24 was heard (class A repeats it every ~6 min).
- **Output:** `rec` — `{t, src:'AIS', id, mmsi, label, lat, lon, icon, heading, speed (kn), course, status, msg, callsign, imo, shiptype, kind, dest, length, width, draught, color}` for every message with a position (icon `ship`, `antenna` for a base station, `diamond` for an aid to navigation; the colour follows the ship type: cargo green, tanker red, passenger blue, fishing orange…); `text` — `!AIVDM` sentences (long messages in two parts) with the channel letter from the parameter, ready for OpenCPN, AIS-catcher and other programs through *Network Out* / *Serial Out*.
- **Not done:** messages 6 / 8 / 25 / 26 (binary), 14 (safety text), 20, 22, 23, 27 are not interpreted; dual-channel reception at once is not done (two decoders on two shifts do it); the receiver was checked against the built-in generator (SNR down to ~6 dB in the whole 256 kHz window, offsets ±3 kHz) and against `pyais` for the field layouts, not against real signals yet.

Presets: *AIS: Vessels on the Map (Generator)*, *AIS: Vessels on the Map (USB SDR, 162 MHz)*.

## NMEA 0183

**NMEA Parser** (Data) turns NMEA 0183 lines into records and numbers. The text comes from *Serial Port (WebSerial)* (a GPS puck, a marine multiplexer), *Text over Network* (`gpsd` → `gpspipe -r | websocat -s 8765`, Termux, an AIS-catcher / OpenCPN relay), a *Table* playing a log, or any other text wire. A line may carry a prefix (a time stamp, `RX `) — the parser starts at the first `$` or `!`.

- **Input:** `text` — the last line (several lines in one block are split); a new line is taken when the text changes, or on a pulse on `go` (wire the *Text over Network* `go` so two identical lines in a row both count).
- **Checksum:** *if present* (default) drops a sentence with a wrong `*HH` and accepts one without; *required* drops those without; *ignore* does not check.
- **Sentences:** `GGA`, `RMC`, `GLL` (position, fix quality, satellites, HDOP, altitude, speed, course, UTC and date), `VTG`, `GSA` (satellites used), `GSV` (satellites in view: PRN, elevation, azimuth, SNR; GPS / GLONASS / Galileo / BeiDou by talker), `ZDA`, `HDT` / `HDG` / `HDM` (heading), `DBT` / `DPT` (depth, m), `MWV` (wind, m/s), `MTW` (water temperature), `TXT`, and **`!AIVDM` / `!AIVDO`** — multi-part ones are put together and decoded by the same code as *AIS Decoder* (vessels with name, type and size from the static reports). Other sentences are counted as skipped.
- **Epoch:** `GGA`, `RMC` and `VTG` of one second arrive as separate lines; they are merged into one record, sent when the time of the next position sentence changes or 300 ms after the first one. Only valid fixes (`A`, quality > 0) give a position.
- **Output:** `rec` — `{t, src:'NMEA', id, label, lat, lon, alt, speed (kn), course, heading, sats, hdop, fix, utc, date, icon}` for the receiver (*record id* parameter) and the vessel records of AIS; `sat` — the satellites of one `GSV` cycle `{sys, prn, el, az, snr, used}`; numbers `lat`, `lon`, `alt`, `sog` (kn), `cog`, `hdg`, `sats`, `hdop`, `depth`, `wspd`, `wdir`, `temp`, which keep the last value; `go` — a pulse per accepted line.
- **Not done:** talker-specific sentences (`$P…`), waypoints and routes (`RMB`, `BWC`, `RTE`), `VHW` / `VLW`, the NMEA 2000 / `$PGRMZ` variants; the parser was checked on sample sentences and hand-made vectors, not on live receivers.

Presets: *NMEA: GPS Track (Table Playback)*, *NMEA: Network Stream (gpsd, AIS-catcher, Termux)*.

## POCSAG

**POCSAG Decoder** (Decoders, worker) reads one-way pagers: 2-FSK ±4.5 kHz, 512 / 1200 / 2400 Bd, in the VHF / UHF paging bands (country dependent) and 929 MHz in North America.

- **Input:** IQ at any rate (decimated to 24–48 kS/s inside; ±8 kHz are passed — put *IQ Frequency Shift* before it if the channel is off-centre) or an already demodulated FM audio.
- **Chain:** FM discriminator → the mean of the middle half of each bit against the mean over 48 bits around it → all three baud rates (or the one chosen) at 8 bit-clock phases each → sync word `0x7CD215D8` (up to 2 bit errors; its inverse means inverted polarity) → batches of 16 codewords; the next batch has to start with a sync word (4 errors allowed), otherwise the transmission is over.
- **Codewords:** BCH(31,21) with polynomial `0x769` and even parity; *correct up to N bit errors* (2 by default) uses a syndrome table, an uncorrectable word is counted as bad and its bits still go into the message. Address word: 18 bits + 2 bits of function, **RIC** = address × 8 + the frame number (position of the pair in the batch). Message words (20 bits each) follow until the next address word, an idle word `0x7A89C197` or the end of the transmission.
- **Text:** 7-bit characters LSB first over the bit stream of the message; digits: 4-bit groups LSB first (`0123456789*U -][`). Function 0 is shown as numeric, anything else as text; both readings are always in the record (`num`, `alpha`), a message with no data is a tone-only call.
- **Phases and noise:** every phase reads the same bits, so messages that start within 8 bits of each other are one message: the one with the fewest errors is kept, and a message with errors has to be confirmed by another phase — that removes the garbage that error correction makes out of noise (up to 2 corrected bits per word means about a quarter of random words "decode").
- **Output:** records `{t, src:'POCSAG', kind:'message', id, to, ric, func, type: alpha|numeric|tone, baud, text, alpha, num, errors, fixed}`; **Messages** takes them too (`to` = RIC, `text`).
- **Not done:** the message layer of paging networks (FLEX, ERMES and the like are other protocols; vendor extensions in numeric messages; long messages split into several RICs) is not interpreted; the receiver was checked against the built-in generator (SNR down to ~8 dB in the whole 256 kHz window, offsets ±3 kHz, all three rates) and against the published sync / idle words (BCH polynomial and parity), not against real signals yet.

Presets: *POCSAG: Pager Messages (Generator)*, *POCSAG: Pager Messages (USB SDR)*.

### POCSAG: Transmit

**POCSAG: Transmit** (Protocols) builds a page: a message for one RIC → a signal that goes through *AM/FM/SSB Modulator* (NFM, deviation 4500 Hz = the ±1 of the signal) into *HackRF TX* or any other IQ sink. The same bit stream is read by the decoder above, so a patch can check itself without a radio (*POCSAG: Transmitter Loopback*).

- **Message:** *RIC* (0…2097151; the `ric` wire overrides it), *function* 0…3 (the pager decides what a function means: 3 = text and 0 = numeric are usual), *type* — **alpha** (7-bit ASCII, ends with EOT, the rest of the word is zeros), **numeric** (`0123456789*U -[]`, `(` and `)` are sent as `[` `]`, the rest of the word is spaces) or **tone** (an address with no data). A RIC / function pair whose address word would be the idle word is refused.
- **Signal:** *baud* 512 / 1200 / 2400, *preamble* (576 bits by default), batches of a sync word and 16 codewords (BCH(31,21), parity), the address in the frame given by the three low bits of the RIC, long messages go on after the next sync word. Polarity as in the standard: **1 = the lower frequency**; *invert* flips it for a pager or a network that uses the other one. The edges are smoothed (one pole at 0.65 baud). *carrier before / after the data* keeps the carrier on a bit longer.
- **Control:** the *Send* button, a rising edge on `go`, or *send every new text on the wire* (off by default — a wire carries its text from the moment the patch loads); messages queue up. Outputs: `out` (the signal), `tx` (1 while a message is being sent — wire it to the `tx` pin of HackRF TX), `done` (a pulse at the end).
- **Not done:** one RIC per transmission (no batching of several pages), no tone / voice pagers, no vendor-specific numeric extensions; the encoder was checked by decoding its output (all three rates, text / numeric / tone, a message that crosses the batch border, FM noise and an offset), the constants against the published sync and idle words — not against a real pager yet. **You must comply with local radio regulations: transmit only where and how the law allows**; start with the lowest power, close to the pager.

Presets: *POCSAG: Transmitter Loopback (no radio)*, *POCSAG: Send a Page (HackRF TX)*.

## DCF77 and WWVB

**Time Signal Decoder** (Decoders, worker) reads the time code of the longwave stations: **DCF77** (77.5 kHz, Germany) and **WWVB** (60 kHz, USA). Both keep the carrier on all the time and drop its power at the start of every second; the length of the dip is the bit. (The older *WWV/WWVH/CHU Decoder* is a different thing — the 100 Hz subcarrier of the HF stations.)

- **Input:** an `iq` stream. *carrier = stream center*: the carrier is at the station frequency minus the stream centre — an SDR tuned near 77.5 / 60 kHz (RX-888 and the like), or a real signal from a sound card at 192 kS/s (*I/Q → IQ* with only `I` wired makes a stream with the centre at 0; DCF77 at 77.5 kHz needs a sample rate of at least 160 kS/s). *audio tone*: the carrier is an audio tone (a receiver in CW mode with a BFO offset; set *tone*). *envelope (AM audio)*: the input is already the level of the carrier — only useful if the demodulator keeps the DC level (an AGC and a high-pass hide the dips).
- **Chain:** mixer → averaging → low-pass (±70 Hz, so a ±8 Hz sound-card clock error is fine) → envelope at 400 Hz → followers of the upper (8 s) and lower (20 s) level, a dip is taken after ≥35% depth with hysteresis → width in ms → symbol (DCF77: <150 ms = 0, <260 ms = 1; WWVB: <350 = 0, <650 = 1, <950 = marker). Time is counted in samples of the stream, so the run speed ×8 … ×32 works.
- **Frames:** DCF77 — a 2 s pause (no 59th second) starts the minute; the frame is accepted when all three parity bits, the start bit (0), the S bit (1), Z1 ≠ Z2 and the ranges are right; the time of the *next* minute in CET / CEST is converted to UTC. WWVB — two markers in a row start the minute; accepted when the markers stand at 0, 9, 19, 29, 39, 49, 59, all other positions are 0 / 1 and the unused ones are 0 (no parity in WWVB); the frame is the minute that has just ended, the record is its end. Two frames one minute apart give `confirmed`.
- **Output:** `rec` — `{t, src: DCF77|WWVB, kind:'time', utc, year, month, day, hour, min, dst, confirmed, text}` and weekday / announce bits (DCF77) or day of year, DUT1, leap flags, DST bits (WWVB); numbers `bit` (0 / 1 / 2 = marker), `sec`, `min`, `hour` (of the last good frame), `ok` (confirmed), `lvl` (carrier), `depth` (depth of the dips, 0…1). The readout shows the bits of the frame so far, so a weak signal is easy to judge.
- **Not done:** MSF (60 kHz, Great Britain) and JJY (40 / 60 kHz) — other pulse codes; DCF77 weather bits, the PM phase code and the WWVB phase-modulated format are not read; the signal strength gives no confidence value apart from `depth`. Checked against the built-in generator (time code of the current clock, 48 kS/s IQ, a 192 kS/s real signal, an audio tone, noise down to 0 dB in the whole stream, offsets of tens of Hz) and against a second encoder written from the frame description, **not on real signals**; the KiwiSDR route (CW mode + *audio tone*) is a guess that has not been tried.

Presets: *Time Signal: DCF77 Clock (Generator)*, *Time Signal: WWVB Clock (Generator)*, *Time Signal: DCF77 / WWVB from a Sound Card (192 kS/s)*.

## SAME / EAS

**SAME / EAS Decoder** (Decoders, main thread) reads the digital header of the NOAA Weather Radio alerts (US 162.400–162.550 MHz, NFM; the same coding is used by the Emergency Alert System): AFSK at 520.83 Bd, 1 = 2083.3 Hz, 0 = 1562.5 Hz, characters of 8 bits LSB first.

- **Input:** audio (`sig`) of an FM receiver — *USB SDR* `audio` with NFM, a sound card, a file.
- **Chain:** a correlator on both tones per bit at 8 bit-clock phases → search for `ZCZC` / `NNNN` in the bit stream (up to 2 bit errors) → characters up to `+TTTT-JJJHHMM-LLLLLLLL-`. The phases vote for the string of one transmission, then the three repeats vote **character by character** (the repeats exist for that). A message is closed after *wait* seconds of silence or after three repeats.
- **Output:** `rec` `{t, src:'SAME', kind: header|eom|raw, org, orgName, event, eventName, locations (PSSCCC codes), states, areas, purge (min), jday, hour, min (UTC), callsign, text, raw, repeats, agree}` — `agree` is how many of the `repeats` match the voted string; a header that does not fit the format comes as `raw`. `text` — the line for a log or *Notify*; `new` — a pulse.
- **Codes:** originators `EAS` / `CIV` / `WXR` / `PEP`, about 60 event codes (Tornado Warning, Required Weekly Test…; an unknown code is described by its last letter: W warning, A watch, E emergency, S statement); the state of a location (FIPS) is shown, county names are not.
- **SAME: Test Signal** (Protocols) makes a header ×3 and `NNNN` ×3 from the fields (default: a Required Weekly Test for one Texas county) as audio — **for checking the decoder on a cable or a sound-card loop only**. Do not transmit it and do not feed it into alerting equipment: a false alert is illegal and harmful. There is deliberately no transmit preset.
- **Not done:** the attention tone (853 + 960 Hz) and the voice message are not touched; no county names or NWR station list; Canadian and other national formats are not distinguished (WXR / EAS only); checked on the generator (SNR down to ~8 dB, a level change, corruption of two of the three repeats in different characters), the frame format is from the description of the code, **not on a real broadcast**.

Presets: *SAME / EAS: Alert Decoder Test (Loopback)*, *SAME / EAS: NOAA Weather Radio (USB SDR)*.

## SELCAL

**SELCAL Decoder** (Decoders, main thread) reads the aviation Selective Calling code: a ground station calls an aircraft by four audio tones sent over the voice channel on HF or VHF. Tone table and timings are from the *ASRI SELCAL Users Guide* (Rev D, table 2-1 and figure 2-2): 16 tones named A…S (no I, N, O): A 312.6, B 346.7, C 384.6, D 426.6, E 473.2, F 524.8, G 582.1, H 645.7, J 716.1, K 794.3, L 881.0, M 977.2, P 1083.9, Q 1202.3, R 1333.5, S 1479.1 Hz. A code is **two pulses of two simultaneous tones**, a pulse lasts 1.0 ± 0.25 s, the gap 0.2 ± 0.1 s; `AC-BD` is A + C, then B + D, and the four tones of a code are different. (This is the aviation system — not the ZVEI / CCIR five-tone selcall of land mobile radio.)

- **Input:** audio (`sig`) of a receiver in USB — *KiwiSDR*, *USB SDR*, a sound card. The tones sit in the audio at their own pitch, so the receiver has to be tuned to the carrier frequency of the transmitter within a few Hz.
- **Chain:** a Hann window of 90 ms, Goertzel on the 16 frequencies every 43 ms; a frame is "two tones" when the two strongest bins hold a good share of the window energy (*tone purity*, default 35%), the weaker is within 11 dB of the stronger and the rest are 10 dB below it; a run of frames with the same pair lasting 0.6–1.5 s is a pulse (a dropout of up to two frames is bridged); two pulses with a gap up to 0.8 s and four different tones are a code. Time is counted in samples (run speed ×8…×32 works).
- **Tuning error:** the offset of the tones from the table is measured (a parabola through ±6 Hz around the peak) and shown with each code and as `offset` in the record. *receiver tuning error* shifts the whole table by a known error. Beyond about ±10 Hz the tones are no longer found; at an error close to the tone spacing (34 Hz at A–B) a call decodes as a **valid-looking different code** (+40 Hz turns AB-CD into BC-DE) — the offset line is the hint, the code cannot tell.
- **Output:** `rec` `{t, src:'SELCAL', kind:'call', id, code, tones, freqs, pulse1, pulse2, gap, offset, match}` — `match` (0 / 1) when a code is typed into *watch for a code* (e.g. the code placarded in your cockpit); `text` — the code; `new` — a pulse; `level` — input level.
- **SELCAL: Test Signal** (Protocols) plays a code as audio (pulse and gap adjustable inside the tolerance). **Only for checking the decoder on a cable or a sound-card loop**: do not transmit it — it calls a real aircraft on a working frequency. There is deliberately no transmit preset.
- **Not done:** the older 12-tone code lists are the same tones (the 16-tone system adds P, Q, R, S); no code database or registration lookup (assignments are the registrar's, ASRI); no decoding of the voice. Checked against the generator (all 16 tones in 9 combinations, pulses of 0.75 and 1.25 s, gaps of 0.1 and 0.3 s, noise down to 0 dB, a −30 dB level, speech-like interference at the level of the tones, 10 minutes of that interference with no false calls, tone errors of 5 and −9 Hz), **not on real recordings**.

Presets: *SELCAL: Test Signal (Loopback)*, *SELCAL: HF Aeronautical Channel (KiwiSDR)*.

## JS8Call

**JS8Call: Receive Slots** (Decoders) and **JS8Call: Transmit** (Protocols) cover the *Normal* submode of [JS8Call](http://js8call.com): 15 s UTC slots, a transmission of 12.64 s from slot start +0.5 s, 8 tones 6.25 Hz apart (the same timing as FT8, so the spectrogram and the SNR estimate are shared with *FT8: Receive Slots*). The codec is ported from the JS8Call sources (GPLv3: `genjs8`, `encode174`, `bpdecode174`, `chkcrc12a`, `varicode`, `jsc`).

- **Frame:** 72 bits of payload + 3 bits *i3* (first / last frame of a message) + CRC-12 (polynomial 0xC06, xor 42) → LDPC(174,87) → 58 data symbols of 3 bits (straight binary, no Gray code) between three Costas 7×7 blocks (the original array).
- **Receive:** candidates by the Costas sync score (*thr*, *top*), soft metrics, belief propagation, CRC-12 check, small time / frequency retries inside the *parse budget*. Output as in FT8: `msg`, `rec` `{t, src:'JS8', kind, msg, f, dt, snr, from, to, grid, cmd, …}` (a locator is remembered per call; with *My Position* the record gets distance and azimuth, so the FT8 map presets work), the log shows each frame's type (`HB`, `CQ`, `DIRECTED`, `COMPOUND`, `DATA`) and *first* / *last* flags. Audio is resampled to 6400 Hz with a windowed sinc (plain averaging put ghost copies of a strong signal ±700 Hz away). In the synthetic test a frame decodes down to about −15 dB in 2500 Hz.
- **Frame types:** heartbeat / CQ (callsign of up to 11 characters, locator), directed (from, to, one of the 32 commands — `SNR?`, `GRID?`, `INFO`, `QSL`, `73`, … — and a number, e.g. `SNR -12`), compound callsigns, data. Text is Huffman-coded or goes through the **JSC** dictionary (262144 words, `data/js8-jsc.txt`, ~2 MB, ~1 MB on the wire), fetched on first use and cached by the service worker; until it arrives a compressed frame is shown as `[JSC …]` and is decoded in the log as soon as the dictionary is loaded (*text dictionary* off — never fetched).
- **Transmit:** `HB [GRID]` and `CQ [DX|QRP|CONTEST|FIELD|FD|CQ CQ]` (the locator defaults to *my locator*), `W9XYZ SNR?` / `W9XYZ GRID?` / `W9XYZ 73` / `W9XYZ SNR -12`, `W9XYZ: free text` (a directed frame, then data frames), plain text (data frames). A message of several frames goes one frame per slot, *repeat the message* loops it; *slots* every / even / odd; *Send again* restarts it. The `text` input replaces the text parameter.
- **Not done:** Fast (10 s), Turbo (6 s), Slow (30 s) and Ultra submodes (other symbol times and the modified Costas arrays — the arrays are in the code, the timing is not); relayed `>` messages and checksummed commands (`MSG`, `QUERY`, …) are shown as their commands only; transmitting from a compound callsign (`CALL/XX`); no inbox, no auto-reply. **The codec is checked against itself** (parity matrix ↔ generator, packing ↔ unpacking, transmitter ↔ receiver in `tools/test-kernels.mjs`, and the loopback preset in a browser), **not against real on-air JS8Call traffic or the reference program** — a field check with a recording is welcome.

Presets: *JS8Call: Transmit and Receive (Loop)*, *JS8Call: Receive Off-Air*.

## TBSK (spread-spectrum data over sound)

**TBSK Decoder** (Decoders, main thread) and **TBSK Modulator** (Protocols) implement the modulation of [nyatla/TBSKmodem](https://github.com/nyatla/TBSKmodem) — data over sound with no carrier or phase lock, from ~5 bit/s to ~1 kbit/s. A bit is one *tone* (a carrier with pseudo-random ±45° phase jumps per sample, so the spectrum is spread) or the same tone with the opposite sign; the coding is differential (a 1 keeps the sign of the previous symbol, a 0 flips it), and the receiver correlates every symbol with the previous one (delay detection): +1 → 1, −1 → 0.

- **Parameters** (the same on both sides): *carrier* (Hz) and *tone length* (carrier periods); bit rate = carrier / tone length (4800 Hz / 10 = 480 bit/s). A longer tone is slower and tolerates more noise and interference. The tone is built as in the Python port (XPskSin: phase step δ + k·2π/8, k = ±1 from XorShift31(999), 299 skipped).
- **Frame:** a preamble of 14 symbols (`0 1`, five `1`, `0 1 0 1`, `0 0 1`: a plateau of correlation +1, then −1), the bits (bytes, most significant bit first, UTF-8 for text), an optional stop symbol (the tone with alternating sample signs, correlation ≈ 0). TBSK itself has no length, CRC or error correction — the *max bytes* parameter cuts a frame, otherwise it ends when the correlation drops below *end of frame* (0.2).
- **Receiver:** running correlation of the last tone with the one before it; the preamble is found by matching the expected ±1 pattern at symbol boundaries (threshold 0.4 — about 13σ above noise), symbol timing is then tracked by the neighbouring |r| (±500 ppm clock error is fine). Output: `rec` `{t, src:'TBSK', kind:'frame', id, bytes, bits, hex, text, quality, bps}` (*quality* is the mean |r|), `text`, `new`, `level`.
- **Modulator:** text from the `text` input (or the *message* parameter), sent by the *Send* button, a rising edge on `go` or, with *auto*, on a change of the text; outputs `out` (audio) and `busy`. Wire `out` to the audio output node and the microphone to the decoder for a real loop through air.
- **Not done / not checked:** no tone types other than XPskSin (no plain PN tone), no pre-set custom `shift` generator, no packet layer. Checked against the module's own modulator: clean signal, three frames in a row, binary payload, any start offset, 20/20 frames at 1 dB SNR (white noise in the whole band; it fails somewhere between 1 and −2.5 dB, tested at 1, −2.5 and −7.5 dB), ±500 ppm sample clock error, no false frames on 20 s of noise or on a pure carrier (`node tools/test-tbsk.mjs`). **Not compared with a signal from the original TBSKmodem** (the preamble and the first differential symbol are written from the Python source; if a recording from the original does not decode, this is where to look first).

**License and patent:** the modulation, the tone generator and its constants, and the preamble structure are taken from [TBSKmodem](https://github.com/nyatla/TBSKmodem) by Ryo Iizuka (nyatla.jp), MIT License, Copyright (C) 2022 Ryo Iizuka, nyatla.jp; the code here is a new implementation (no source copied). The original's README mentions patent WO-A-2010/016589 (modulation / demodulation device) and says some rights may belong to YAMAHA CORPORATION — check it before commercial use.

Preset: *TBSK: Spread-Spectrum Data Over Sound (Loopback)*.

## Five-tone selcall (ZVEI, CCIR, EEA)

**Five-Tone Selcall Decoder** (Decoders, main thread) reads the selective calling of land mobile radio — fire brigades and rescue services (ZVEI in German-speaking countries), taxis, security, industry. A call is a **sequence of tones, usually five, with no gaps**, 33–100 ms each; one tone = one symbol 0–9 or A–F; **E is the repeat tone** that stands for the previous digit, so `11223` is sent as `1E2E3`. This is the ground system; the aviation one is [SELCAL](#selcal).

- **Standards** (the table of 16 tones, Hz): ZVEI-1 (2400, 1060, 1160, 1270, 1400, 1530, 1670, 1830, 2000, 2200 for 0–9; A 2800, B 810, C 970, D 885, E 2600, F 680), ZVEI-2, ZVEI-3, DZVEI, PZVEI, CCIR (1981, 1124, 1197, 1275, 1358, 1446, 1540, 1640, 1747, 1860; A 2400, B 930, C 2247, D 991, E 2110, F 1055), EEA, EIA — as listed in the `demod_*.c` files of multimon-ng (numbers only, no code taken); the usual tone length is 70 ms (ZVEI, DZVEI), 100 ms (CCIR, PZVEI), 40 ms (EEA), 33 ms (EIA) — the *tone length* parameter overrides it, and the decoder takes ±40%. In the PZVEI table 0 and E share 2400 Hz: such a tone is a 0, and a 0 after a 0 is shown as E.
- **Input:** audio (`sig`) of an FM receiver — *USB SDR* `audio` in NFM, a sound card, a file.
- **Chain:** a Hann window of 0.7 tone, Goertzel on 16 frequencies every 1/8 tone; a frame is a tone when it holds a good share of the window energy (*purity*, 40%) and is 6 dB above the next one; ≥3 frames in a row (one miss is bridged) make a tone; tones whose centres are 0.6–1.8 tone lengths apart are one sequence, which is closed after 2.5 tone lengths of silence; shorter than *shortest sequence* (4) is dropped. Time is counted in samples.
- **Output:** `rec` `{t, src:'SELCALL', kind:'call', id, std, raw, code, tones}` — `raw` with E, `code` with the repeat expanded; `text` — the code; `new` — a pulse; `level`.
- **Five-Tone Selcall: Test Signal** (Protocols) plays a code in the chosen standard. **Only for a cable or a sound-card loop**: such tones start real alarm receivers — do not transmit it. There is deliberately no transmit preset.
- **Not done:** no group-call / emergency symbols meaning, no addresses of fire stations, no 7-tone or 4-tone profile checks, no automatic choice of the standard (the digits are the same in ZVEI-1/2/3 and CCIR/EEA, so the letters tell them apart only if you pick right). Checked against the generator in all eight standards (7 codes each, digits and letters, doubled digits), with noise down to 0 dB, speech-like interference at the level of the tones (9 minutes of it, no false calls), a tone length 20% off — **not on real alarms**; the standard's tone lengths are from the usual specifications, not from a document at hand.

Presets: *Selcall ZVEI / CCIR: Five-Tone Decoder Test (Loopback)*, *Selcall ZVEI / CCIR: Five-Tone Decoder (USB SDR, NFM)*.

## ACARS

**ACARS Decoder** (Decoders, worker) reads aircraft datalink messages on VHF (131 MHz band, AM, 25 kHz channels): position and OOOI reports, gate and weather messages, link tests.

- **Input:** IQ at any rate (decimated to ~24 kS/s inside) or an already demodulated AM audio. The envelope is taken, so the carrier may be off-centre within the passband (about ±10 kHz); put *IQ Frequency Shift* before it to stay away from the DC spike of the SDR.
- **Chain:** AM envelope → DC block → mix 1800 Hz to zero → low-pass → phase step per bit (MSK, 1200 / 2400 Hz) at 8 bit-clock phases → sync word `'*' SYN SYN SOH` (two bit errors allowed) → bytes (7 bits LSB first + odd parity) → ETX / ETB, BCS, DEL. The tone polarity and the differential variant (tone change or repeat = bit) are not assumed: all four readings of the bit stream are tried and the sync word picks the right one, shown in the node header.
- **Check:** BCS is CRC-16 (reflected, polynomial `0x8408`, initial 0) over the bytes from the mode to the BCS, with parity bits included; the result must be zero. With *fix up to 2 parity errors* on, the bits of bytes that fail parity are flipped one by one, and the block is accepted only if the CRC then matches (those are marked `fixed`). Frames with a wrong CRC are only counted.
- **Output:** records `{t, src:'ACARS', kind:'message', id, reg, mode, ack, label, blk, dir: air2gnd|gnd2air, no, flight, etb, txt, text, rssi, fixed}`. For downlink blocks (block id is a digit) the message number and flight id are split off the text. Rec Log saves CSV, *Rec: Unique by Key* (key `reg`) gives a list of aircraft heard.
- **Not done:** the message layer is not interpreted (labels, ARINC 620/622 formats, OOOI fields) and positions are not extracted — feed the text to *Geo from Text* if a message carries coordinates (`N55123E037456`). Multi-block messages (ETB) come out as separate records. One channel at a time. The decoder was checked against the generator (including noise at 8 dB SNR, ±300 ppm clock error, inverted and differential tones, single parity errors), **not against real recordings**: the CRC convention and the tone mapping follow the common ACARS decoders from memory, so check them on your own captures first.

Presets: *ACARS: VHF Messages (Generator)*, *ACARS: VHF Messages (USB SDR, 131 MHz)*.

## Analog video

Two blocks turn an IQ stream into a picture: **TV Demodulator** (IQ, worker) and **TV Decoder** (Decoders, worker; the picture is on the `img` output — show it with a *Frame* node).

- **TV Demodulator:** *FM* (FPV video transmitters on 5.8 GHz, analog satellite TV) — a discriminator with a fast arctangent; *AM (negative)* / *AM (positive)* — envelope detector for broadcast TV (tune to the vision carrier). Then a 4th-order Butterworth low-pass (*video bandwidth*, up to 0.45 × the sample rate). The output is a real stream at the input rate with the sync tips down; *invert* flips the sign. *Deviation* only scales the level.
- **TV Decoder:** input is a real stream at ≥ 8 MS/s (colour: ≥ 2.4 × the colour subcarrier — 10.6 MS/s PAL, 8.6 MS/s NTSC; HackRF at 12–20 MS/s, Airspy 10 MS/s).
  - *Sync:* a slicer between the sync tip and the blanking level (first from the minimum and maximum, then from the measured levels), sub-sample edge times, pulse widths tell a line sync (4.7 µs) from equalizing (2.35 µs) and field (27 µs) pulses. The line period is tracked (±3 %), a missing line is filled in, a lost lock is found again. The sync tip and the back porch of every line set the black level and the gain, so the picture does not need a level setting.
  - *Standard:* *auto* counts the lines between field syncs (≈ 305 → PAL 625, ≈ 253 → NTSC 525); or set PAL / NTSC by hand.
  - *Sync:* *free* needs no sync at all (weak signal, narrow bandwidth, a lost carrier): lines are drawn at the nominal line rate, the levels come from the statistics of each line, so even a trace of a picture shows. The picture slants or rolls until *trim* (ppm of the line period) matches the transmitter; *picture shift* moves it. No colour in this mode.
  - *Polarity:* *auto* flips it if there is no sync for half a second.
  - *Colour:* the burst of every line gives the phase and the gain of the subcarrier (the reference phase runs on across lines). PAL: the swinging burst gives the V-switch, the chroma is averaged with the previous line of the field. Luma = the composite minus the reconstructed chroma, so the subcarrier does not show on coloured areas; a weak chroma is treated as luma leakage. No burst (B/W) — luma only; *colour* off gives luma only too.
  - *Picture:* 384…960 px wide, 576 (PAL) or 480 (NTSC) lines; *weave* puts two fields in one frame, *bob* doubles the lines of every field (no combing on motion); brightness, contrast, picture shift in µs and lines.
  - Outputs: `img`, `lock` (0 / 1), `fps` (fields per second).
- **Not done:** SECAM (the picture comes out B/W), teletext and VBI lines, sound, VCR and other unstable line timing beyond ±3 %, blind PAL / NTSC colour of a signal whose subcarrier is off by several kHz. The field order (top or bottom first) is not detected, it follows the order of the syncs. Checked against the generator and hand-made signals, not against real cameras — the timing constants are the standard ones (PAL-B/G/I, NTSC-M without setup), check your own captures. At 12–20 MS/s the chain takes about one core: it runs in one worker island, lower the run speed if it does not keep up.

The IQ Generator mode **Analog TV** sends a test card (75 % colour bars, grey scale, white frame) in PAL or NTSC, FM (*deviation*) or AM with negative modulation.

Presets: *Analog TV: Test Card (Generator)*, *Analog TV: FPV / TV Receiver (USB SDR)*, *Analog TV: FPV Channel Scanner (HackRF)* (Band Scanner over the Raceband channels; the TV Decoder `lock` stops the scan on a live video, add rows to the table for more channels). *Analog TV: FPV Multiband Scanner (HackRF)* is the same scan over 21 channels: 0.9, 1.2, 2.4 GHz and Raceband. *Analog TV: FPV Mosaic (one HackRF)* shows several channels at once with the **TV Hopper**.

- **TV Hopper** (Decoders, worker): a mosaic of several analog channels from one receiver. Wire the receiver's `iq` to `in` and the hopper's `freq` to the receiver's `freq`: it tunes the receiver to each channel of *channels, MHz* (up to 16) in turn and takes one field (*bob*) on each, throws away chunks of the old frequency (the stream's `fc` must match) and puts the picture into the channel's tile (384×288, a grid on `img`). Every channel has its own demodulator and decoder whose state (polarity, sync levels, standard) is kept between visits, so a channel locks again within a field or two. *dwell* is the longest time on a channel without a finished field (a silent channel stays black), *settle* — how long to wait for the receiver to reach the channel. A tile refreshes every channels × (retune time + ~50 ms of signal for one field): the retune time of a real receiver decides, 4 channels ≈ 2–4 frames per second each. `hold` (input or parameter) = N stays on channel N and puts its full frame on `sel` at the full rate; `lock` — channels locked now, `ch` — the current channel. An empty channel is probed for a shorter *probe* time and visited less often. Checked against a simulated receiver (`node tools/test-tvhop.mjs`: two PAL channels and one noise channel, a field per visit takes ~50 ms of signal), not against real hardware

## Digital TV (MPEG-TS)

**TS → Video** (`tsvid`, Decoders): an MPEG transport stream on `bin` (or a `.ts` / `.m2ts` file chosen in the node) → PAT / PMT → the video and the audio of one program. The picture is on `vid` (a canvas: *Video Overlay*, *Record Video*, *WebRTC Video Out*), the sound (MP2, and AAC-ADTS through the browser's AudioDecoder) on `L` / `R` (to *Sound Card*); `lock` and `fps` are numbers. *program* picks the program number (0 — the first one with video), *buffer* is the delay before a frame is shown (frames go out by PTS), *audio delay* shifts the sound against the picture, *loop* repeats a file.

- **H.264, HEVC:** the browser's WebCodecs `VideoDecoder` (Annex B; the codec string comes from the SPS). A frame with a lost packet (continuity counter) is dropped; decoding goes on (a short artifact until the next I frame) — the `strict` option waits for the next entry point instead. The mode that worked (hardware / software, any I frame / IDR only) is remembered.
- **MPEG-2 video** (DVB-T SD, ATSC): browsers have no decoder, so `vendor/mpeg2.wasm` does it — 4:2:0, frame and field pictures, I / P / B, interlace (field prediction and field DCT), both scans, both coefficient tables, non-linear quantiser, DC precision up to 11 bits, custom matrices, 8 bit → RGBA (BT.601, BT.709 from 720 lines up), picture shaped by the display aspect ratio (4:3, 16:9). Interlaced pictures are shown as they are (no deinterlacing yet). Not supported: dual prime, scalability, 4:2:2, MPEG-1 video, `repeat_first_field` (3:2 pulldown is ignored).
- **MP2 audio** (MPEG-1 Layer II, what DVB-T carries): `vendor/mpeg2.wasm`, resampled to the engine rate, played by the video clock. AAC / AC-3 are named in the readout but not decoded yet.
- **Reception:** from a USB stick — *DVB USB (Astrometa)* below; a TS file works as well. **Not done:** showing teletext and subtitles (they are listed), encrypted programs, PTS wrap (every 26.5 h).
- **Channel list and what else the stream carries:** the service tables are parsed while it plays — SDT (names, provider, type, scrambled), NIT (network name, logical channel numbers, mux frequencies), PMT (every stream with its language: audio tracks, subtitles, teletext), EIT (now / next and the schedule), TDT / TOT (UTC and local offset), plus the bitrate of each program. Outputs: `all` (both kinds in one list; better keep a Table per kind: `chan`, `epg`), `chan` (one row per service: lcn, program, name, type, scrambled, video, audio, extra, mbit, now, until, next, provider, playing) and `epg` (one row per event: channel, start in local time, min, title, text, state) — wire one of them to the `rows` input of a **Table** (rows in: replace). `info` is a text with the network, the time and the number of services. The `prog` input takes a program number (e.g. from a Table column) and selects it. Tables arrive within a few seconds (EIT schedule within about 10 s).
- **For a Table without flicker:** set the Table's *rows wire* (fields panel → List) to **sync** (the upsert key column, `id` by default: rows are changed, added and removed in place — nothing is cleared, the selected row stays) or **new only** (adds the rows whose key is not in the list yet, e.g. into a band plan). Every row from `all` / `chan` / `epg` / `mux` has an `id`. A Table column wired to `prog` switches the channel (a number, or a part of the channel name).
- **Multiplex frequencies:** `mux` (TS → Video: the muxes the NIT announces, DVB-T and DVB-T2; DVB USB: the muxes the stick locked on, also from a scan) are band-plan rows in kHz (`name`, `lo`, `hi`, `step`, `color`, `kind`, `note`). The `freq` input of DVB USB takes MHz, kHz or Hz (by size), so the `mid` output of such a band plan tunes the stick.
- **Teletext:** the teletext stream of the program (DVB, EN 300 472) is decoded into pages (EN 300 706 level 1: colours, block graphics, double height; Cyrillic G0 for Russian, Ukrainian, Bulgarian streams or by the `characters` option). *node window shows → teletext* puts the page into the node window; the `ttx` output is the picture, `ttxt` the text, the `page` input and the ◀ ▶ buttons choose the page. Not done: fastext links, X/26 accents, hold mosaic, flash.
- **Preset:** *Digital TV: DVB-T2 full* — the stick, TS → Video, three Tables — channels (click a row to switch), programme, muxes (band plan; its mid output tunes the stick).
- **Build:** `tools/mpeg2/build.sh` (clang, wasm32, no libc; 41 kB). The video decoder (`mpeg2.c`) is new code; its macroblock syntax and VLC trees follow [pl_mpeg](https://github.com/phoboslab/pl_mpeg) (MIT, Dominic Szablewski), the run / level tables are the standard's Annex B tables as listed in FFmpeg's `mpeg12data.c`. The MP2 decoder (`mp2.c`) is pl_mpeg's audio decoder (kjmp2 by Martin J. Fiedler) with two corrections: its requantisation scale was 1 / (levels + 1) short of the standard's table, which made coarse quantisers up to 25 % too quiet, and the output divisor compensated for it by 1.5 %.
- **Tests:** `node tools/test-ts.mjs` (demuxer: PAT / PMT / PES, SPS size and cropping, chunked input with junk, a lost packet); `node tools/test-mpeg2.mjs` (needs ffmpeg: streams made by ffmpeg's `mpeg2video` / `mp2` encoders are decoded by ffmpeg and by the wasm module and compared — progressive, intra only, P only, interlaced 720×576 with field MC and field DCT, alternate scan, intra VLC table B.15, non-linear quantiser, DC precision 10 bit, custom matrices, 1920×1080; the pictures agree to 60–64 dB PSNR, max difference 5 levels, the IDCT rounding; audio −73 dB). 300 corrupted streams ran without a trap. **Not tested:** field pictures (no encoder here makes them), dual prime (not implemented), real DVB captures; the H.264 / HEVC path was not run in a browser with a decoder yet.

### DVB USB (Astrometa)

**IPTV** (`iptv`, Sources): a TV stream from the network as `bin` for **TS → Video**. The URL (parameter, or the `url` text input) can be an HTTP MPEG-TS stream, an **HLS** playlist (a media playlist with `.ts` segments, or a master playlist — *quality* picks the variant: highest / lowest / first; live streams start at the live edge, a VOD plays at the segment pace) or an **M3U channel list** (`#EXTINF`, `tvg-id`, `tvg-logo`, `group-title`): the channels go out on `rows` (wire to a *Table*; a row's `url` column to the `url` input switches the channel) and *channel* / the `channel` input plays one — a number (1-based; the `n` column of the rows), a `tvg-id` / name, or a ready `http(s)://` address, so a Table column (`n`, `id`, `name` or `url`) can be wired straight to it; the `next` pulse input and the ◀ ▶ buttons step through the list; a list can also be loaded from a file. Outputs `name`, `live`, `kbps`. The server must allow cross-origin reads (CORS) — otherwise tick *through the CORS proxy* (Settings); an `http://` stream from an `https://` page is blocked by the browser (mixed content). **Not possible in a browser:** UDP / RTP multicast (`udp://@239…`), RTSP, RTMP; **not supported:** fMP4 / CMAF HLS and encrypted (AES-128) HLS — the node says so. Dropped streams reconnect. Preset *Digital TV: IPTV (HTTP TS / HLS / M3U) → TS → Video and Sound*. Logic: `modules/iptv-kernels.js`, `node tools/test-iptv.mjs`; the network side is not checked against a real server.

**Stream Check** (`streamCheck`, Data): checks a channel list before you play it. Wire *IPTV* `rows` (any rows with a `url` column) to `rows`: the node requests every distinct address in *parallel checks* threads (1–64) and sends the working channels on `rows` (the fields are kept, plus `alive`, `check` — `TS` / `HLS` / `playlist` / `reachable` / the content type — and `ms`) and the rest on `dead` (with `why`: `HTTP 404`, `timeout`, `not a stream (text/html)`, …); `ok` / `bad` / `left` are counts. *check*: `content` reads the first *bytes* of the answer (HTTP status, the MPEG-TS sync byte 0x47 at 0 and 188, an M3U / HLS playlist, an audio / video content type) and needs CORS on the server; `reachable` only learns whether the server answers (works without CORS, the content is not checked); `auto` (default) reads the content and falls back to *reachable* where the browser forbids reading. *HLS: also fetch a segment* goes through a master playlist to the last segment of a media playlist and checks it is MPEG-TS (fMP4 / encrypted playlists are reported as such). *timeout* is per stream. Results are kept while the node lives, so changing the list only checks the new addresses; *Check again* forgets them, *Stop* cancels the run. The output list grows as the checks finish (at most about once a second): in the *Table* set *rows wire* = `sync`, so the list is updated in place and the selected row stays. An `http://` stream from an `https://` page is blocked by the browser and shows as dead; *through the CORS proxy* applies the Settings proxy. A stream is checked for the first bytes only, not for being watchable. Preset *Digital TV: IPTV* has it between IPTV and the Table. Logic: `modules/streamcheck-kernels.js`, `node tools/test-streamcheck.mjs` (a local HTTP server: TS, 404, HTML, a stalled stream, HLS with a good and a missing segment, the thread limit); the browser side is not checked against real IPTV servers.

### IP cameras and archive

Camera links live in a **Table** (`name`, `url` columns), the picture is loaded by a module over HTTP and goes on to the video modules — the same chain as IPTV.

**IP Camera** (`ipcam`, Sources): `url` is an **MJPEG stream** (multipart, the frames are cut out of it), a **JPEG / PNG snapshot** fetched every *snapshot: refresh every* seconds, or an **HLS** playlist (goes out on `url` — wire it to **IPTV**). *kind* `auto` decides by the address and, failing that, by the server's `Content-Type`. `user:password@host` in the address is sent as Basic auth. The address comes from the *url* parameter or input, from a Table row on `rec`, or from a list on `rows` (Table `all`) and the camera number / name in *cam* (the `cam` input, `next` pulse steps through the list) — so any number of IP Camera nodes share one Table, each with its own number. Outputs `img` (up to *max width*), `url` (HLS), `name`, `new`, `fps`, `live`. A stalled stream is reopened after 15 s, a dropped one reconnects. The camera must send CORS headers, otherwise tick *through the CORS proxy* (Settings); an `http://` camera from an `https://` page is blocked by the browser. **Not possible in a browser:** RTSP / RTMP / UDP — the node says so (a gateway such as go2rtc turns them into MJPEG / HLS).

**Camera Archive** (`camarch`, Video): *record* — frames from `img` go to the browser storage (Files → images → `Cameras/<name>`) every *a frame every* seconds, and also when the picture changes by more than *motion* (a 32×24 grey preview is compared; at most once a second); the `rec` input (≥ 0.5) is a gate. *keep days* / *keep at most frames* drop the oldest. *playback* — the archive of the camera named in *cam* (or on the `name` input) is shown with a **timeline** (frame density, the cursor): click the strip, the *position* slider, ◀ ▶, `seek` (0…1), `step` and `play` inputs, *playback: frames per second*, *loop*; `img` is the shown frame, `t` / `time` its time, `count` the frames. *Download this frame* saves a JPEG; the files are ordinary files in the Files manager (also exported with the backup).

**Fullscreen:** the ⤢ button in the module header (or in the tile header) and a double-click on the picture open the picture of **IP Camera**, **Camera Archive**, **Frame** and **TS → Video** (IPTV, DVB) fullscreen with the aspect ratio kept; Esc or another double-click returns. Where the browser cannot fullscreen a canvas (iOS), the picture fills the window instead. In fullscreen the archive answers ←, →, space. A module opts in with `fs:true` in its definition.

Preset *Cameras: Table of links → IP Camera → Frames + Archive*. Logic: `modules/camera-kernels.js`, `node tools/test-camera.mjs` (address kinds, auth, camera choice, motion, save schedule, pruning, timeline). The browser side was run against a mocked camera (MJPEG, snapshot, archive, playback, fullscreen), **not against real cameras**.

### Camera tools

Four *Video* nodes take the picture from `img` (*Camera*, *IP Camera*, *Video*…); *Timelapse* also takes `vid`. Logic — `modules/camtools-kernels.js`, nodes — `modules/camtools.js`, `node tools/test-camtools.mjs` (motion, pulse of a synthetic signal with noise and a drift, timelapse schedule and deflicker, ZIP checked by Python, colour temperature, lines and calibration of a spectrum). The browser side was run on the fake camera of headless Chromium, **not on real cameras or fingers**.

- **Motion Detect**: the share of pixels that changed by more than *pixel change that counts*, in the whole frame or a zone. Outputs `motion` (0…1), `go` (a pulse when the motion starts), `on` (a level, kept for *keep on after the motion stops* seconds; it needs the share above *alarm: % of the zone*), `level`, `cx` / `cy` (where the motion is), `img` (the map of changes) and `count`. *Compare with*: the previous frame (quick motion) or a slowly updated background (slow motion and left objects; a still object fades into the background). The `thr` input sets the alarm level, `reset` clears the background. `go` / `on` can start *Camera Archive* (`rec`), *Timelapse* (`snap`), *Beep*, *Camera → Record video* (`rec`).
- **Pulse (camera)**: heart rate from the colour of the picture. Cover the lens of the phone with a fingertip and turn *Camera → torch* on — or show a face (green channel, *only a zone*: forehead, cheeks). The mean colour of the zone over time → a band 0.15–1 s → a spectrum → `bpm`; `conf` (0…1) is the share of power at the peak and its second harmonic, below *trust above this confidence* the `ok` output is 0 and `bpm` is 0. `beat` — a pulse per heartbeat (about 0.4 s late, the filter looks ahead), `hrv` — RMSSD in ms, `finger` — the lens is covered. Needs ≥ 12 frames per second and a steady hand; it is not a medical device.
- **Timelapse**: a frame every *a frame every* seconds from `vid` (full resolution, up to *frame width*) or `img`, JPEG in memory (the page's memory: **frames are lost when the tab closes** — download them), up to *stop after frames*. Start / stop by the button or the `rec` level, `snap` — one more frame by a pulse, `interval` input. Outputs `count`, `go` (a pulse per frame), `on`, `left` (seconds to the next frame), `mb`. **Render video…** plays the frames at *frames per second* into webm / mp4 (takes as long as the video itself); *even out flicker* levels the brightness of neighbouring frames, a slow change (sunset) stays. **Download frames (zip)** — the JPEG files without re-encoding. For a long watch with a disk archive and a timeline use *Camera Archive* (a frame every N seconds or on motion).
- **Color / Spectrometer**: *color* — the mean colour of a zone: `r`, `g`, `b`, `hex`, `name`, `h` / `s` / `v`, `cct` (colour temperature, K; a white screen is about 6500 K). *spectrum* — a camera behind a diffraction grating (a CD piece or a grating film in front of the lens, a slit in front of the light): a strip of the frame across the rainbow becomes a spectrum by wavelength. Outputs `nm` (the strongest line), `lines` (the lines, nm) and `profile` (a signal for the scope). Calibration is a straight line through two points (*position in the strip* ↔ *nm*); **Calibrate on a fluorescent lamp** fits it to the three brightest lines of the lamp (Hg 436.6, Hg 546.1, Eu 611.6 nm). *Take dark frame* (lens covered) is subtracted, *hold maximum* collects the spectrum over time. The accuracy is a few nm at best, set by the grating and the sensor; a phone's auto exposure and white balance are better off (*Camera → auto exposure / auto white balance* off).

- **Screen (video)** (Sources): the picture of a screen, a window or a browser tab (`getDisplayMedia`; the browser asks what to share) on `vid` (for *Record Video*, *Files*, *Timelapse*, *Video Overlay*, *Object Detect*) and `img` (for *Color / Spectrometer*, *Motion Detect*, *Pulse*). **A live pixel**: click or drag over the preview — the circle marks a pixel (or the average of a square around it, *± px*) that is read in the full resolution of the capture, not from the small `img`; `r` `g` `b` `hex` `name` follow it while the screen changes; the `x` / `y` inputs move the point. The **Eyedropper** button is the browser's `EyeDropper` (Chrome / Edge, desktop): one pixel anywhere on the screen, even outside the browser window — but only once, the API has no live colour; switch *colour outputs from* back to *screen pixel* for a live one. What the capture shows depends on the browser (a window that is minimised stops updating; a browser tab is the most reliable). Preset *Screen: Live Pixel Color + Video Recording*.

Presets (*Images & TV*): *Pulse: Fingertip on the Phone Camera (torch)*, *Camera Alarm: Motion → Archive + Beep*, *Timelapse: Camera Frames → Video*, *Color Meter and Spectrometer (camera + grating)*, *Screen: Live Pixel Color + Video Recording*.

### Podcasts, Webcams, Free Music

Three network sources in the manner of *Internet Radio*: a search fills a list (`rows`), a pick gives an address (`url`, `name`) for **Audio Stream (URL)**, **Playlist** or **IPTV**. No keys or accounts. A browser needs the server to send CORS headers: where it does not, tick *through the CORS proxy* (the proxy from Settings; it wraps the search, the feed and the `url` output). An `http://` file from an `https://` page is blocked by the browser (mixed content).

**Podcasts** (`podcasts`, Sources): *query* searches Apple's public podcast directory (iTunes Search API; *store country* sets the catalogue) and the found shows go out on `rows` (`url` is the RSS feed). *podcast* takes a name from the results or any RSS feed URL (also the `feed` text input, e.g. a Table column): the feed is parsed (title, publication date, `itunes:duration`, the audio `enclosure`) and the episodes go out on `episodes`, the newest first (*episodes kept from a feed* limits their number). *episode* (the number, 1 is the newest; the `episode` input also takes a title part or an address), ◀ ▶ and the `next` pulse choose one: `url` is its audio file, `name` its title. Podcast hosts often have no CORS headers and redirect through trackers — the proxy is usually needed for the audio.

**Webcams** (`webcams`, Sources): *Find webcams* asks OpenStreetMap (Overpass, the same mirrors as the map's object layers) for objects with `contact:webcam` and for `man_made=surveillance` + `surveillance:type=webcam` within *radius* of the centre — the `lat` / `lon` wires, then the *centre* parameters, then My Position. The cameras go out on `rows` for the **Map** (wire the map's `sel` back to `select`: a click picks a camera). The kind comes from the address: a **picture** (`.jpg` / `.png`…) or an **MJPEG** stream (the first whole JPEG frame is cut out) is fetched, drawn in the node and sent on `img` (*picture width*), refreshed every *picture refresh* seconds (0 — once, *Refresh now*); an **HLS** stream goes out on `url` (wire it to **IPTV**); a **web page** is not a picture: *Open page* opens it. A picture URL can also be typed into *camera* or sent to the `url` input. Only cameras that someone entered into OpenStreetMap are found, and many of them are web pages.

**Free Music** (`freeMusic`, Music): *source* **Openverse** (Creative Commons tracks from Jamendo, Free Music Archive, Freesound, Wikimedia…; one file per result, 20 per page without a key; the licence and the source are in the row) or **Internet Archive** (a search over audio items, by default in the collections `opensource_audio` and `netlabels` — *extra search terms* is a Lucene fragment you can change). `rows` is what was found, *Next page ▶* the next page. In the Archive the results are albums: pick one in *album* (a name or the identifier) and its mp3 files (ogg / flac if there are none) become `tracks`; with Openverse `tracks` are the results. *track* (the number, or the `track` input with a number, a title part or an address), ◀ ▶ and the `next` pulse choose one: `url` and `name` (*artist — title*). Wire `tracks` to the Playlist's `rows` (the *stations* button adds them all). Without a key Openverse limits the number of requests, the node says so on HTTP 429.

Presets *Podcasts: Search → Episodes → Sound*, *Free Music: Openverse / Internet Archive → Playlist*, *Webcams: OpenStreetMap map → Picture*. Logic: `modules/online-kernels.js`, `node tools/test-online.mjs` (feeds, API answers, MJPEG, the nodes against a mocked network). **Not checked against the live services** (the sandbox it was written in has no outside network): the iTunes, Openverse, Internet Archive and Overpass request and answer formats follow their documentation.

**DVB USB (Astrometa)** (`dvbUsb`, Sources, WebUSB — Chrome / Edge): a DVB-T / T2 / C receiver on a USB stick with **RTL2832U + R828D + Panasonic MN88473 / MN88472 or Sony CXD2837ER** — the Astrometa DVB-T2 (`15f4:0131`; the kernel's `rtl28xxu` driver finds out which of the three demodulators is on the board, and so does this node) and sticks built the same way. The node brings the stick up the way the Linux drivers do, tunes it and puts the transport stream on `bin` for **TS → Video**; outputs `lock`, `cnr` (dB) and `level` (0…1) show the reception. Preset *Digital TV: DVB-T2 Astrometa → TS → Video and Sound*.

1. **Firmware** (Panasonic MN88473 / MN88472 only; the Sony CXD2837ER has none): the demodulator needs a file that is not bundled — `dvb-demod-mn88473-01.fw` (MN88473) or `dvb-demod-mn88472-02.fw` (MN88472), from the `linux-firmware` package (`/lib/firmware`). Choose it once in the node (parameter *fw*): it is kept in the browser's IndexedDB; it can also be placed in `vendor/` next to the app.
2. **Connect USB…:** the node finds the R828D tuner and the demodulator on the stick's I2C bus, loads the firmware and shows every step in its log. On Linux free the stick from the kernel first (`rmmod dvb_usb_rtl28xxu`), on Windows give it a WinUSB driver (Zadig).
3. **Tune:** *frequency* is the channel centre in MHz (UHF channel 21 = 474, step 8; VHF channels 5–12 are 7 MHz wide, 177.5 + 7 × (n − 5)), *bandwidth* 8 / 7 / 6 MHz, *standard* auto (DVB-T2, then DVB-T after 3 s without lock) or fixed, *PLP* for DVB-T2. A wire on the `freq` input retunes (a *Band Scanner* or a *Table* of channels). **Scan the air** walks over the channels *from…to* (default UHF 21–69; VHF III is 5–12; DVB-T2, then DVB-T on each), waits up to 1.6 s for a signal and up to 6 s for a lock on a channel that shows one, logs every channel with a signal and C/N, and leaves the node tuned to the first channel with a lock.

- **What is ported:** the sequences of the Linux drivers `rtl28xxu` (power, GPIO, endpoint, I2C commands), `rtl2832` (TS pass-through from the slave demodulator), `r820t` (tuner for DVB: filters, IF 3.57 / 4.57 MHz, PLL, input), `mn88473` and `mn88472` (firmware, tuning, lock, C/N), `cxd2841er` (Sony CXD2837ER: DVB-T, T2, C; 6 / 7 / 8 MHz; the register sequences are not typed in but **recorded by running the kernel's own functions** — `tools/cxd-gen/gen.py` cuts them out of `cxd2841er.c`, compiles them with a stub bus and writes `modules/cxd-sequences.js`; the state machine, the status and C/N are ported by hand; manual PLP selection of DVB-T2 and the signal level through the demodulator's AGC are not). Not ported: the tuner's image-rejection calibration (IMR — the kernel can run without it too), DVB-C on the MN88472 beyond the register set of the driver, signal-level measurement through the tuner.
- **Checked:** the tuner is compared with the kernel's own functions compiled in C (`tools/dvb-ref`): the sequence of I2C operations is identical for 9 tunings (DVB-T / T2 / C, 6 / 7 / 8 MHz, the special 506 / 666 / 818 MHz cases); the Sony sequences are the kernel's own output (a switch of the standard in the kernel is checked to be "sleep, then activate"; the tuner's IF equals the IF the sequences were recorded for); the demodulators, the RTL2832 and the firmware transfer run against an emulated bus (`node tools/test-dvb.mjs tools/dvb-ref/tuner_ref`), and the whole chain — connect, firmware, tune, lock, stream into *TS → Video* — against an emulated USB stick in a browser. **Not checked on a real stick** — nothing in the emulator can prove the registers do what they should on silicon. If it does not lock or the stream is empty, the node log shows the last step; send it along.
- Sources: GPL-2.0-or-later code of the Linux kernel (drivers/media/…, Antti Palosaari, Mauro Carvalho Chehab and others) — a port of the register sequences. `tools/dvb-ref/tuner_ref.c` is made of the kernel's `r820t.c` functions with a stub bus.

### DVB-S / DVB-S2 / DVB-T2 from IQ

**DVB Receiver (IQ)** (`dvbsoft`, Decoders): digital TV straight from the IQ of any SDR — no demodulator chip. One node with a *standard* choice (DVB-S, DVB-S2 and DVB-T2 now; every standard is an engine in the worker, DVB-T is the next one; for it today use **DVB USB (Astrometa)**). One node: `iq` in, picture and sound out. It demodulates, corrects errors and hands the transport stream to the same decoder as **TS → Video** (H.264 / HEVC / MPEG-2, MP2, teletext, service tables; the same parameters and the `prog` / `page` inputs). Outputs: `vid`, `L`, `R`, `bin` (the transport stream), `lock` (carrier and FEC locked), `level` (0…1, from −60 dBFS), `mer` (dB), `ber` (Viterbi bit error rate), `freq` (carrier offset, Hz), `vlock` (a picture is decoded) and the `ttx` / `ttxt` / `mux` / `chan` / `epg` / `info` outputs of TS → Video. Preset *Digital TV: DVB-S/S2 from IQ (USB SDR → one receiver node)*.

- **Settings:** *standard* (DVB-S: QPSK, rates 1/2…7/8; DVB-S2: QPSK / 8PSK / 16APSK / 32APSK, rates 1/4…9/10), *symbol rate* in kBd, *roll-off*, *offset* from the input centre (the carrier search covers about a quarter of the symbol rate), *force Viterbi* for weak signals. The input needs at least 2 samples per symbol; the sample rate is taken from the stream, a change restarts the demodulator. The signal level is not critical (AGC; locks over a 46 dB range in the test).
- **Practical use:** amateur DATV on QO-100 (333 kBd – 1.5 MBd; an RTL-SDR at 2.4 MS/s covers up to ~1.2 MBd), satellite feeds through an LNB (L-band IF). A broadcast 27.5 MBd carrier needs an SDR with 40+ MS/s (RX-888) and a fast machine; the demodulator runs in a worker and the node skips blocks (and says so) when it falls behind.
- **How it is built:** [LeanSDR](https://github.com/pabr/leansdr) `leandvb` (by *pabr*) compiled to WebAssembly (`vendor/leandvb.wasm`, GPL-3.0-or-later; `tools/leandvb/build.sh`, a small patch that lets the host step the scheduler instead of reading stdin to the end). The glue is `modules/leandvb-kernels.js`, the worker `dvbsoft-worker.js`, the node `modules/dvbsoft.js`.
- **DVB-T2** (`vendor/dvbofdm.wasm`, own receiver, `tools/dvbofdm`): any sample rate not below the channel rate (resampled inside); settings *bandwidth* (8 / 7 / 6 / 5 / 10 / 1.7 MHz) and *PLP* (-1 = first). Chain: P1 search, L1-pre / L1-post, channel estimate from the pilots, CFO / sampling drift tracking, de-interleaving, QAM demapper (also rotated constellations), LDPC (int8 layered min-sum) + BCH, BBFRAME → TS. FFT 1K…32K, all guard intervals, PP1…PP8 pilot patterns as the tables allow. The readout shows FFT / GI / PLP / modulation / SNR / FEC blocks. Build: `tools/dvbofdm/build.sh <gnuradio checkout>` (tables are cut from gr-dtv); test: `node tools/test-dvbt2.mjs <cfile> <ref.ts> [bw] [rate]` (bit-exact TS compare against a GNU Radio transmitter).
- **DVB-T2 checked:** GNU Radio gr-dtv profiles (16K / 32K, GI 1/32…19/128, PP2 / PP3 / PP4, QPSK / 64QAM / 256QAM, rates 1/2…2/3, rotated 64 / 256QAM) bit-exact; noise down to SNR ≈ 11–12 dB (64QAM 1/2), CFO ±15 kHz, ±50 ppm, echoes up to 0.8 at 1000 samples. **Not checked** (no reference signal): type-2 PLP, common PLP, time interleaving over several frames, MISO, PAPR reduction, FEF, T2-Lite, a real transmitter. One thread decodes 3–5 MS/s on a clean signal (32K 64QAM), so a full 8 MHz channel (9.14 MS/s) goes through a **pool of workers**: the receiver worker only searches the preamble, decodes L1-pre and puts the stream together in the order of the frames, while whole frames (demodulation, L1-post, FEC) run in `dvbsoft-pool.js` workers, one frame per worker. The parameter *workers* is -1 for automatic (cores − 2, at most 6; fewer than 2 means one thread) or a number; if all workers are busy the frame is skipped and the readout says so. Measured on 4 cores: 1 / 2 / 3 workers decode 4.5 / 6.5 / 8.9 MS/s of a 32K 64QAM signal, bit-exact; a time interleaver over several frames needs the previous frames, so such a PLP is decoded in the receiver worker alone. Test with real threads: `node tools/test-dvbt2-par.mjs <cfile> <ref.ts> <workers> [bw] [rate]`; `PAR=n LAG=k node tools/test-dvbt2.mjs …` runs the same job protocol with delayed, reordered results.
- **Tests:** `node tools/test-leandvb.mjs <leansdr checkout>` builds the LeanSDR transmitter and feeds its signal to the wasm module: DVB-S QPSK 1/2, 2/3, 3/4, 7/8, 2 and 3 samples per symbol, with noise (MER 11.7 dB), and a real MPEG-2 + MP2 stream from ffmpeg through the transmitter into the demultiplexer (all frames, no lost packets). The node itself was run in Chromium on the same signal (lock, MER, transport stream on `bin`). **Not tested:** DVB-S2 (it is compiled in, but LeanSDR's transmitter makes only DVB-S framing, so there is no test signal here), a real satellite, an SDR in the loop, 8PSK / APSK, Fs/Fm below 2.

## Digital Voice Decoder (4FSK)

**Digital Voice Decoder** (`fskRx`, Decoders, worker) is a universal receiver for the digital voice systems that share one physical layer: an FM discriminator, a root-raised-cosine matched filter, eight clock phases per symbol and a sync-word search.
