The former separate *DMR Decoder* is now this node with *protocol = dmr* (old patches are converted on load; the DMR presets use it that way). The *protocol* parameter is *auto* (all of them at once: channels of the same baud rate, roll-off and level count share one filter chain — 4800 Bd RRC 0.2 for DMR / P25 / NXDN / YSF, 4800 Bd RRC 0.5 for M17, 4800 Bd 2FSK for D-STAR, 2400 Bd for dPMR and NXDN 4800) or one protocol. Input is IQ at any rate (or FM audio); polarity is found by the sync words and by FEC. Every protocol has its own lock, so a decoder that sees a wrong sync gives it up after the first frame that does not decode.

**Expand into blocks** (button on the node, for protocols that have a block chain — M17 so far): the node is replaced by *IQ Decimator* (when the receiver was decimating — run the graph once first so the rate is known) → *FM Discriminator* → *RRC Matched Filter* → *Symbol Slicer* → *Symbol Sync Search* → the protocol's frame parser (*M17 Frame Parser*), with the protocol's baud, α, sync words and frame length filled in; the input and output wires are moved over and Undo puts the single node back. Use it to see every stage, change a parameter or a sync word, or to build a new protocol on the same chain. On the generator's M17 signal the chain takes about 0.14 s of CPU per second of signal against 0.175 s for the single node (it looks at one clock phase per symbol where the single node watches eight), so nothing is lost by building the receiver from blocks.

Outputs: `rec` — records (`src` = protocol, `kind` = call / end / message / …, and a `text` line), `voice` — raw vocoder frames as hex (`ambe`, `imbe`, `codec2` fields). Voice is **not** decoded to audio. The IQ Generator has a **4FSK** mode with a test transmitter for each protocol.

| Protocol | What is decoded |
| --- | --- |
| **P25 Phase 1** (C4FM 4800 Bd) | NID (NAC, DUID; BCH(63,16)), HDU (Golay + RS(36,20), MI, algorithm, key, talkgroup), LDU1 link control (group / private call, source, destination, emergency, encryption) and LDU2 encryption sync (RS(24,12) / (24,16), Hamming(10,6)), low-speed data, TDU, TDULC (link control at the end of a call), **TSDU / TSBK** with 1–3 blocks (trellis ½ + CRC): channel identifiers (IDEN_UP, with frequency arithmetic), RFSS / network / adjacent-site status, group grants with frequency, affiliation / registration, service broadcast; **PDU data**: header (½ trellis, CRC-16), unconfirmed blocks (½) and confirmed blocks (¾ trellis, DBSN, CRC-9), CRC-32 of the packet, extended address and encryption sync headers, IPv4 / UDP (TMS, LRRP…), NMEA location, responses (ACK / NACK), alternate multi-block trunking messages (NET / RFSS / adjacent status, group grant). Frame clock drift is tracked across the 180 ms LDUs. |
| **NXDN 9600** (4FSK 4800 Bd) | LICH, SACCH superframes (RAN, layer 3 in four segments), FACCH1 (CRC-12), UDCH (CRC-15), K=5 convolution with puncturing, scrambler; layer-3 messages (voice call, TX release, disconnect, data call header…), 72-bit AMBE frames. Control channel (CAC, 300 bits, CRC-16 as in dsd-fme): SITE_INFO (location, channels, services), VCALL_ASSGN with channel and other layer-3 messages. **NXDN 4800** (2400 Bd, same frame layout at 80 ms) is a separate protocol in the list. |
| **M17** (4FSK 4800 Bd, RRC 0.5) | LSF (callsigns, TYPE, META: text / GNSS / extended callsigns, CRC), stream frames (LICH with Golay(24,12), frame number, 16 bytes of Codec 2, last-frame flag), late-entry LSF from LICH chunks, packet mode (SMS text, CRC), BERT (PRBS9 statistics). |
| **YSF** (C4FM 4800 Bd) | FICH (Golay + convolution + CRC-16: frame type, DT, FN / FT, DG-ID), header / communications / terminator, callsigns (destination, source, downlink, uplink) in V/D mode 1 and 2, **GPS position** (short / long packet from the data blocks: radio model, latitude, longitude; parser compared with YSFGateway `GPS.cpp`), raw AMBE blocks; data and voice full-rate frames come out raw. |
| **D-STAR** (GMSK 4800 Bd) | Header (K=3 convolution, 24×28 interleave, scrambler, CRC-16: MY, YOUR, RPT1, RPT2), voice frames, slow data (text, GPS NMEA, header copy — a call is recognised even if the header was missed), end pattern. |
| **dPMR** (4FSK 2400 Bd) | FS2 payload units: colour code, control channel (scrambler, Hamming(12,8), CRC-7: called and calling ID, mode, version), 8 raw traffic-channel frames per unit. **Header** (FS1: type, called and own ID, mode, format; Hamming(12,8) ×10, CRC-8) and **end frame** (FS3) are decoded, and so is the packet-data header (FS4: same layout, version / format / emergency / message information; the packet frames after it are not). FS4 is FS1 with the levels flipped, so the word's CRC decides the polarity. |

How much is checked: the codes and frame layouts are compared with the code of other implementations — P25, NXDN, YSF and D-STAR against MMDVMHost / the MMDVM firmware (real encoder output), M17 against libm17, dPMR primitives against dsd-fme, the header word against dsdcc (the reference programs and vectors are not in the repository). Whole signals are checked generator → decoder (all sample rates, noise, inverted spectrum, ±300 ppm clock error); **on-air reception has not been tried on real hardware yet**, and dPMR has no independent frame encoder to compare with.

### Messages and Subscribers

Two output nodes work on the `rec` output of *Digital Voice Decoder*, whatever the protocol:

- **Messages** (`msgLog`) — the meaningful messages as a separate stream in one common shape (`time, protocol, type, from, to, text, service, lat, lon, ip …`): DMR SMS / LRRP / IP-UDP / NMEA, P25 data PDUs (IP / UDP, NMEA position), NXDN data calls, M17 packets (SMS), D-STAR slow-data text and GPS, YSF GPS. `type` is *text*, *location*, *data* or *signalling*; the *signalling* check adds trunking messages, headers and encryption sync (P25 TSBK / MBT, DMR CSBK, NXDN messages, dPMR headers). Filter by protocol, save CSV, replay. The `msgs` output carries the records on.
- **Subscribers** (`subLog`) — a registry per protocol: ID or callsign, talker alias, calls, times heard as a destination, talk seconds, messages sent, last group, repeater / reflector (D-STAR, YSF), position when a message had one. Talk groups are counted apart (P25 TG, DMR TG, NXDN conference, `CQCQCQ`) with their stations. The `stations` records carry `lat` / `lon` for the map; CSV for subscribers and groups.

Both *Digital Voice Decoder* presets have them wired. D-STAR callsigns are taken without the `/suffix` (the header has it, the end of the call does not).

### Vocoder (mbelib)

**Vocoder (mbelib)** (`mbeVoice`, Decoders) turns the raw vocoder frames from the `voice` output of *Digital Voice Decoder* into sound: **P25 IMBE**, **DMR / NXDN / dPMR / YSF (V/D mode 1 and 2) AMBE+2** and **D-STAR AMBE**. The AMBE / IMBE decoder is [mbelib](https://github.com/szechyjs/mbelib) (ISC licence) compiled to WebAssembly — `vendor/mbelib.wasm`, built by `tools/mbelib/build.sh` with clang's wasm32 target (no Emscripten; `cosf` / `powf` / `exp` / `log` come from the browser's `Math`). Frames are put into mbelib's matrices with the interleave schedules of dsd-fme, decoded to 8 kHz (160 samples per 20 ms), then queued (120 ms pre-buffer), resampled to the engine rate and mixed. Streams are kept apart per protocol and DMR slot (the *DMR slot* parameter picks one). **M17** goes through [Codec 2](https://github.com/drowe67/codec2) (LGPL 2.1, no patent issue) in a second module, `vendor/codec2.wasm` (`tools/codec2/build.sh`; modes 3200 — two 8-byte frames per stream frame — and 1600 — one frame plus 8 data bytes, chosen by the TYPE field of the LSF). *YSF V/D mode 2* carries the 49 AMBE+2 bits without Golay FEC (27 bits sent three times, 22 bits plain): the Vocoder takes a majority vote, rebuilds the Golay words and the PRNG modulation of the 72-bit frame and decodes it as DMR AMBE+2 (layout from MMDVM_CM `ModeConv`; the rebuilt frame decodes back to the same 49 bits in mbelib). **TETRA** speech (ACELP, from the `voice` output of *TETRA Decoder*) is a third, optional module, `vendor/tetra-acelp.wasm`, which you build yourself (`tools/tetra-acelp/build.sh`, see [TETRA](#tetra)); two 137-bit frames per traffic slot, 240 samples (30 ms) each. The `err` output (num) is 1 while the frames decoded in the current block had corrected bit errors, 0 otherwise (IMBE / AMBE only; Codec 2 and ACELP report none). The `vad` output (num) is 1 while speech is present: a decoded frame (any codec — IMBE / AMBE, Codec 2, ACELP) counts as voiced when its RMS exceeds the *voice detect threshold* (int16 units), and the flag is held for 300 ms after the last such frame; for IMBE / AMBE the frame must also have decoded cleanly in mbelib (not an erasure, tone, repeat or mute), and the fundamental frequency of consecutive frames with voiced bands must change smoothly (over the last 30 frames): encrypted speech passes the FEC cleanly but its parameters are random and the pitch jumps, so it is rejected, and so is any call whose ALG says it is encrypted and has no working key. Codec 2 and ACELP carry no such parameters and are judged by energy only. Silence, lost frames and idle frames give 0. Tested on synthetic frames only (random parameters: about 6 % of windows pass; smooth pitch: all pass), not on real captures. The readout shows voiced frames per stream. Encrypted calls come out as noise unless a key is given: **P25 ADP** (ALGID 0xAA) and **DMR Enhanced Privacy** (ALG 0x21), both RC4 with a 40-bit key, are decrypted (`modules/voicecrypt.js`). The *RC4 keys* parameter and the `key` input (text, e.g. from *Text Source*; both are merged, the input wins) take entries separated by `;` or new lines: `0123456789` (any call), `7=0123456789` (key ID 7 from the call header) or `tg:5000=0123456789` (talkgroup / destination 5000); the most specific entry wins, so several streams can have their own keys. The keystream is applied to the vocoder parameter bits before FEC, so each frame is Golay / Hamming-decoded, XORed and re-encoded for mbelib (the layout was checked bit for bit against the mbelib decoder). RC4 key = key ‖ MI (P25: first 8 bytes of the MI, keystream from byte 267, 11 bytes per frame, +101 for LDU2, +2 for the LSD before frame 9, the MI of the next superframe comes from the LDU2 ESS or the 64-bit LFSR; DMR: 4-byte MI from the PI header, keystream from byte 256, 7 bytes per frame counted from the voice LC header — a lost burst breaks the sync until the next call). The offsets follow dsd-fme and were not tested against a real capture. The stream line in the readout shows the algorithm, key ID and whether it is decrypting, has no key, waits for the MI or the algorithm is not supported (DES, AES, Hytera, …).

- **Checked:** the wasm gives the same samples and error counts as a native build of the same mbelib sources for IMBE, AMBE+2 and AMBE frames (the reference program is not in the repository), and the whole chain generator → Digital Voice Decoder → Vocoder produces sound. The Codec 2 module is only smoke-tested (it loads and decodes frames without errors), not compared with a native build. **Not checked on real speech** — no encoder for these vocoders is available, so the frame layouts are those of dsd-fme and the output quality is that of mbelib (AMBE+2 in particular is only approximated). The generator sends random frames, so it plays noise.
- **Patents:** IMBE / AMBE / AMBE+2 are DVSI vocoders and their algorithms may be covered by patents in some countries (mbelib's own README says so). The ISC licence covers the code, not that. Check what applies to you before using or distributing it; to leave it out, delete `vendor/mbelib.wasm` — the node then just says the module is missing.
- The page's Content-Security-Policy allows `'wasm-unsafe-eval'` for this.

## DMR

**Digital Voice Decoder** with *protocol = dmr* (the old *DMR Decoder* node is the same engine; saved patches with it open as this node) decodes ETSI DMR (TS 102 361) from raw IQ or FM audio: MotoTRBO, Hytera, Anytone and other radios, amateur and commercial repeaters, direct mode.

- **Input:** IQ (any rate, decimated to ≥48 kS/s inside; put *IQ Frequency Shift* before it if the channel is off-centre, about ±4 kHz is fine) or FM audio. A 12.5 kHz channel, 4FSK at 4800 Bd (±1.944 kHz).
- **Chain:** FM discriminator → slow DC block → RRC (α=0.2) matched filter → 8 bit-clock phases → search for the 48-bit sync words (repeater, mobile, direct mode TS1 / TS2, voice and data, 4 bit errors to lock, more once locked) → the frame grid (30 ms, CACH + burst) is held from the sync words; levels and clock phase are refitted on every sync. The voice and data sync words of a system are each other's inverse, so the polarity of the spectrum is taken from whichever reading makes the slot type / EMB pass FEC — an inverted signal (swapped I/Q) is decoded too.
- **Slots and colour code:** repeater downlink — slot from the CACH (TACT, Hamming 7,4); mobile and direct mode have no CACH — direct mode gives the slot from the sync word, a mobile uplink is shown as *slot A / B*. Colour code from the slot type (Golay 20,8) and EMB (QR 16,7); the CACH Short LC (4 fragments, Hamming 17,12 + CRC-8) gives slot activity and Tier III net / site.
- **Voice:** LC header and terminator (BPTC 196,96 + Reed-Solomon 12,9 with the masks): from, to, group / private, emergency, encrypted, priority; PI header (algorithm, key id, MI); embedded LC (Hamming 16,11 + CRC-5) for late entry; **talker alias** (7-bit, ISO 8-bit, UTF-8, UTF-16). The AMBE+2 voice is *not* turned into sound (no vocoder) — the `voice` output carries the 3 raw frames of each burst (27 bytes hex) with slot, call and A…F position, ready for an external decoder.
- **CSBK / MBC:** **multi-block control** (header + continuation blocks, CRC-16 over the continuations) is assembled into one record; a grant with an absolute channel gets its `rx` / `tx` frequencies (MHz) and `freq` — a place to start when a Tier III system shows up. Opcode names (preamble, BS_Dwn_Act, grants, ACK, alert, radio check…), from / to for the common ones, raw hex; CRC-CCITT with the mask. Tier III: **C_ALOHA** (net / site / model, registration, version) and channel grants (logical channel, slot, emergency).
- **Data and SMS:** data header (unconfirmed, confirmed, short data, UDT, response, proprietary) + rate ½ (BPTC), rate ¾ (8-state trellis) and rate 1 (no FEC) blocks are collected into a message, checked with the CRC-32 (confirmed blocks also with their own CRC-9), and read: IPv4 / UDP (**Motorola TMS text**, LRRP / ARS / XCMP by port), short data by DD format (7-bit, 8-bit), UDT text, **UDT dialer digits (BCD), appended addresses, IP address and address + UTF-16 text** (layout of dsd-fme `dmr_udt_decoder`; the pad nibbles from the header are applied). **Positions:** LRRP (UDP 4001: circle / point tokens, time, heading) and NMEA GGA / RMC in UDT come out as `lat` / `lon` (+ `id`, `label`) and show up on a *Map* node wired to `rec`.
- **Output:** records `{kind: call|end|alias|csbk|data-header|message|pi|slc|emb-lc|…, slot, cc, from, to, text, …}`; the node shows the mode, colour code, both slots, counters and the last events.
- **Checked against:** MMDVMHost (BPTC, LC, CSBK, slot type, EMB, embedded LC, Short LC and CRC vectors from its own classes) and dsd-fme (rate ¾ trellis decoder, CRC-32 convention).
- **Not done:** AMBE+2 to audio; the LIP position report in USBD (only the raw block is shown); Hytera / Motorola manufacturer extensions beyond the generic LC; encryption (only flagged). The CRC-32 byte order, the TMS layout and the LRRP tokens follow dsd-fme and were not checked on a real recording. The generator sends valid frames built by the same tables as the decoder, so it proves the chain, not the interoperability.

**DMR Call Log** (Output) turns the decoder's records into an activity log: who called whom, when and for how long (calls, with the length from the terminator or from the voice-burst count; messages as rows too), counters **per radio ID** (calls, seconds, messages, emergency / encrypted calls, last heard, last group, talker alias, position) and **per talkgroup** (calls, seconds, number of stations). The readout shows the active slots, the latest stations and the last calls. Buttons: *Calls CSV*, *Stations CSV*, *Groups CSV*, *Replay stations*, *Clear*. Outputs: `stations` (one record per changed station, `id` = `dmr:<radio>`, with `lat`/`lon` once it has sent a position — wire it to a *Map*), `calls` (one `call-log` record per finished call), `count`.

Presets: *DMR: Calls, SMS and CSBK (Generator)*, *DMR: Repeater or Direct Mode (USB SDR)*, *DMR: Activity Log and Station Map (Generator)*, *DMR: Activity Log and Station Map (USB SDR)*.

## TETRA

**TETRA Decoder** (`tetraRx`, Decoders, worker) takes the IQ of one 25 kHz channel (any sample rate from 48 kS/s; a signal within about ±6 kHz of the center is fine, the offset is measured and shown) and decodes the **downlink** of a base station's main carrier. Receiver: decimation to 72–144 kS/s, root-raised-cosine 0.35, eight timing phases per symbol (cubic interpolation), differential detection of the π/4-DQPSK phase steps. The *synchronization burst* is found without any prior knowledge: the 32-symbol frequency-correction field (constant +π/4 steps — it also gives the carrier offset), then the 19-symbol synchronization training sequence (normal or spectrum-inverted). The BSCH block (60 bits) is decoded with the fixed scrambling code; it gives colour code, MCC, MNC and the slot / frame / multiframe number, which fixes the cell scrambler. After that the slot grid (255 symbols, ±3 symbols of search per slot, offset tracked) is followed by the training sequences; a cell that is not heard for 12 slots, or gives no valid block for two multiframes, is dropped and the search starts again.

Blocks: descrambling → deinterleaving → depuncturing (RCPC 2/3) → soft Viterbi (K = 5, rate 1/4) → CRC-16. AACH goes through the Reed–Muller (30,14) code. Records (`rec`, `src: TETRA`):

| kind | contents |
|---|---|
| `sync` | MCC, MNC, colour code, TN / FN / MN, sharing mode, late entry |
| `sysinfo` | downlink / uplink frequency (band, carrier, offset, duplex spacing), location area, BS services (voice, SNDCP, air encryption …) |
| `resource` | MAC-RESOURCE: address type and SSI (`to`), event label / usage marker, **encryption mode** (the part after the address is then not readable), channel allocation (timeslot, direction, carrier and frequency from SYSINFO), LLC type and — for MM / CMCE / MLE — the message name (`D-SETUP`, `D-LOCATION UPDATE ACCEPT`, `D-SDS DATA` …); the first bytes of the message as `hex` |
| `message` | **SDS** (see below): `from`, `to`, `service`, `message` (text), `lat` / `lon` / `speed` / `heading` (LIP), `status`, `hex`; with a position also `id` / `label` for the map |
| `call`, `end` | a traffic usage marker appears / disappears in the AACH of a timeslot; duration and number of traffic bursts at the end |

**SDS** (short data, the SMS of TETRA): fragmented MAC messages (MAC-RESOURCE start, MAC-FRAG, MAC-END on the same timeslot) are put together, the LLC / MLE / CMCE layers are peeled off and `D-SDS-DATA` is read: calling SSI, the receiver comes from the MAC address. Decoded: **text** — simple text messaging (PID 2 / 9) and SDS-TL text (PID 130 / 137, with the storage-and-forward fields skipped), alphabets GSM 7-bit, ISO 8859-1 and UCS-2; **status** (the 16-bit short data type); **position** — the LIP short location report (PID 10: longitude, latitude, speed, heading, accuracy, reason); anything else as hex with its protocol ID. Position records carry `id` and `label` like DMR's, so [Messages and Subscribers](#messages-and-subscribers) and the map take them as they are (Messages / Subscribers have a *TETRA* protocol; *signalling too* adds SYNC, SYSINFO and RESOURCE). Not decoded: concatenated SDS (PID 12 / 140), LLC-level segmentation (AL-*), the long LIP report and other LIP PDUs, SDS-TL GPS, other text alphabets; encrypted messages stay encrypted (their records show only the address). The field layouts follow EN 300 392-2 / SDS-TL as implemented in dsd-neo's parser and TS 100 392-18-1 (the PDU type of the short report is 2 bits, which an open-source Go library gets wrong; scaling checked against XPro's LIP PDU generator); the decoder is tested only against the generator here, **not against real SDS captures**.

Slots with a traffic marker are counted, and their speech goes out as `voice` records (`slot`, `usage`, `acelp` — the two 137-bit codec frames of the slot, hex; `encrypted` repeats the air-encryption flag of SYSINFO). The channel decoder is for the full-slot traffic channel TCH/S: matrix deinterleaving, class 0 (102 bits, unprotected) and the 1/3 convolutional code of classes 1 and 2 (8/12 and 8/18 puncturing), bit order of table 4 of EN 300 395-2. Encryption is not looked at: an encrypted call is decoded just the same and sounds like noise. Slots where the first half is stolen (STCH) are tried as signalling and not played.

The ACELP decoder itself is [Vocoder (mbelib)](#vocoder-mbelib)'s third module, `vendor/tetra-acelp.wasm`, built by `tools/tetra-acelp/build.sh` (clang with the wasm32 target, no Emscripten, no libc; fixed-point, nothing is imported from the browser) from [outerplane/tetra-codec](https://github.com/outerplane/tetra-codec), a port of the ETSI EN 300 395-2 V1.3.1 reference decoder — the old TETRA ACELP (240 samples / 137 bits per 30 ms frame), not AMR-WB or EVS. The wasm gives exactly the samples of the ETSI reference decoder on the 30 slots of the "Hello Tetra" recording (14 400 samples, no differences). **Patents and licence:** the TETRA codec may be covered by patents, and the ETSI source terms and the licence of that port are not clear (the port carries no licence file): check them before embedding this in a product. If the file is missing (a fork without it) the Vocoder says *TETRA ACELP not built* and the rest of the decoder works as before. Several PDUs in one block are walked through by their length; fragmented messages are counted but not reassembled, so a long SDS is not shown as text — the message names and addresses are what the decoder gives.

IQ Generator, mode *TETRA*: a test cell on 391.0 MHz (MCC 262, MNC 1011, colour 5) — sync burst in frame 18, control channel in slot 1 (SCH/F and SCH/HD, messages in turn, a channel allocation now and then), a call in slot 2 (traffic bursts with random bits), and SDS: text in three encodings (one long, sent in MAC fragments), statuses and LIP position reports of three moving radios. Presets *TETRA: Test Cell (Generator)* and *TETRA: Control Channel (USB SDR)*.

How much is checked: the channel coding (scrambler, block interleaver, RCPC puncturing and the convolutional encoder, CRC-16, the Reed–Muller generator matrix) gives the same bits as the reference code of [osmo-tetra](https://github.com/osmocom/osmo-tetra) (`tetra_conv_enc`, `tetra_interleave`, `tetra_scramb`, `tetra_rm3014`, `crc_simple`, built and compared on random blocks for BSCH, SCH/HD and SCH/F). The speech channel decoder reproduces the codec frames of the ETSI reference decoder bit for bit on a real recording (the 30 slots of telive's "Hello Tetra" sample, from the test fixtures of dsd-neo: 8220 bits, no differences). The speech channel decoder reproduces the codec frames of the ETSI reference decoder bit for bit on a real recording (the 30 slots of telive's "Hello Tetra" sample, taken from the test fixtures of dsd-neo: 8220 bits, no differences). The ACELP wasm is bit-exact against the ETSI reference output on the same recording (the whole chain: channel decoder + codec → 14 400 samples without a difference). Burst layout, training sequences, SYNC / SYSINFO / MAC-RESOURCE fields and message names follow the same code. Whole signals are checked generator → decoder at 72 kS/s … 2.4 MS/s, with carrier offsets up to ±5.5 kHz, inverted spectrum, ±300 ppm clock error and noise (a broadband SNR of about −4 dB at 288 kS/s still decodes most blocks). The generator and the decoder are written by the same hand, so a wrong symbol-to-bits mapping (the 01 / 10 steps) would not show there — it follows the table of EN 300 392-2 as osmo-tetra uses it. **On-air reception has not been tried on real hardware**; direct-mode, uplink, π/4-DQPSK channels with other bandwidths and the QAM modes are not supported; the speech CRC-8 is not checked, bad frames are not concealed.

## Inmarsat STD-C

**Inmarsat STD-C Decoder** (Decoders, worker) decodes the LES TDM / NCS carriers of the geostationary Inmarsat satellites (L band, 1537–1545 MHz; a patch or helix antenna is enough). It follows SatDump's `inmarsat_stdc` (GPLv3).
- **Demodulation:** PSK Demodulator in BPSK mode, 1200 Bd, RRC α=0.6. For offsets comparable to the symbol rate it searches the frequency on the wide band before the matched filter.
- **Frames:** 64×162-symbol frames every 8.64 s. Unique word (≥ 120 of 128, either polarity), row de-permutation, de-interleaving, Viterbi K=7 (polynomials 109, 79), bit reversal and descrambling → 640 bytes.
- **Packets:** short / medium / long descriptors and the two-byte checksum (bad packets are dropped); multi-frame packets are joined. Bulletin Board gives satellite, LES and frame number. EGC packets (single and double header) are assembled into messages by sequence number.
- **Output:** `rec` per message (`service` — SafetyNET NAVAREA/METAREA, coastal, SAR, FleetNET…, `priority`, `text`, `sat`, `les`) and `text`.

The chain is tested with the generator's STD-C mode (frames with a Bulletin Board and two SafetyNET warnings split over frames) down to Es/N0 ≈ 5 dB, but not yet on a real recording.

Presets: *Inmarsat STD-C: EGC Messages (Generator)*, *Inmarsat STD-C: EGC Messages (USB SDR, 1.5 GHz)*.

## Sample editor

The **Files** node is a file manager for the browser storage (database *dsp-files*). The root `/` is the *files* storage (anything else, e.g. received over the network); the other storages are system folders in it: *audio* (the sample library: clips stored as float32 WAV), *images* (photos of Table rows and pictures) and *tables* (the Table lists — database *dsp-lists*, every list in its own object store; folders are the `folder/name` prefixes). Make your own folders anywhere, **⤒ import** (or drop files). A click marks a row; **long press**, right click or **Ctrl / Shift + click** starts selecting several (then a tap toggles a row; a folder is marked this way too, a plain click opens it). The action bar appears over the marked items: **✎** rename, **⇢** move, **⧉** copy, **⤓** download, **✕** delete — without a question, an **Undo** note appears instead. Move and copy stay inside one storage. Search by name (current storage), one sort list (name / newest / largest / type). Under the list a **preview** of the selected file: picture, start of a text, waveform of a clip. In *audio*: **▶** plays the selected clip on the `out` port with a progress bar you can drag to seek (inputs `clipId` / `play` / `seek` 0…1, `loop` parameter, outputs `clipId` / `pos`), **✎** opens the clip editor (clips only). **● rec** (or the `rec` input — at 0.5 and above it records, below it stops) records from the `in` port when it is wired and the graph runs, otherwise from the microphone; *record from* can also be the **camera**, the **screen** or **video in** — the picture on the `vid` / `img` inputs (TS → Video, decoders, Camera…; like Record Video, but straight into the store; preset *Digital TV: DVB-T2 → record video to Files*) — video as webm / mp4 as the browser allows, the audio format is *clip* (editable, goes to *audio*), *wav16* or *opus* (microphone). **Receiving**: bytes on the `bin` input (File → Bytes, Bytes → Frames…) are saved as a file (*Received* folder, pictures into *images*; `ok` pulses when saved; *also download* for a copy to disk). The `path` input — `[storage/]folders/[name.ext]`, folders are created — says where a recording or a received file goes (empty: the open folder / *Received*). Any sound file (wav, opus, mp3…) gets the same player in the preview — ▶, seek bar, the `out` port (it is decoded on the first ▶); a video shows in the preview with its own controls and goes to the `vid` output (Video Overlay, Record Video…). This is what the **Sample Library** and **Bytes → File** nodes did — they are gone, old patches open them as Files. Outputs `id` / `name` / `size` of the selected file; **Send selected →** sends every marked file one after another (pulse on `send`, bytes on `bin`; tables go as CSV). Below the list: browser storage meter (free / total / used from `navigator.storage.estimate()`); the first import asks the browser for persistent storage, and an import larger than the free space is refused. Old databases *dsp-samples* and *dsp-photos* are copied into it once with the same ids (patches and Tables keep working) and stay as a reserve; backups include *dsp-files* (old backup files still import).

**File transfer over WebRTC**: *WebRTC Data* opens two channels in one connection — `dsp` (text: Chat, games) and `file` (files, in 16 KB pieces with back-pressure, so a big file does not hold the chat). **Send file…** sends a file from this device; the `sendFile` input takes an id from the Files storage (the Files node's `send` output gives a pulse with the selected file's id when you press *Send selected →*). A received file goes into the *Received* folder of the matching storage (sound → *audio* as a clip, pictures → *images*, the rest → *files*); `file` is its id, `got` a pulse, `progress` 0…1; the readout shows the state. Files over *refuse incoming files over, MB* (200) are declined. Both sides need this version (an older peer has no `file` channel). Preset *Files over WebRTC*.

**Auto-connect and reconnect (WebRTC Data)**: instead of copying codes by hand fill *auto-connect: MQTT broker WebSocket* (`wss://…`, any public or own broker) and *room* (a long secret name, the same on both sides) and tick *auto-connect and reconnect*. The nodes meet in the room, exchange offer / answer through the broker and open the connection; when it drops (or a side reloads the page) they do it again by themselves. Signalling messages are encrypted with AES-GCM from a key derived from the room name and the topic is a hash of it, so the broker sees only noise and a stranger cannot inject an offer without the room name — still, use a long random name. Only the signalling goes through the broker; data flows directly. Needs a secure page (https). The `open` output is 1 while the channel is up; the games take it on `link`: after a reconnect both sides start the game over (the score stays).

**Voice and video (WebRTC Data)**: tick *microphone*, *camera* (*front / rear*) or *share screen* and the track goes into the same connection — together with text, games and files, nothing has to be reconnected. A track can be switched on and off at any time (new offers travel over a service channel inside the connection; both sides may start at once). *mute my microphone* silences without dropping the track, *play the other side* mutes the speaker. The picture of the other side (and your own preview in the corner) is shown inside the node; the `remote` output is a bitmask of what comes in (1 voice, 2 video). Needs https for the microphone and camera; the screen is not shared on most phones. Tracks of the graph's own audio and TV/SSTV pictures are not sent yet. Preset *Call: voice, video, chat and files over WebRTC*; both sides need this version (an older peer has no service channel).

**Sound and picture of the graph over WebRTC** — separate nodes on the wire `rtc` (*WebRTC Data* → `link`): **WebRTC Audio Out** sends a `sig` from its `in` (gain, mute), **WebRTC Video Out** a `vid` (Video file / URL, camera) or `img` (TV, SSTV and any frame; *fps*, *max width*), **WebRTC Audio In** gives the received sound as `sig`, **WebRTC Video In** the received picture as `img` and `vid`. A track has a name (*label*: `voice` / `video` by default): the receiving node takes the track with the same name, so one connection carries as many tracks as you like (several Out nodes with different labels, several In nodes). The microphone, camera and screen of *WebRTC Data* are named `mic`, `camera` and `screen` — *Audio In* with label `mic` gets the other side's microphone into the graph (demodulator, effects, recorder). Tracks come and go on the fly, and return by themselves after a reconnect. The sound is mono at the engine's sample rate with a short buffer (≈0.25 s on send, ≈0.4 s on receive). Preset *WebRTC: audio and video of the graph over a connection*. Turn off *play the other side* in *WebRTC Data* when a received track goes to the graph, otherwise it is heard twice.

The editor fills the screen and plays through its own audio output, no wiring needed:

- **Selection**: drag on the waveform; drag the edge handles to adjust it; tap to place the cursor. Drag on the time ruler or the overview strip to scroll; pinch horizontally to zoom in time, vertically to zoom the amplitude (mouse: wheel zooms, Shift+wheel scrolls, Alt+wheel zooms the amplitude)
- **Zoom** goes down to single samples (dots and stems); the amplitude zoom goes down to one 16-bit step. **✏ draw** redraws samples by hand, e.g. to fix a click
- **Playback**: the selection or from the cursor, with **🔁 loop** (the loop region follows the selection)
- **Edits**: crop to selection, delete, cut / copy / paste (between clips too, resampled when the rates differ), silence, fade in / out, normalize (−1 dBFS, DC removed), gain, reverse. Every edit can be undone / redone (Ctrl+Z / Ctrl+Shift+Z)
- **Spectrogram** (〰): under the waveform or alone, with a frequency scale (linear or log), FFT size, palette and dB range. It is computed for the visible range, so it stays sharp at any zoom
- **Noise reduction by profile**: select a piece with noise only → **noise profile**, then select the part to clean (or nothing for the whole clip) → **apply**. It is a spectral gate on the original samples (reduction, sensitivity, frequency and time smoothing); **preview** listens without changing the clip, **residue** lets you hear what gets removed
- **save** overwrites the clip, **＋copy** saves a new one, **⭳ wav** downloads the selection or the whole clip as 16-bit WAV

Keys: Space play/stop, L loop, Del delete, T crop, Ctrl+A/X/C/V, Ctrl+S save, +/−/0 zoom, S view, ←/→ move the cursor, Esc close.

## Tracker

The tracker is a set of nodes in **Music**, wired by the pink `song` port:

- **Tracker Song** — the song, its sequencer and the channel voices. Open a `.mod`, `.s3m` or `.xm` file, tick **play**. `L`/`R` is the mix of all channels (when they are not wired, it plays straight to the default output). `clk` gives a pulse on every row — wire it to the Drum Sequencer, Piano Roll or Sequencer `clk` and they follow the tracker's tempo, speed changes included. **stereo** is the channel separation, **interpolation** — linear or none (the raw Amiga sound). `row`/`pos` give the current row and position. **✎ Edit** opens the full-screen editor
- **Tracker Instrument** — makes an instrument play a generator of the graph instead of samples: every note of instrument **N**, from any channel, goes to up to 4 voices `freq`/`gate`/`vel` (`freq2`/`gate2`… for the others) — exactly the inputs of Synth (4 voices), Sampler, Acid Bass, Synth (2 osc). Portamento, vibrato, arpeggio are in `freq`, volume effects in `vel`, key off / note cut close the `gate`. While the node is wired, the tracker does not look for samples of that instrument (the list marks it `⇢ module`); the synth's output goes wherever you wire it
- **Tracker Channel** — one channel on its own: `out` (mono, after volume), `L`/`R` (with the channel's panning), and the notes: `gate`, `freq`, `note` (MIDI), `vel`, `inst`. **take out of the song mix** removes the channel from the song's `L`/`R`, so it can go through its own effects
- **Tracker Pattern View** — the live pattern with channel meters; double click opens the editor
- **The editor, panel by panel**: **Tracker: Transport**, **Pattern Editor**, **Pattern Sequencer**, **Instrument List**, **Sample / Instrument**, **Keypad**, **Mixer**. Each shows one panel of the same editing session (cursor, current instrument, octave, undo are shared): put them into dashboard tiles (▦) and you get your own tracker layout. Keys go to the tracker after a click in any of its panels. Preset *Tracker Studio (tiles)* lays them out like Renoise: transport on top, pattern sequencer left, pattern in the middle, instruments right, sample/instrument editor, mixer and the module graph below — switch on ▦ after loading it

Preset *Tracker: Synths as Instruments* plays a bass line on the 303 and chords on the 4-voice synth through two Tracker Instrument nodes, and runs the drum machine from `clk`. *Tracker: Channels through Effects* sends channel 1 through a delay.

The **Sampler** node plays a clip from Files › audio on 4 voices: pick the clip (↻ library re-reads the list), set **root note** — the MIDI note the clip sounds at — and wire `freq`/`gate` from a Tracker Instrument, the piano roll or a MIDI keyboard. In the tracker itself a slot can also take a clip straight from the library (**library…** in the sample panel).

Formats: MOD (M.K./M!K!/FLT4, 2–32 channels xCHN/xxCH, 15-sample Soundtracker), S3M (8/16-bit samples, channel panning), XM 1.04 (instruments with several samples and a note map, volume and panning envelopes with sustain and loop, fadeout, auto-vibrato, 16-bit and ping-pong samples, linear or Amiga frequencies, 1–256 rows per pattern). Each song is saved back in its own format; MOD converts to S3M or XM, S3M to XM.

The replayer follows each tracker's effect rules: MOD/XM 0–F and E-commands, XM G H K L P R T X and the volume column, S3M A–X with the ST3 shared memories (D/K/L, E/F), fine and extra fine slides, note cut/delay, pattern loop and delay, retrigger, tremor, key off. It was checked against libxmp tick by tick on its test modules.

**Editor** — full screen (**✎ Edit**) or the panel nodes above. Edits are heard right away and are kept in the browser (IndexedDB) together with the patch; **⭳** downloads the song as a regular module file.

- **Pattern**: the cursor row stays in the middle. Arrows move, Tab / Shift+Tab jump between channels, PgUp/PgDn by 16 rows, Home/End. Mouse: click places the cursor, drag selects a block, wheel scrolls (Shift+wheel — channels). Touch: tap places the cursor, drag scrolls
- **Entry**: **Enter** toggles edit mode (red cursor). The keyboard is a piano in the FT2 layout (Z S X D C… — lower octave, Q 2 W 3 E… — upper), physical keys, so it works with any layout; `1` or `` ` `` enters key off (XM) / note cut (S3M). F1–F8 or numpad +/− set the octave, **step** is how many rows the cursor goes down after a note. Instrument and parameter columns take hex digits (S3M instrument and volume — decimal), the effect column — 0–F for MOD, 0–9 and A–Z for XM, letters for S3M. The XM volume column: 00–40 is the volume, 6x–Fx are commands (slides, vibrato, panning, portamento). The status line explains the effect under the cursor
- **Block**: Shift+arrows or mouse drag select, Ctrl+A selects the channel (twice — the whole pattern), Ctrl+C/X/V copy / cut / paste, Del clears, Ctrl+↑/↓ transposes by a semitone (with Shift — by an octave), Insert / Backspace insert / delete a row in the channel. Ctrl+Z / Ctrl+Shift+Z — undo / redo of every edit
- **Channels**: click a channel header to mute it, double click — solo; `⇢` marks a channel taken out by a Tracker Channel node
- **Song** tab: order list (tap a position to edit its pattern), insert / delete positions, change the pattern at a position, new / clone / clear pattern, pattern length (XM), channels, initial speed / tempo, global volume, restart position, linear frequencies (XM)
- **Samples** tab (**Instruments** for XM): name, volume, finetune (MOD/XM), C-4 rate (S3M), panning and relative note (XM), loop off / forward / ping-pong; drag on the waveform to set the loop, ▶ plays the sample. **load** (or drop an audio file on the tracker) imports any audio file — for MOD resampled to play at its own pitch on C-3, for S3M/XM kept at its rate and tuned to C-4; **✎ edit** opens it in the [sample editor](#sample-editor), **save** there writes it back. XM instruments: several samples, the note map (tap or drag over the keys to give them the selected sample), volume and panning envelopes (drag points, double click adds one, **sus** / **loop** at the selected point), fadeout, auto-vibrato
- **Keys** tab / Keypad — the same on screen for phones: two-octave piano, hex and letter pads, key off, cursor keys, block operations
- **Mixer** tab / node — per channel: level meter, volume, panning, mute, solo (volume is kept with the song in the browser; the formats have no place for it)
- Space plays the song from the current position, Shift+Space loops the current pattern from the cursor row; **⇣ follow** keeps the cursor on the playing row

## Table

One node for everything that is a list of records: the former Data Sequencer, Band Plan, Bookmarks, List (CSV/DB) and CSV Log. (They are still in the palette under *show legacy*, so old patches keep working; new work should use **Table**.)

- **Layout**: one list picker with search (nested folders that collapse and expand, only the name is shown; a search opens everything it finds): a click on a name makes the list active and closes the panel, a tick next to it (or next to a folder) shows the list on the `rows` output (Map, band plan on the spectrum) and keeps the panel open (*none* clears; the button shows ☑N when several lists are on), **＋** right after it (new list: empty or from a template) and **⋯** with every action in groups — Rows, Data (import / open data / export), List (copy, rename, move to folder, template columns, delete), View (columns, first / last rows). The tile has two rows of controls: the list picker with ＋ (new list) and ⋯, and the filter with `+ row`, the view switch (cards / full list / brief list / icons) and the ▦ schema button (fields, roles, columns, colours, list settings — the one panel; the rows wrap on a narrow tile); a point at your position is the **Mark Point** node (preset *Map Points: Mark My Position → Table → Map*), not a Table button. Editing a row opens a panel at the bottom of the tile (two columns, *save* / *cancel* at the top) over a fixed-height list — it does not scroll with the rows and the list never changes height; double-click a row to edit it. The former sections below describe the same functions.
- **Lists and folders** (Sources → *Table*): a list is a table of records. It lives in the browser database (like the sample library, `folder/name` makes a folder; ＋ ✎ 🗑 create, rename / move, delete the active list). The lists are shown in **one picker, nested collapsible folders, each list and folder with a checkbox**: a click on a name makes the list *active* (its rows are listed and edited) and does not touch the checkboxes, only a checkbox shows the list on `rows` — tick any number of lists or a whole folder and `rows` becomes their union, one *filter* applies to all of them (`kind=video`, `note:ELRS`). The ticked lists and the open folders are remembered in the patch, or in the patch itself (list `@patch`: the data travels with the patch file), or it is one of the built-in band plans (folder `presets`, read-only, ~55 plans in folders — *Drones* (FPV video 5.8 / 1.2 / 2.4 / 3.3 GHz channels, RC and telemetry links, DJI / Autel / Parrot models, military link classes, counter-UAS bands, Remote ID), *Wireless* (Wi-Fi 2.4 / 5 / 6E / 60 GHz channels, Bluetooth and BLE channels, Zigbee / Thread, nRF24 ch0–125), *LoRa* (LoRaWAN EU868 / US915 / AU915 / Asia channels, Meshtastic, Sigfox, Z-Wave, wM-Bus), *SubGHz* (315 / 433 / 868 / 915 MHz), *Audio* (wireless microphones, DECT, baby monitors), *Navigation* (GNSS incl. GLONASS FDMA, aero), *Satellite* (Inmarsat, Iridium, weather, amateur / CubeSat, UHF 225–400, TV Ku/C, Starlink), *Cellular*, *Voice* (CB, PMR446, LPD433, FRS, MURS), *Stable* (time stations, VLF navy, beacons, 24/7 channels) and *Bands* (ITU names from ELF, IEEE / NATO letter bands L S C X Ku K Ka, infrasound and geophysical signals). Columns `kind` (`video`, `control`, `telemetry`, `channel`…) and `note` explain the rows; frequencies are a guide, not an official document; **copy** turns one into your own list, or moves a list into the patch and back). Old Bookmarks / Band Plan / List lists open as they are.
- **Your own database (cards, fields, links)**: **▦ schema → Fields** gives each column a type — *text, long text, number, checkbox, date, choice (your options), photo, audio, link to a list* — and a tick *show on the card*; the type is kept with the list (a list in the patch keeps it in the patch; copy, export of all lists and backup carry it). The entry form follows the types (number / date pickers, checkbox, choice menu, a photo strip with camera / gallery in any column typed *photo*, a picker for links with search and **＋ create** for a new entry in the linked list). Three views, switched by the button next to **▦ schema**: **cards** (cover photo, name, chosen fields, links as chips; a tap opens the entry card), **full list** (columns) and **brief list** (name · values). The *entry card* shows the big photo with thumbnails, all fields, the links (a tap on a chip opens that entry, even in another list) and *linked from* — the entries of other lists that point here; ◀ ▶ step through the entries. A link stores the names of the entries (`A | B`), so it survives export and import; renaming an entry or a list updates the links that point to it. Template *Collection (cards with a photo)* gives a ready start. Logic: `modules/table-cards.js`
- **Checklist** (field type *checklist*): a task list for every record — shopping in several shops, things to do at each point of a trip. The field is not stored: it shows `☑ 3/7` (done / all), counted from another list whose rows have a *link* to the record and a *checkbox*. Pick the type in **▦ schema → Fields** and the sub-list (`<list> <field>`: `name`, a link to this list, `done`) is created for you, or point the field at any list with a link and a checkbox column. A tap on `☑ 3/7` in the list (or the card of the record) shows the items with checkboxes and a field to add one; **open list ▸** drops into the sub-list filtered by the record, with **← record** above it to come back to the card, new rows there get the link filled in, and a tap on the ✓ cell ticks an item. For other nodes the row carries `<field>` (`3/7`), `<field>_done`, `<field>_total` and `check_done` / `check_total` (sums over the checklist fields) — filter by `check_done < check_total`. The Map shows `3/7` on the point (green when all done); a tap on such a point opens its items right on the map (tick, add), and the numbers follow through the Table. Only the active and the ticked lists are counted; deleting a record leaves its items in the sub-list.
- **Card layout, files, back** (▦ schema → *Card layout*): *customize* puts the fields on lines under the card title — a field list per line, ▲ ▼ move a line, a click on a field removes it; *captions* adds field names before the values, *photo block* off gives text-only cards (a card without a photo keeps its letter tile and no longer collapses in a long list). Field type **file (from Files)** stores file ids (`fl-12`; photos of the *images* space as `ph-…`) in the cell: **📁 pick** in the form opens the file manager (double click or *＋ selected*), the cell shows the file name — click opens an image / plays audio / downloads the rest. After a jump along a link (a chip on a card) the card has **←** to return to the previous entry and list; ✕ clears the trail.
- **Table, list with icons** (👤 view button): a row = avatar, name, values. Column types *avatar / image* (an `https://` link, a `data:` image, a photo from Photos, an emoji; otherwise a letter on a colour), *online status* (online / away / busy / offline, or 1 / 0 — a dot on the avatar) and *typing* (animated dots instead of the values); columns named `avatar`, `online`, `typing` work without a schema. Good for a contact list and a list of received SSTV pictures.
- **Column templates**: **＋** opens a menu — an empty list, or a list created at once with the columns of a template — and **⊞** adds the columns of a template that are missing to the active list (the data and the other columns stay; a name that already exists is only completed, never overwritten). Templates: *Map points* (`name lat lon alt h icon color note`), *Field measurements (point + height)* (what Mark Point writes: `name t session tx lat lon alt h freq rssi azimuth elevation rx_ant rx_gain note photo`), *Frequencies* (`name freq demod bw color note`), *Frequency ranges / band plan* (`name lo hi step demod color kind note`), *Transmitters* (`name lat lon h freq erp_w pol azimuth beamwidth note`), *Receiving points* (`name lat lon alt h rx_ant rx_gain note photo`), *Links* (`name lat lon h lat2 lon2 h2 freq kind color label note`), *Antennas* (catalogue), *Antenna tests* (the same source with another antenna / height / site: `name t site lat lon ant h freq rssi snr note photo`), *Signal log*, *Track* and *Photo log*. Column names are the ones the rest of the app reads (the Map, Video Overlay, Mark Point, Source Locator, Radio Reach, Signal Paths, the Sequencer, the Band Scanner); each template proposes a folder (`field/`, `sources/`, `links/`…). Logic: `modules/table-templates.js`, `node tools/test-tabletemplates.mjs`.
- **Field rules** (▦ schema → Fields; `modules/table-rules.js`, `node tools/test-tablerules.mjs`): *record name* — a template like `{Surname} {Name}` for links and cards; *auto number* — the next number on every new row (the counter lives in the list and does not roll back after deletes; clearing the list resets it); *calculated* — a formula such as `[price] * [qty]`, `round([a]/[b],2)`, `if([x]>5,'big','small')` (own parser, no `eval`: operators, `?:`, functions `round floor ceil abs sqrt pow min max if coalesce concat upper lower trim len substr contains num text now year month day`); with *stored* the value is written to the list when the row is saved (frozen until it is edited or recalculated), without it the value is computed on load and never stored; *unique* — groups of fields separated by `;` (`Surname, Name; Phone`): a repeat is refused in the form and skipped for wired rows (an empty part of a group is ignored, case and spaces do not count); per field: *default* for new rows and *required*.
- **Output ports** (▦ schema → Fields, the **→** tick): which fields come out as wires. Old patches keep every field as a port until you touch a tick; after that only ticked fields have one (wires to unticked ports are removed). Under **⋯** of a *link* field, tick fields of the linked record (*linked record → outputs*): ports `Link.Field` give that field of the first linked record of the current row (read when the list loads or the ticks change). `node tools/test-tableports.mjs`
- **Rows**: `+ row`, ✎ edit, 🗑 delete, fields are added, renamed (click the name; the data stays), typed and removed in **▦ schema**; a `color` column is a colour picker, `demod` a mode list, frequency fields (`lo`, `hi`, `freq`, `step`…) accept `7.1M`, `433k`, `14 MHz`. A row shows on one line — colour, name, value (a frequency range as `7.0M-7.2M`). Click a row: it is selected and goes out. Lists of any size work, 300 rows are drawn (*tail* shows the last ones).
- **Columns view** (**▦ schema**, or *⋯ → Fields & colors…*; the panel stays open while you edit fields — one list: a **show** tick (card and column), name (click to rename; data stays), type, **⋯** for choices / link, format and width, **⧉ duplicate** and **✕ remove** (no question — an **Undo** note brings the field and its values back); drag a row to change the order; *Row colors* is folded): the list is a grid like a packet list in Wireshark — a sticky header, the row number, one column per field (layout *compact* brings back the old *name · values* look). Click a column header to sort by it (▲ → ▼ → off). Per column: show / hide, order (drag), format (`auto`, `text`, `hz`, `time`, `hex`, `num`, `swatch`) and width in px (empty — by content). `auto` shows frequencies as `7.1M`, time columns as `hh:mm:ss.mmm`, photo and audio columns as clickable icons. The view is saved in the patch.
- **Row colours**: rules *filter → colour* (the same syntax as the filter above: `err>0`, `proto:udp`, `level:warn`); the first matching rule paints the row. A row no rule matched gets the default colour (*otherwise colour by*): the `color` column → an error column (`err`, `error`, `crc_err`, `bad` not 0) → `level` / `severity` / `status` (`err…` red, `warn…` yellow) → a hue per value of the first of `proto`, `type`, `kind`, `mode`, `service`, `modulation`, `demod`, `class`, `category`, `dir`. The select also takes any column of your own, or *nothing*.
- **⛶ Expand canvas**: in the header of a module with a main canvas (spectrum, scope, waterfall…) and of Table. The parameters are hidden and the canvas (or the list) takes the whole module; the size of the module does not change and the ports stay. ⛶ again brings the parameters back. In a tile, ⛶ in the pane header does the same for Table: only the list tools stay.
- **Filter**: words from the start of any field (`20m` does not find `120m`), all words must match; `demod:am` — in one field (`a|b` — either, `to:` — the field is not empty, a leading `-` excludes: `-kind:ambe`), `lo>7M`, `step<=1000`, `name=FM` — comparisons. The filter limits the list on screen, what is played and the `bands` output.
- **Import / export**: `import` takes CSV, TSV, TXT (a line is a record), JSON (an array of objects, an object holding such an array, JSON Lines), KML, GPX, GeoJSON — several files at once, each becomes a list named after the file (in the current folder). `export` saves the list as CSV, TSV or JSON (the ⋯ menu); `export all` saves every list of the browser DB in one JSON file, `import` brings them back.
- **Records in**: the `rec` wire adds records (decoders, other tables, any source), `text` adds lines (a line is a record with a `text` field; with *text → columns* in the fields panel CSV lines go into the list's columns), new fields become new columns. Logging numbers is a separate node, **Value Log** (Output, `modules/vallog.js`, `node tools/test-vallog.mjs`): inputs `a–d` become a `rec` with a timestamp `t` and the *columns* names every *period* s, on a `trig` edge, or once with *Add row now* (save the current frequency as a bookmark); wire its `rec` to a Table (with a *key* column it updates a row instead). Old patches with *log inputs a–d* get a Value Log next to the Table. *max rows* (fields panel) drops the oldest rows (a log does not grow forever; in `@patch` at most 1000).
- **The settings are the columns' (and the list's), not the node's**: how a list takes records is set in the **fields panel** (▦ schema) and is kept with the list — in its schema, so it travels with copies, exports and backups, and every Table on that list behaves the same. A column has *key*, *text out*, *sort* (also a click on its header: ▲ → ▼ → off) and *from packet*; the list has *unique*, *max rows*, *text → columns*, *all packet fields*, *newest first*, *rows wire* (what a set on the `rows` input does: replace / add / sync / new only) and *first row selected at start*. The node has no settings of its own any more; the list scrolls after new rows by itself while it is at the end (with *newest first* it stays on top), and the status line shows only an error. Old parameters of the node (`key`, `textCol`, `maxRows`, `pfields`, `textCsv`, `rev`, `sortCol`, `sortDir`, `initial`, `rowsIn`) are moved into the list the first time it is loaded.
- **Upsert** (*key* on a column): with a key column set, a record from `rec` whose key already exists updates that row (non-empty fields win) instead of adding a new one, and the `count`, `first`, `last` columns are kept. One table per entity type (towers by `plmn-lac-cid`, subscribers by id…) gives a deduplicated list.
- **Packet fields** (*from packet* on a column): the column is filled from every wired `rec` before it is stored. The value is a path into the record (`a.b.c`) or a field of the record's `hex` parsed by its `hexSchema` (as in HEX Structure; with no `hexSchema` the schema is detected from the bytes), e.g. column `type` ← `mtype_name`, column `addr` ← `body.devaddr` for a LoRaWAN frame; *all packet fields* (list) adds every parsed field as a column. After you edit it, the rows already in the list are filled too (empty cells only, nothing is overwritten).
- **Time**: *▦ schema* → *time: UTC / local* sets how time columns (`t`, `time`, `ts`, `date`, or the *time* format) are shown; the stored values are not changed.
- **Keys**: `↑` `↓` `PgUp` `PgDn` `Home` `End` move the selected row of the list (click it first); the selection goes to the outputs as with a click.
- **Out**: one record at a time. Every column is an output port (a column named like a fixed port gets a `_`); `rec` carries the whole row as a record (for the map, Rec Log, **Graph**), `text` — the column marked *text out* (or the whole row), `row` / `count` / `next` (pulse per selection), `list` (the name of the active list). Nothing goes out until a row is selected; stepping through rows is Row Player. `rows` is the one list output: the records of all lists ticked in the tree (the active one and the others, with the common filter) as one set; it changes only when the lists do. Wire it to Graph, Map and Video Overlay `rows`, to another Table's `rows` input (stores the set), and to a Spectrum Analyzer `bands`, Signal Identifier `plan`, Channel / Band Scanner `bands` and `skip`: rows with `lo` (or `freq`), optional `hi`, `name` / `label`, `color`, `step` get numeric `lo` / `hi` / `step` / `label` / `color` added, and the receivers of frequency ranges take only the records that have a `lo`. (Older patches with the `bands` output are converted to `rows` on load.) `mid` and `span` of the selected row appear next to `lo` / `hi` / `step`. `sigs` takes the labels of a Signal Identifier, as the band plan did. The `rows` input stores a whole set (Internet Radio stations, another Table, any list of records) in the active list: *rows wire* — replace its rows (a new search replaces the old list) or add to them; new columns appear from the fields of the records. Raw packets work too: put them in a column (hex, text) and wire the port.

### Points on the Earth's surface

Everything that stands on or near the ground — airfields, runways, towns, peaks, **your own points** (transmitters, observation posts, test locations) — is a row in a Table list, so one set of points serves the map, the 3D overlay and the analysis.

- **Lists** (created by the loaders, editable by hand): `geo/airfields` (`id name icao iata kind lat lon ele src`), `geo/runways` (`id name airport lat lon lat2 lon2 width surface src`: both ends of the runway, `name` is the designator «09/27»), `geo/places` (`id name kind lat lon pop src`), `geo/peaks` (`id name lat lon ele src`). `src` says where a row came from (`ourairports`, `osm`, `builtin`, or `user` for your own). A row with a known `id` is never overwritten by a loader, so edits and deletions of columns stay; add rows with any `id` of your own.
- **Your points**: any list with `name`, `lat`, `lon` (and, optionally, `h` — antenna / mast height above ground in m, `alt` — above sea level, `color`, `freq`, `erp`… any other columns). Wire the Table's `rec` or `rows` to the **Map** (labels are taken from `label`, else `name`, else `id`; a runway with `lat2` / `lon2` is a line) and type the list name into *Video Overlay → own points* to see the same points in 3D at their real height over the terrain.
- **Where the data comes from**: *Video Overlay* (*airfields*, *towns*, *Download area for offline*) and the *OSM* option fill the lists around the camera; *Flight Sim → start: nearest runway* reads `geo/runways`. Terrain tiles stay in the browser database too (Horizon), apart from the tables.
- The trash button in the toolbar only **refreshes the app files** (cache, service worker): lists, patches, terrain and every other kind of data are never erased by it. To keep a copy or move to another browser use **Settings → All data → Export… / Import…**: one `.dspbackup` file with the patches and settings, all Table lists, terrain and map caches, samples, tracker sessions and the spectrum archive, in groups you tick; an import replaces records with the same key (a Table list is replaced as a whole), erases nothing and reloads the page. Logic: `modules/geostore.js`, `modules/backup.js`, `node tools/test-backup.mjs`.
- **Sync between devices** (**Settings → Sync devices → Sync…**): the file storage (Files, Audio, Images) and the records of every Table list are kept the same on all devices that share one key; the newer edit wins, a deletion is passed on and does not come back, folders are created as needed, the column setup of a list travels with it. Not synced: patches and settings, terrain and map caches, the spectrum archive, tracker sessions (use Export / Import for those); ids of files differ between devices, so a patch that points to a file by id keeps pointing at the local id. How it works: *New key* makes a 128-bit key of the pair; **Save to Google account** puts it into Chrome's password manager (Credential Management API), so the other device signed in to the same account gets it with **Get from Google account**; as a fallback *Copy link* gives an address with `#sync=<key>` for “Send to your devices” and the key can be typed in. The key derives the room and the cipher of the connection offers that two devices exchange through an MQTT broker over WebSocket (default `wss://broker.emqx.io:8084/mqtt`, any public or own one: the broker sees a hashed topic, addresses and timing, not the content); the data itself goes straight between the devices over WebRTC (a STUN server is needed unless they are on one network; no TURN, so some mobile networks will not connect). More than two devices connect each to each. Both devices have to be open at the same time; a file larger than *largest file* is skipped. The key stays out of the `.dspbackup` file. **Sync Data** (Sources): a text channel for the graph over the same link — a second WebRTC data channel (`app`) next to the file one. Each device has a name (Sync devices → *Name of this device*); `send` goes to *all devices* or to the one picked in *send to* (the `to` input overrides it with a name); `line` / `go` / `rec` (JSON becomes records) / `from` (sender) / `count` / `devices` come out. The *channel* name keeps different uses apart: a node takes only messages of its own channel. Not only text: `sendFile` (a Files id — a video, a recording, an IQ file…) or the *Send file…* button send a file, `img` sends the graph's pictures as PNG (no more often than *imgGap*), `rec` sends records as JSON; on the other side `file` (id in the Received folder of Files / Audio / Images), `got`, `img` and `rec` come out. Files go over a third channel (`file`) with the file-transfer core `modules/xfer-kernels.js` (the same wire format as the file channel of WebRTC Data; `node tools/test-xfer.mjs`): a file is refused before its content is sent if it is over *largest file* or no Sync Data with that channel name is open. Live video is not streamed this way (send a recorded file; a live picture goes as a stream of PNG frames at most). Works while the sync is on and the devices are connected; presets **Sync: Chat Between Your Devices** and **Sync: Files, Video and Pictures Between Devices**.
- **Sync Video Out / Sync Video In** (Output / Sources, `modules/syncvideo.js`, format in `modules/mjpeg-kernels.js`, `node tools/test-mjpeg.mjs`): live picture over the same sync link as **MJPEG**. Out takes `vid` (camera, video file) or `img`, makes JPEG frames (*fps* up to 25, *max width*, quality *wish*, *target frame size*) and sends them to *all devices* or the chosen one over a separate data channel without retransmits (a busy channel skips the frame, a lost frame is not resent, an older frame never replaces a newer one); the JPEG quality lowers itself when frames are bigger than the target. In with the same *stream name* outputs `img` (width 160 – 640), `active`, `fps` and `from`; only the newest frame is decoded. Several streams at once — different names. Not sound: for a voice use WebRTC Audio Out / In on a WebRTC Data connection. Preset **Sync: Live Video (MJPEG) Between Devices**.
- **Voice and video in packets** (`modules/media.js`, format, jitter buffer and framing in `modules/media-kernels.js`, `node tools/test-media.mjs`): four nodes that turn sound and picture into messages of the `bin` wire and back, so *any* channel that carries `bin` can carry a call — Sync Data between your own devices, MQTT, Packet Link (WebSocket, serial, BLE UART), and through a radio modem. **Voice → Packets** (microphone or any sound, `ptt` input, *send only above* gate so pauses cost nothing): **Codec 2** 1.6 / 3.2 kbit/s (for radio: 8 bytes per 40 / 20 ms; the codec2.wasm of the Vocoder), **Opus** 6–32 kbit/s (WebCodecs; conference quality), **μ-law** 64 kbit/s (works in every browser); *frames per packet* trades delay for header overhead. **Packets → Voice**: a jitter buffer (*delay to smooth the link*, at least 1.5 packets) puts packets back in order, hides a lost packet with a faded repeat, drops late ones, never lets the queue grow, and starts over when the sender restarts; outputs `lost` and `buf`. **Video → Packets**: JPEG frames from `vid` / `img` (*fps* from 0.1 to 25, width 64–960, quality wish, target size, grayscale) — a slow-scan picture over a few kbit/s or a 25 fps stream; **Packets → Video** shows the newest frame (`img`, `fps`). A packet is 5 header bytes (codec, stream name, number, frames) + the codec frames; several streams share a link, each receiver takes its own *stream name* and kind. *Framing* (none / len8 / len16be / slip / kiss / cobs) wraps a packet for a byte stream (serial radio modem, KISS TNC, BLE UART): set the same *frame* in Packet Link, which cuts the stream back into packets (checked against its framers). Sync Data takes and gives `bin` as datagrams (channel *conf-…*, a lost one is not resent, up to 200 KB). Presets **Conference: Voice and Video Between Your Devices (Sync Data)** and **Voice and Video over a Byte Link (KISS / SLIP, no hardware)**. Checked in headless Chromium: all four codecs through the nodes (a 440 Hz tone comes back at 440 Hz with Opus and μ-law, Codec 2 as a vocoder tone), a picture with a QR code read back from the received JPEG, voice + video through two Sync Data nodes with an in-page device, KISS / SLIP through Packet Link; real microphones, cameras, two devices and a radio modem have not been tried. Codec 2 is built here with the modes 3200 and 1600 only (700C / 1200 need a rebuild of `vendor/codec2.wasm`). Logic: `modules/sync-kernels.js` (merge, protocol), `modules/sync.js` (storage, connection, dialog), `node tools/test-sync.mjs`. Checked in headless Chromium with two profiles and a local broker (records, a file in nested folders, column setup, edit, deletion of a record and of a file); the account password manager and real phones have not been tried.

### Terrain height, more layers, vector files, open data

- **A point with the height map** (answer to «how do I add a point that knows the terrain»): a tap on an empty spot of the **Map** shows `ground: N m` (terrain height above sea level at that spot) and the `pick` record carries `ground`. Wire `pick` (or any record with `lat` / `lon`: a *Table* row, *Mark Point*) through **Point Elevation** (Geo): it adds `ground` and, from `h` (height above the ground, m; the `height above ground` parameter fills it when a point has none), `alt = ground + h`; a record with `alt` (GPS, above sea level) and no `h` gets `h = alt − ground`. `force` recalculates `alt` even when it is set. Wires `lat` / `lon` give the numbers `ground` and `alt`. The heights are the Terrarium tiles that *Horizon* uses (one download, kept in the browser; with *download missing terrain tiles* off only the saved ones are used). In a **Table** list, *🌐 open data → ⛰ Fill ground elevation* adds the column `ground` for all rows with `lat` / `lon` (and `alt` / `h` where those columns exist and are empty).
- **More maps and layers** (Map): `tiles` — OSM, OpenTopoMap, OSM Humanitarian, CyclOSM, CARTO Dark / Light, Esri Satellite / Topo / Streets, **Relief (DEM)** (hypsometric colours with shading, drawn from the same terrain tiles — no extra server) or `custom` (a URL with `{z}/{x}/{y}` in advanced). `overlay` — Hillshade (DEM or Esri), Railways, Sea marks, Hiking trails, Cycling routes or custom — drawn over any base with the `overlay opacity`. Everything viewed is cached for offline use; the attribution of the active layers is shown in the corner. Tile servers have their own usage policies — bulk downloading is not done.
- **Vector layers** (Map): *Load vector layer* takes **GeoJSON, KML or GPX** — lines, polygons (with holes) and points. Colours come from `stroke` / `fill` / `fill-opacity` / `marker-color` properties (otherwise a palette); points become ordinary map objects with their properties. The layers are kept in the browser and restored with the patch (only their names are in it); *Remove vector layers* clears them. Shapefile / GeoPackage must be converted to GeoJSON first.
- **Open data → Table** (button **🌐 open data**; the area is «lat, lon, radius km» or `view` — what the Map shows): **cell towers** from OpenStreetMap (Overpass, no key; masts tagged as mobile / GSM / UMTS / LTE / 5G), from **OpenCelliD** (`getInArea` with your free API key, kept in this browser) or from an OpenCelliD **dump file** (`cell_towers.csv` / `.csv.gz`, read as a stream and filtered by area and MCC — nothing else is kept in memory). Rows go to `geo/towers` (`id name operator kind radio ref lat lon h ele mcc mnc lac cid range samples updated icon radius src`; an OpenCelliD estimated `range` becomes the `radius` circle on the Map), a known `id` is never overwritten. Also: broadcast FM / TV masts, all communication masts, amateur radio repeaters (`geo/repeaters`), peaks (`geo/peaks`) and **your own Overpass query** (`{{bbox}}` is replaced by the area; columns come from the tags found, → `geo/osm`). The Map has the same for the visible area (*objects from OpenStreetMap → Load objects in view*): the objects appear on the map at once and are saved to the list. OSM coverage of towers depends on volunteers; OpenCelliD positions are crowd-sourced estimates, not surveyed sites. The Overpass servers are shared — requests go one at a time with pauses. The kernels are checked by `node tools/test-geodata.mjs`.

- **Signal Paths** (Radio): what paths a radio signal can take between two points. The ends are `lat` / `lon` and `lat2` / `lon2` (wires, *My Position*) or one record with both (a *Table* row: `lat lon h lat2 lon2 h2 freq name`, which also overrides the parameters *h*, *h2*, *frequency*); the terrain is the *Horizon* height map (both points must lie inside its radius; without Horizon the ground is flat at *ground height*). **Direct path**: line of sight over the terrain with the curvature of the Earth (*k*), how much of the **first Fresnel zone** is clear (≥ 60 % is the usual target), the **diffraction** loss on the main obstacle (knife edge, ITU-R P.526) and the total loss with the free-space part. **Reflections**: the single specular reflections off the terrain — the point where the reflected ray from one end hits the other (a grid search, then refined with the mirror-image method), kept only if both legs run over the ground; a flat ground gives the ground bounce in the middle, a steep slope gives a reflection off the slope. For each path: the reflection point, extra length and **delay** (ns), level against the direct path (*reflection loss* is a parameter: 8 dB rough ground, 2…6 dB water or smooth ground); *tolerance* is how far (m) the reflected ray may miss the other end, *longest path ÷ direct* limits how far off the axis a reflector may be. The tile shows a **side view** (terrain with the curvature, Fresnel zone, direct path coloured green / yellow (Fresnel < 60 %) / red (blocked), reflected paths dashed with their lateral offset) and the numbers. **Out**: `rec` — the paths as **links** (the direct one with `alt` / `alt2`, `loss_db`, `fspl_db`, `diff_db`, `los`, `fresnel`; the reflected ones as `path3` through the reflection point with `rel_db`, `delay_ns`, `excess_m`), once per calculation — wire to the Map, to Video Overlay `rec2` (3D, free camera) or to a Table; `loss`, `los`, `fres`, `dist`, `n`, `spread` (the largest delay of the reflections within 20 dB of the direct path, ns) as numbers. *Save to table* keeps the links in `analysis/paths` (`path3` as JSON text — Overlay and the Map read it back). Single reflections only (no double bounces, no vegetation, buildings or diffraction on the reflected legs). Preset *Signal Paths: Direct and Reflected Between Two Points*. Logic: `modules/paths-kernels.js`, `node tools/test-paths.mjs`.
- **Coverage Map** (Radio): a preliminary calculation of where the signal of your transmitters is weak or absent. Transmitters are Table lists in *transmitters* (`name lat lon h freq erp_w azimuth beamwidth`; the *Transmitters* column template) and/or records on `rec` (accumulated by `name`; *Forget transmitters from rec* clears them); `azimuth` + `beamwidth` give a directional lobe (−25 dB at most), `freq` empty → *frequency*; *only these* filters by name or frequency. The terrain is the *Horizon* height map (the radius must fit in it); for every cell of the grid (*cell*, 0 = auto, ~120 across) and every transmitter the path loss is: free space + **diffraction** over the terrain (Deygout, up to 3 edges, curvature with *k*) + optional **two-ray ground interference** on line of sight (lobes and nulls), + *extra loss* (buildings, trees). The level is ERP + 2.15 dB − loss + receiving antenna gain (*rxgain*, *rxh* height). The map shows the strongest signal (or the number of signals above *receiver sensitivity*); cells below it are **dead zones** — their percent and area are in the readout and on `dead` / `area`, and their edge goes out on `poly` as dashed `path` records for the Map. **Field comparison**: *field measurements* is a Table list (`lat lon rssi` dBm, `h`, `tx`, `freq`); every point is calculated from its own transmitter (the `tx` column) or the strongest, the readout shows **bias** and **rms** (measured − calculated, dB; `bias` / `rms` / `n` outputs); `rec` carries a summary and a *residual* record per point (blue — stronger than calculated, orange — weaker); *Save residuals to table* → `analysis/coverage`; *Calibrate* removes the bias through the extra loss (never below 0). **Raster on the Map**: the `raster` output is a coverage image (strongest signal, or the count above the threshold; cells without terrain are transparent) with its lat / lon bounds — wire it to the Map (`rec` / `rec2` / `rec3`) and it is drawn under the objects and over the tiles, with the *raster opacity* of Coverage; a record `{kind:'raster', id, canvas, north, south, west, east, opacity}` from any calculator works the same way (`gone` removes it; *Clear* clears rasters too; they are not saved with the points). Preset *Coverage: Transmitters from Table → Dead Zones*. No buildings, vegetation or reflections off the terrain (see Signal Paths for two-point reflections). Logic: `modules/coverage-kernels.js`, `node tools/test-coverage.mjs`.
- **Photos**: a Table column named `photo` (or `photos`) holds photo ids (`ph-…`); in the row form **📷 add** takes pictures from the camera / gallery (compressed to JPEG ≤1600 px and kept in the browser database *dsp-photos*, so they work offline and go into the full backup), **view** opens them, and when `lat` / `lon` / `alt` / `t` of the row are empty they are filled from the EXIF of the photo (a phone without GPS in the node still gets the place of the picture). A row with photos shows a 📷N badge — a click opens the viewer (arrows, place, time). Export of a single list as CSV carries the ids only; the photos travel in *Settings → All data → Export* (group *Photos of measurements, samples…*).
- **📍 here** (Table toolbar): a new row at your current position — GPS of the browser, otherwise the last *My Position* — with the time and the next name (`P1`, `P2`…); the same form has the icon picker, **📷 add** (a photo; the place and time of a picture without GPS come from its EXIF) and the sound buttons, so a point with a photo and a recording needs no other module. A list without `lat` / `lon` gets the columns of *Map points* on request. Wire the list's `rows` to the Map `rows`.
- **Audio**: a Table column named `audio` holds ids of clips of Files › audio (`sm-12 sm-15`); in the row form **🎤 rec** records from the microphone, **📁 file** adds audio files, **🎵 library** takes a ready clip, **▶** plays. New clips go to the library folder *Attachments*, so the sound stays one object: it opens in Sample Library / Sampler and travels in *Settings → All data → Export* together with the samples (group *Photos of measurements, samples…*); a row with sound shows a 🔊N badge. Logic: `modules/audio-kernels.js`, `node tools/test-audio.mjs`.
- **Icons**: the `icon` column of the row form is a picker (map icons and a few emoji; any short text still works when typed into the cell).
- **One dataset — a network and a map**: node rows (`id`, `name`, `lat`, `lon`, `icon`, `color`, `size`…) and link rows (`from`, `to`, `weight`, `label`, `color`) can sit in one list (or in several ticked lists) and go with `rows` both to **Graph** and to **Map**: a row with `from` and `to` is a link, any other row is a node. Graph draws the links as a network, Map puts the nodes at their coordinates with icons (`dot square diamond triangle star flag antenna tx rx plane ship sat balloon` or any emoji; without `icon` the node's `shape` is mapped, and Graph takes a node's shape from `icon` the same way) and draws the links as lines between them (width — `weight`, `label` in the middle, *arrows on links*). Both take the whole set and apply only the difference: an edited, added or removed row changes just that node or link; a node without `lat` / `lon` stays only in the graph. Preset *Network: Same Nodes and Links as Graph and on the Map*. **Video Overlay** takes the same `rows` wire: the nodes become objects in the camera view (name, distance, bearing, line of sight; `icon` picks the model, `color`), the links become segments between them in 3D. Height: `alt` — above sea level, otherwise `h` — above the terrain (from Horizon when its heights are loaded; the default is the ground). Nodes and links of a set do not expire by *keep an object without updates*; a node without `lat` / `lon` is skipped.
- **Links** (the signal path between two points): a record with two ends — `lat lon h` → `lat2 lon2 h2` (`h` above the ground, `alt` / `alt2` above sea level) — or a polyline `path3: [[lat, lon, alt], …]` for a path with reflections (the inner vertices are the reflection points); `kind` `reflect` is drawn dashed, `color`, `label`, `loss_db` are shown.
