The **Map** draws them (`lat2` / `lon2` as a great-circle line, `path3` as a polyline), **Video Overlay** draws them in 3D at their real heights over the terrain — records on `rec` / `rec2` with an `id`, or rows of a Table list typed into *own points* (a row with `lat2` / `lon2` is a link, its start is also a point). Calculators that find the paths between two points output these records. Preset *Signal Paths: Links in 3D (Free Fly)*.
- **Free-fly camera** (*Flight Sim* → `cam` = *free*): the camera flies on its own, the aircraft stands still — `ail` turns, `elev` looks up / down, `thr` flies forward / back **along the view** (with a *Joystick* in *hold* mode it is a cruise), `rud` steps sideways, *free camera speed* sets the speed; the camera never goes below the ground. Space (`cam` input) switches between *chase*, *cockpit*, *tower*, *orbit* and *free*.

### Timeline

**Timeline** (Analysis): rows of a Table drawn as intervals on a time axis — for airtime analysis (who transmitted on which frequency, how often and for how long) and, with the same columns, as a clip arranger. The storage stays the Table: wire its `rows` (or `all`) to the Timeline; the Timeline gives out the selected rows and the measurements and, when clips are edited with the mouse, the rows with the changes (`edited`) to write back into the Table. It also mixes the audio of clips, replays IQ clips, has markers and takes live events.

- **Row = event**: a start (`t`, `time`, `ts`, `start`…), a length (`dur` / `duration` / `airtime`, or an end column `end` / `t1`; without them the event is a point), any other columns are free: frequency, subscriber, packet type, `rssi`, `file`. The time unit is recognised by size (epoch milliseconds / microseconds, small numbers are seconds) or set by hand. Columns are found by name or set in the advanced parameters.
- **Lanes** by a column or several (`freq,src`: one lane per pair); **colour** by another column (packet kind); **bar height = rssi**; **filter** `kind=req,ack src=A* rssi>-70 freq!=433.92 name~regex word` (all terms must match; the `filter` input adds to it).
- **Transmissions**: *merge packets closer than, ms* glues the packets of one lane into one air transmission; everything below is measured on transmissions.
- **Measurements** per lane (and over the whole selection): count, **period** (median start-to-start; min / max / mean), **duration** (median / min / max), **duty**, rate, idle, **jitter** (spread of the repeats relative to the period), `ok` (share of intervals that are a whole multiple of the period within *period tolerance* — a repeat even if some were skipped) and `miss` (skipped repeats), rssi. *Reference events* (a filter such as `kind=req`) add `lag`: the time from a reference event to the next event of the lane (a reply to a request), its spread and count. *Link by column*: select an event — every event with the same value (a subscriber, an address) is outlined on all lanes.
- **Mouse**: wheel — zoom around the pointer, drag — pan, **drag the ruler** — move the playhead, **Shift+drag** — measure a span (the numbers and outputs then cover only it), click an event — its fields, the interval from the previous start and the pause, click a lane name — only that lane, **double click** an event — play from it, double click an empty spot — fit all, Shift+wheel — scroll lanes. *Follow the end* keeps the view on a Table that is still collecting live packets.
- **Playback**: *Play* (or Space) moves the playhead (speed ×0.1…64, loop, or loop within the selection); the `at` input sets the playhead from outside (seconds from the first event). Events that start under the playhead go to `rec` (all columns plus `_t`, `_dur`) and `file`. With *play audio files of events* an event whose `file` column holds an id or a name from Files is played by the browser (audio / video files, cut at the event length, optional `off` column — start offset, s). For mixing and IQ see *Clips: mix and IQ*.
- **Editing clips** (*edit clips with the mouse*): drag a clip — move it in time and to another lane (the lane column(s) of the row get the lane's values), drag its edge — length, **Alt+drag** — copy, **Ctrl+click** — add to the selection, Ctrl+A — all, Delete — remove, Ctrl+D — duplicate after the selection, arrows (Shift — ×10) — nudge, **double click on an empty spot** of a lane — a new clip (*length of a new clip*), **Ctrl+Z / Ctrl+Y** — undo / redo (the canvas must be focused: click it), *Set a column on the selected clips…* — `file=beep.wav`, `gain=-6`, `fade=0.2`, any column. While dragging, edges **snap** to the ruler grid, edges of other clips, markers and the playhead (Shift — without snapping). The Table stays the storage: the changes lie over its rows (in the patch parameter `edits`) and go out of **`edited`** (all rows with the changes) and **`diff`** (only the changed ones: `_op` = set / add / del, `_key`). To write them back wire `edited` into a Table (*rows in* = sync, a key column `id`); when the Table has accepted a row the overlay of that row is dropped on its own. The edits are matched to the rows by a key column (`id`, `key`, `uid`, `uuid` or *key column* in the advanced parameters; a new clip gets a unique value in it), without it by the row number — then wire `edited` into a separate Table. The time is written back in the units of the columns (`t`, and `dur` or `end`; a point event gets a `dur` column when it is resized). *Export rows with edits (CSV)* saves the same rows.
- **Clips: mix and IQ**: with *mix clips to the audio output* the audio / video files of the clips under the playhead are mixed lane by lane into the mono **`audio`** output (wire it to a Sound Card, a Recorder or any filter): row columns `off` (start in the file, s), `gain` (dB), `fadein` / `fadeout` / `fade` (s); *mix level* in dB; at speed ≠ 1 the pitch follows the speed. **Lane effects** (*lane effects*): a line per lane, `Voice*: gain=-6 lp=3000 hp=150 echo=250,.4 mute solo` — a name with `*`, gain in dB, low-pass / high-pass in Hz, echo (delay ms, feedback), `mute`, `solo` (the other lanes go silent); the lane tags `M` / `S` / `fx` show in the lane label. With *replay IQ files of clips to the iq output* the IQ recording in the `file` column (cu8 / cs8 / cs16 / cf32 / cf64 raw — the sample rate from the name like `rec_2Msps.cu8` or from *sample rate of raw IQ files* —, SigMF, WAV IQ; from Files) is sent to the **`iq`** output from the clip start (plus `off`) at its real sample rate × speed, the first IQ clip under the playhead wins. Video clips: their sound is mixed, the picture is not shown. Files are decoded in memory (up to about 40 M samples of audio, an audio file up to 200 MB); the readout shows how many are ready.
- **Markers**: **M** — a marker at the playhead (or the view centre), click the flag on the ruler — go there, drag — move, double click — rename, Delete — remove; they are kept in the patch. The `marks` input shows external markers (rows with a time and `label` / `text` / `name`, another colour); the `marks` output gives the own markers (`label`, `t` — s on the axis, `abs` — absolute time in seconds), `mark` — a record when the playhead passes a marker.
- **Live events** without a Table: the `rec` input appends records (an `t` stamp is added when the record has no time column) and the `gate` input turns a signal into events — a rising edge over *gate threshold* starts an event, a falling edge ends it (lane *lane of gate events*, time = the computer's clock in ms, `dur` in ms); they join the rows of the Table (so with a Table in seconds from the start the live events lie far away: use a Table with absolute time or only live events). *live events kept* limits the buffer, *Clear live events* empties it. With *follow the end* the view follows.
- **Export**: *Export PNG* saves the picture of the timeline (lanes, markers, selection).
- **Outputs**: `rows` (the selected events as the original rows), `stats` (one row per lane: `lane n t0 t1 span dur… duty rate period… idle ok miss jitter rssi lag…`, seconds; wire it to a Table with key `lane` and *rows in = sync*, or to Table Chart), `edited`, `diff`, `marks`, `mark`, `audio`, `iq`, `rec`, `file`, `on` (events under the playhead), `t`, and for the selection `count`, `period`, `dur`, `duty`, `hit`.
- Not there yet: effects other than gain / filters / echo on a lane, a picture of video clips, stereo mixing, creating a new lane by dragging below the last one (a new lane appears when a clip with a new lane value is added in the Table).

**Activity Events** (Analysis) feeds a Timeline from a spectrum: it takes a grid of channels (*first channel centre*, *step*, *channels*, *width*) from the `spec` of a USB SDR / FFT and turns channel activity into events. Each frame the common level of the band (the lower third of the channels) is subtracted, so an AGC / gain step or a wideband noise rise does not make every channel busy at once; the result is smoothed over *smoothing* ms. The floor of a channel is a low quantile of that level (learned fast during the first *learn* ms, then it follows the drift at *floor adaptation* dB/s and stays frozen while the channel is busy). A channel is busy when it is *busy above the floor* dB over it and free when it stays below *threshold − hysteresis* for *hold* ms (shorter pauses are bridged); events shorter than *shortest event* are dropped. *Channel level*: mean power of the channel band, or *peak* (the strongest bin — weak narrow carriers stand out more). The `rec` output gives one record per finished transmission — `t` (epoch ms), `dur` (ms), `freq` (MHz), `ch`, `rssi` (peak, dB), `avg`, `snr` — for a Table, which is the storage: Table → Timeline (lanes by `freq`). With a sweeping SDR the channels outside the current window are not observed and keep their state until the sweep comes back (the resolution is then one pass). Outputs `active` (channels busy now) and `count`. Check: `node tools/test-activity.mjs`.

Presets: *Timeline: Periods, Durations and Replies on the Air (Table)*, *Timeline: Clip Arranger (edit with the mouse, mix, markers)* (Table ↔ Timeline with write-back, mix to the sound card). *USB SDR: Channel Activity Timeline (PMR446)*: RTL-SDR → Activity Events (16 channels of 12.5 kHz) → Table → Timeline → Table with the results per channel (needs the hardware). Check of the kernel: `node tools/test-timeline.mjs`.

### Row Player

**Row Player** (Sources) plays the rows of a list one by one — to imitate a moving vehicle, step through frequencies, replay packets and calls, or send a beacon. Table stores and edits the list; Row Player only walks along it (`modules/rowplayer.js`, the stepping is `seqProcess` in `modules/sequencer.js`, `node tools/test-rowplayer.mjs`).

- **Rows come two ways.** Wire a [Table](#table)'s `rows` (or any set of records) to `rows` — the filter, the sort and *newest first* of the table apply, and this is the only way for a list that lives in the patch (`@patch`). Or give a **reference to storage**: the `list` field (or a text on the `list` input, e.g. from Table's `list` output) names a list of the browser DB or a built-in `presets/…` one; the player reads it itself, so a long list is not copied along a wire, and it follows the edits of the list (a reread at most once a second). The wire overrides the input, the input overrides the field.
- **Advance** (`trig` always steps by one row as well): **rate** — rows/s (0 = only `trig`); **dwell** — each row is held for the seconds in its *dwell col* (frequency stepping: `freq,dwell`); **time** — rows come out at their own timestamps (column `t`, `time`, `timestamp`… ISO, epoch s/ms or `HH:MM:SS`), speed `time ×`; with absolute timestamps and a *Time Base* on `t` the rows follow that clock (set it back and the replay restarts); **distance** — the next row comes out when the path along `lat`/`lon` reaches it at the given `speed` (km/h, m/s or knots; a *speed col* overrides it per row). With *interpolate* the marker moves smoothly between points (*rows/s* = update rate). `dist`, `bearing`, `progress` describe the current leg. Only the fields of the chosen mode are shown.
- **Order**: sequential, ping-pong, **random**, **shuffle** (every row once, then again); *loop*, *first row* / *last row* range, the `row` input picks a row (1…N).
- **Outputs**: `rec`, `text` (the *text col*, or the whole row), `row`, `count`, `next` (a pulse per row), `done`, `dist`, `bearing`, `progress`, every column as its own port, and `mid` / `span` for rows with a frequency (`lo`/`hi` or `freq`). A set that grows while it plays (a log) keeps the position; another list starts over.
- **Table now only shows and selects.** A click on a row (or the `row` input) sends that row to Table's own `rec`, `text`, `row` and field outputs; *first row selected at start* (List settings) sends the first row once. Its `list` output (the name of the active list, empty for `@patch`) is the reference for Row Player. Old patches are converted when they are opened: a Table that played rows (an *advance* other than *rate*, *rows/s* above 0, a wire on `trig` / `t`, or on `done` / `dist` / `bearing` / `progress`) gets a Row Player under it with its playback settings, the `trig` / `t` / `row` inputs and the row outputs move to the player, and Table's `rows` is wired to the player.
- **Trigger Clock** (Control): `trig` pulse and `gate` window. Modes: *interval* (with *jitter* %), *random* (min…max), *poisson* (mean = interval), *schedule* (`08:00, 12:30:15, */15s, */5m` — time of day or every N s/m/h, UTC or local; uses `t` from Time Base when wired). *burst* + *gap*, *delay*, *max N*, `gate` width (a window of N s after every trigger), `reset` input. The pulse lasts one block with a gap of one block between pulses.
- **Trigger Clock, mode *cron*** (Control; the former Cron node): a schedule written like crontab — five fields *minute hour day month weekday* (`*`, `a`, `a-b`, `a-b/n`, `*/n`, comma lists, names `mon…`/`jan…`, 7 = Sunday, `@hourly` `@daily` `@weekly` `@monthly`); if both day-of-month and weekday are given either one fires, as in Vixie cron. Outputs `trig` (a one-block pulse), `gate` (high for *gate length* seconds after each firing — record 10 minutes every hour), `n` (firings), `next` (seconds to the next). Zone UTC or local; time comes from the `t` input (*Time Base*, so a sped-up virtual clock works) or the system clock. A firing missed by rewinding or by a sleeping tab (> 2 min) is not caught up. Wire `gate` into a recorder or `trig` into the *Spectrum Archive*
- **Spectrum Archive** (Output): writes spectra into the browser's IndexedDB (`dsp-spectra`), so a long unattended watch of a band survives a reload. Input `spec` from any spectrum source (RTL-SDR, FFT, IQ Spectrum, tinySA…). A row is the *mean power* (or *max hold*, for sporadic signals) of all frames inside the period, reduced to *bins per row* by taking the maximum of each group, stored as 8 bits at 0.5 dB steps below the row's peak (127 dB of range, about 1 KB per 1024-bin row). Modes: *periodic* (a row every period), *gate* (rows only while the `gate` input is high — wire a *Cron* `gate` here; what is left when it drops is written), *trigger* (on each rising edge of `trig`, e.g. a *Cron* `trig`, record the next period). A session is a run of rows on one frequency axis; changing the axis or **New session** starts another. **Export CSV** (time, one column per bin, dB) and **Export PNG waterfall** take the current session. The oldest rows beyond *rows kept* are dropped; **Clear all** empties the archive. Outputs `rows` (this session) and `total`. Time stamps are the computer's clock (UTC in the export); a browser may throttle a background tab, so for days-long runs keep it in the foreground or use a kiosk window
- **Time Base** (Control): virtual time — the system clock or a manually set start (UTC) with any speed; outputs `t` (epoch s), `iso`, `tod`; the `set` input takes a new time.
- **Stopwatch** (Control): measures the time between a `start` event and a `stop` event — delays, data transfer time, round trips. Inputs `start`, `stop`, `reset` take any wire: a number or a signal (an event is a rising edge through *threshold*; in a signal the crossing is found between samples, so the edge is placed to a fraction of a sample, so the resolution is finer than the 21 µs sample step at 48 kHz) or a text / record / bytes wire (every new value is an event). *Buttons* **Start / Stop** and **Reset** work by hand and are stamped at the click. *Clock*: **wall** — real time from `performance.now()` (0.1 ms in a normal page, 5 µs when the page is cross-origin isolated; a wire event is stamped when the block is processed, so it is as coarse as the block, a few ms), **samples** — the node's own sample count: exact and independent of CPU load, the right choice for delays inside a signal chain (a pulse through a filter, a delay or a loopback), but it does not see real-world waits. *Mode*: **start/stop** or **toggle** (every `start` edge flips). *Start while running*: ignore or restart. Outputs: `ms` (last measurement), `elapsed` (live, ms), `run`, `done` (a pulse), `text` (`12.345 ms`), `mean`, `n` and `rec` — one record per measurement `{n, start, end, ms, clock}`: in *wall* `start` / `end` are epoch ms with a fraction, in *samples* ms on the node's sample timeline. The readout keeps n / min / avg / max / σ (jitter). Logic: `modules/stopwatch-kernels.js`, `node tools/test-stopwatch.mjs`

Presets: *Sequencer: Vehicle Track on the Map*, *Sequencer: Frequency Stepper (CSV → Oscillator)*, *Sequencer: Random Beacon (Trigger Clock)*, *Graph: Links from CSV*, *Sound Level Meter with Log*.

### Control logic

Small universal blocks on number wires (a pulse is 0/1, an edge is a crossing of 0.5; unwired inputs are ignored). The *inputs* select (2…8) adds or removes ports; every numeric parameter of every block can also be driven by a wire.

- **Math**: sum, mean, product, min, max, `in1 − rest`, `in1 ÷ rest`, `in1 ^ in2`, `|in1 − in2|`, then gain and offset.
- **Compare**: *above / below / inside / outside* a threshold or window, with hysteresis and a minimum on-time; outputs `out`, `rise`, `fall`. The tile is a small screen: the level history, threshold and hysteresis lines, the on-state shaded. Feed it from Level (dB), Frequency Meter, CFAR SNR, a trend or any number.
- **Logic**: AND, OR, XOR, NAND, NOR, XNOR of 2…8 inputs, `out` and `not`.
- **Select**: `in1…inN` picked by the `sel` index (0 = in1) — e.g. a Flip-Flop output chooses between two frequencies.
- **Flip-Flop**: `set`, `reset`, `clk` (toggle) on rising edges; priority and start state. Two clocks switch something on and off.
- **Sample & Hold**: samples `in` on the `trig` edge, or follows it while `trig` is high.
- **Counter**: counts `clk` edges by *step*, modulo N; `wrap` is a pulse every N-th clock (divider), `phase` is count/N.
- **One-Shot**: a trigger starts a `gate` of *width* after *delay*; `end` pulses when it finishes (a delayed trigger). Retriggerable.
- The **Oscilloscope** has a `hit` output: a pulse each time its trigger fires.

Presets: *Control: Clocks Switch a Tone On and Off*, *Control: Level Trigger (Compare, Counter, One-Shot)*, *Control: Logic Test Bench (all blocks)* (every block in one patch: arpeggio from Counter + Select + Math, gate from Flip-Flop + Compare + Logic, Sample & Hold, One-Shot, scope `hit` counter).

### Indicators

Visual blocks on number wires (Indicators). They draw on a dark screen in both themes and work in the dashboard as tiles.

- **Lamps**: 1…16 round lamps with a glow and labels, one input each. *Threshold* mode lights a lamp at or above the level, *brightness* mode follows the value (0…full scale). *Hold / fade* lets a lamp fade out over the given time, so a one-block pulse (Compare `rise`, a trigger, a flip-flop) is still visible; *blink while on*. Colors are a cycled list: `green, amber, red, blue, teal, violet, pink` or `#rrggbb`.
- **Gauge**: analog needle with a 240° scale, tick labels, optional green / amber / red zones (`amber from`, `red from`), peak marker, needle damping and the value with a unit under the hub. *min* / *max* can be wired.
- **LED Bar**: segmented ladder (horizontal or vertical, by the tile's shape) with the same zones, instant attack, smooth fall and a peak marker. Defaults fit a dB level: −60…0, amber from −18, red from −3.
- **Compass**: azimuth dial with N / E / S / W; `az` is the arrow, `az2` a second marker on the rim (a bearing and a heading, a satellite and the antenna). *Rotate the dial* turns the dial to `az2`. The needle takes the shortest way round 359° → 0°.
- **7-Segment Display**: seven-segment digits (1…12, decimals, leading zeros, unlit segments); *frequency* format turns Hz into kHz / MHz / GHz with the unit. Overflow shows dashes.

- **Sky Plot**: polar plot for azimuth / elevation — north up, the horizon on the rim, zenith in the centre, rings at 30° and 60°. `az` / `el` is the main object, `az2` / `el2` a second one (a satellite and the antenna); a fading trail shows the pass (*trail*, s). An object below the *horizon* is drawn hollow. Wire the `az` / `el` outputs of *Satellites* or a rotator controller to it.
- **S-Meter**: receiver scale S1…S9 (6 dB per step), then +10 … +60 dB; *S9 level* is set in dBm (−73 is the HF standard, use −93 for VHF). Fast attack and slow fall like a real meter, peak marker, the S reading and the level in the corner. The `s` output is the reading in S units (9 = S9, 15 = S9 + 36 dB).
- **Switch**: 1…16 clickable switches, each gives 0 / 1 on `out1…`. Styles: *toggle* (slider), *lever* (tumbler), *rocker*, *button* (latching push button with a glowing ring), *push* (a key that is on only while pressed). The state of latching ones is kept in the patch. Labels and colours as in Lamps; works as a dashboard tile (touch-friendly).
- **Attitude**: artificial horizon — sky / ground, pitch ladder, roll scale with a fixed pointer, aircraft symbol and a slip ball. `roll`, `pitch` in degrees and `slip` −1…1; without roll / pitch, `x` / `y` / `z` of an accelerometer (device flat, screen up) give the angles. *Damping*, *pitch from centre to edge*.
- **ADS-B Radar**: a PPI of the aircraft from the ADS-B Decoder's `rec` around the centre (`lat` / `lon` inputs, the fields, or My Position): range rings, headings, altitude colours, trails, labels (callsign, flight level) and a sweep. Range is *auto* or fixed. Click an aircraft to select it: the line under the dial shows callsign, altitude, speed and distance, and the outputs follow it — `rec`, `az` and `range` from the centre, `alt`, `gs`, `track`, `fpa` (flight path angle from the vertical rate) and `bank` (from the turn rate); `count` is all aircraft. Wire `bank` / `fpa` to an Attitude, `az` to a Compass.
- **Pass**: pass of a satellite — elevation arc between `aos` and `los` (ms, the *Satellites* outputs `aos` / `los` / `maxel` give the next pass), a marker at the current `el`, AOS / LOS countdown, `max` elevation. Without a forecast it draws the elevation history of `el`.
- **Knob**: 1…16 rotary knobs (`out1…`). Drag up / down (Shift — fine), mouse wheel for a step, double click resets to *reset value*. *min* / *max*, *step* (0 — smooth), *linear* or *log* scale (log needs min > 0), unit, labels and colours; the values are kept in the patch. Works as a dashboard tile.
- **Fader**: 1…16 sliders with a scale, the same range / step / scale / reset as Knob; a click sets the value, drag moves it, the wheel nudges.
- **XY Pad**: a touch pad with `x`, `y` (each with its own min / max) and `touch` (1 while pressed). *Spring back* returns to the centre on release, *grid lines* and *snap to grid*; the position is kept in the patch.
- **Tank**: level of a number as a filled tube (vertical or horizontal by the tile's shape) with a scale, damping, dashed *low* / *high* alarm lines and min / max markers (a click resets them; for a history use *Trend Chart*). The fill turns amber below *low* and red above *high*; `low` / `high` outputs give 0 / 1 for the alarms.
- **Text Ticker**: the latest lines from `text` (a new string is a new line) and `rec` (the *field* is shown, or all fields as `key=value`), as a scrolling log with optional timestamps or as a running line (*marquee*). *Clear* empties it. Good for a decoder's messages next to the other indicators.

- **Graph** (Output): a link graph (vis-network, bundled). There is no data window, everything comes over wires. **`rows`** takes the whole set at once — wire a [Table](#table)'s `rows` to it: a record with `from,to[,weight[,label[,color]]]` (also `source`/`target`, `src`/`dst`) is an edge. The other records of `rows` are the nodes: `id` (or `node`, `name`), `label`, `shape` (`dot`, `circle`, `square`, `box`, `diamond`, `triangle`, `star`, `hexagon`, `ellipse`, `database`, `text`), `color`, `size`; a node without a record gets the defaults (size by degree), a node record without edges is drawn alone. Fields are found by name in this order: `from` / `source` / `src`, `to` / `target` / `dst`, `weight` / `value` / `count`, `label` / `name` (a record that has only one end is skipped, so aliases and voice frames do not make edges; `src` is not taken for a source when the record has `kind` or `msg` — there it is the protocol name); an `alias` field of a record (or a record with only `from` and `alias`) labels the sender node as `alias` over its id; *advanced → prefix for to* (`call=group:TG `) puts a prefix on the addressee id when a field has a value, so talkgroup 9 and radio 9 are two nodes; *advanced → … col* names the columns explicitly. Protocol logs work the same way: wire a decoder's `rec` (DMR, M17, P25… and FT8 give `from` / `to`) into a Table, filter it (DMR: `kind:call|alias`, FT8: `to:` — CQ calls have no addressee) and wire `rows` to `rows` — every repeated call thickens the edge. A set replaces the previous one: what is gone from the table disappears from the graph, edits are applied, and only the difference is sent to the view (the layout stays, nothing is redrawn without a reason); unwiring a port empties its layer. `rec` (one record = one edge) and `text` (a chunk of CSV on every change) only add edges as they come, for streams. A repeated pair without a weight thickens the edge. *arrows*, *weights on edges*, *physics*; *Clear* wipes what `rec` / `text` collected; clicking a node puts its name on `sel`; `nodes` / `edges` are counters. Preset *Graph: Links from CSV*. (The old *csv* field of saved patches is still read.)
- **Table Chart** (Output): a chart from the rows of a Table (wire `rows` or `all`, like Graph). *x* / *y* columns are found by name (`chan`, `t`, `rssi_max`, `busy`, `csi`…) or set by hand; several *y* columns or a *series by* column give several series. Bars (up to 64 different x, e.g. channels) or lines (time, many rows); *peak hold* keeps the highest value per x; a fixed range or auto. A cell like `3,5,4,…` (or an array) is a vector: it is drawn as a line over the index, the last *N* rows overlaid (older fainter) or as a waterfall (*heat*) — CSI, spectra. Outputs `max`, `count`. Aggregate first with *Rec → Entities* + a keyed Table (one row per channel / device) if needed.
- **Rec → Entities** (Data): turns one decoder record into entities and links by rules, one per line: `[out:] conditions | fields`. Conditions (space-separated, all must match): `field` (has a value), `!field`, `field=a|b`, `field!=a`, `field~regex`. Fields (comma-separated): `name=template` (`{f}` a record field, `{f:x}` number as hex, `{a|b}` the first non-empty one, `{f*}` an array field — one record per element), a bare `name` (copied if present), `*` (all fields); a template with an empty field skips the rule for that record. `out` names an output port (a table per entity type: cell, operator, subscriber, talkgroup, link…); `rec` carries everything. *insert rules* adds ready sets: **GSM cells** (cell → operator, cell → ARFCN) and **Digital voice** (subscribers, talkgroups, membership and private calls; the `{src}` prefix keeps DMR / P25 / NXDN ids apart). Wire `rec` into a **Table** with *upsert key col* = `id`, then its `rows` into **Graph**: nodes with `shape` / `color` / `label`, edges `from` → `to`. Presets *Entities: …* — made-up data and real USB SDR chains (DMR, GSM).
- **Entity Card** (Output): the card of one entity from a Table's `rows` (the same set as for Graph). `sel` takes the selected id — wire **Graph**'s `sel` to it: a click on a node shows the title and type, `count` / `first` / `last`, the other columns as attributes and the links in both directions grouped by label (→ outgoing, ← incoming). A click on a linked entity opens it (← goes back), the search box finds an entity by id or name. Outputs: `sel`, `rec` (the row of the open entity) and `links` (their number). Preset *Entities: …*.
- **Chat** (Output): incoming text from the `text` wire goes into the log, the field at the bottom sends (Enter or *Send*): `text` holds the last sent message, `go` pulses for one block. A text on the `send` input is sent the same way. Preset *Chat: Text In and Out*. **Group chat**: the `rows` pin takes the participants (a Table: `name`, `avatar`, `online`, `typing`) — a strip of avatars with the online dot above the log and «… is typing» under it; `from` names the author of incoming `text`, `rec` takes ready messages (`from`, `text`); with one other participant in `rows` the dialog needs no `from`. *my name* marks your own messages (shown on the right). Preset «Chat: Group with Avatars and Contact List».
- **Two-player games** (Output): *Tic-Tac-Toe*, *Connect Four*, *Battleship*, *Reversi* (legal moves are marked, a player with no move is skipped, the game ends when nobody can move). Ports like Chat: `in` — the opponent's moves, `out` — yours (text), `go` pulses per move; plus `turn` (1 on your move), `result` (1 win / −1 loss) and a `new` input (rising edge = new game). Any text channel works: *WebRTC Data* (`line` → `in`, `out` → `send`), *MQTT* (one shared topic — a node ignores its own messages), *Text over Network*. A hello handshake picks the first player (or set `side`), every next game starts with the other one; the *New game* button restarts both sides. Messages are `<game>:<nonce>:<seq>:<command>`, e.g. `gtictactoe:k3j9x0aa:5:m 4`. Presets *Game: … over WebRTC*, *Game: Tic-Tac-Toe over MQTT*, *Game: Reversi over Sound (TBSK)*. **Lossy channel** (`rel`, off by default): for a link that drops or corrupts messages (sound, radio) every message gets a checksum, moves are numbered, the other side confirms them (`ack`) and an unconfirmed move is sent again after *resend* seconds (random +0…50%), a duplicate is not applied twice, a corrupted or out-of-order one is dropped. Both sides need the same setting. `go` is a rising edge for every message (one block of pause between them), so it can be wired to the *TBSK Modulator*'s `go` (with `out` → `text`); the modulator queues frames that arrive during a transmission instead of cutting the current one. Over TBSK the channel is half-duplex (a move is ~30 bytes: ≈1 s at 300 bit/s), a collision of two bursts is covered by the resend. Checked in `node tools/test-games.mjs`: two Reversi nodes through the real TBSK modulator and decoder, with noise and with every fifth burst cut by a 0.25 s dropout, both sides finish with equal boards; not tried with real speakers and microphones yet.

Code fields (Script, Builder, Note, tables) are a plain text area with a light syntax highlight underneath, so the caret stays in place at any canvas zoom; the Note has no highlight.

Presets: *Indicators: Lamps, Gauge, LED Bar, Compass, Display*, *Indicators: Sky Plot, S-Meter, Text Ticker*, *Indicators: Switches and Attitude*, *ADS-B: Radar and Attitude (Generator)*, *Satellites: Pass and Sky Plot*.

### Measuring instruments

Meters with numeric outputs: a value can go to a *Number*, *Gauge*, *Compare* or a log. Presets: *Measure: Modulation Meter — FM and AM (Generator)*, *Measure: SINAD of an FM Receiver (Generator)*, *Measure: IQ Quality — DC and Imbalance (Generator)*.

- **Modulation Meter** (IQ, worker): a carrier near the center of the stream (shift it to zero and decimate first). The envelope gives **AM** — depth from the positive and negative peaks and their mean (a difference is overmodulation or distortion); the instantaneous frequency gives **FM** — peak deviation (half of the peak-to-peak), the modulating frequency (from zero crossings of the deviation) and the index β = deviation / modulating frequency. Also the carrier offset from the center and the level. Both signals pass two one-pole low-pass filters (*measurement band*), a window of 100…1000 ms; samples with the envelope below 10 % of the mean do not take part in FM. Outputs `am` (%), `dev`, `offset`, `fm`, `idx`, `level`. Needs complex IQ; with no carrier the outputs are empty. The modulating frequency is counted per window, so its resolution is 1 / window
- **SINAD / THD+N** (Analysis, signal): the standard receiver test. The tone is taken out by a least-squares sine fit (cos, sin and a constant) over the whole window, the frequency is found by a golden-section search around the FFT peak, so even 80 dB gets no leakage into the residual; what is left in the band is noise and distortion. **SINAD** = (S+N+D)/(N+D), **THD+N** = √((N+D)/S), also the tone frequency, its level, the residual level and ENOB. *Band*: 300–3400 Hz (voice, 4th-order filters), 20–20000 Hz or full; *tone* 0 = the strongest in the band, or a number / the `freq` input. Window 0.25…2 s, a result every half window. The FM receiver test is *IQ Demodulator → IQ → Audio → SINAD*. No psophometric weighting
- **IQ Quality** (IQ, worker): over a window of 250…2000 ms — the **DC offset** (dBFS and relative to the signal, dBc: LO leakage), the **Q gain** and **phase** error (the same model as *IQ Balance*: Q′ = g·(Q·cosφ + I·sinφ)) and the **image rejection**, from the circularity of the stream: ρ = |E[x²]| / E[|x|²] after removing the mean, rejection = 20·lg((1 + √(1 − ρ²)) / ρ). Works on a tone away from zero, noise or a busy band; a carrier exactly at the center (real envelope) reads too low, as does anything that is not symmetric by nature. Real streams are refused. Outputs `dc`, `dcc`, `gain`, `phase`, `irr`
- **Signal Meter** (IQ, worker): one signal in the stream — the strongest cluster of bins above the noise. Welch spectrum (Hann, 50 %, up to 64 frames); the noise floor is the 25th percentile of the central 90 % of bins (corrected for the averaged spectrum), the cluster is bins above *threshold* with gaps up to 1 % of the span merged. Outputs: `snr` (signal power minus noise, over the occupied bandwidth), `obw` (the band holding *share of power* — 99 % by default, equal tails), `bw` (width at −X dB from the peak, outermost crossings, so holes in an FM spectrum do not cut it short), `offset` / `freq` (from the center / absolute), `level` (signal, dBFS), `noise` (dBFS/Hz). A narrow signal (OBW ≤ 6 bins) is treated as a carrier: peak with a parabola on the log spectrum, then a fine estimate from the phase slope (shift to zero, boxcar, Σ x[k]·x*[k−1] over the window) — sub-hertz on a clean tone; a wide one gets the power centroid. Not a substitute for a calibrated analyzer: the noise floor assumes the signal fills less than ~75 % of the band, and the frequency is only as good as the SDR's clock (calibrate the PPM first)
- **Signal Database** (`sigDb`, worker): a lookup in the spirit of the Signal Identification Wiki, offline. Give any of *occupied bandwidth*, *symbol rate*, *FM deviation* and *frequency* (wire them from *Signal Meter* `obw` / `freq`, *Baud Estimator* `baud`, *Modulation Meter* `dev`, or type them) and it ranks about 80 known signals — analog voice and broadcast, HF digital modes, paging, aero / marine, digital voice and trunking, ISM / IoT, satellites, wideband (GSM, LTE, DVB, Wi-Fi, GPS…). Every given value scores 1 inside the signal's typical range and falls off with a Gaussian in octaves outside it (half an octave → 0.37); a feature the entry does not specify counts 0.85, a frequency outside the entry's bands ×0.2, so a specific match outranks a vague one. Outputs `name`, `mod`, `score` (0…1) and `text` (the ranked list). Several signals can tie at 100 % — DMR, P25, YSF and NXDN differ by little in these numbers, which is the honest answer. A hint, not a decoder; the table is `SDB` in `modules/sigdb-kernels.js` and easy to extend
- **PSK/QAM Receiver (EVM)** (`pskRx`, worker): an IQ stream of a BPSK / QPSK / 8PSK / 16QAM carrier (shifted near zero, ≥ 2 samples per symbol) → root-raised-cosine matched filter → symbol AGC → Gardner timing recovery with cubic interpolation → carrier recovery: a coarse offset from the M-th power of the symbols (for 16QAM only the corner points, whose 4th power is constant) while unlocked, then a second-order decision-directed loop. Against a constellation of unit average power it reports `evm` (%, RMS error over the last *EVM window* symbols divided by the RMS of the ideal points), `mer` (dB, −20·lg EVM), `foff` (carrier offset, Hz, as found by the loop — pull-in about ±2 % of the symbol rate, so shift and decimate first) and `lock`; the `out` wire carries the derotated symbols at the symbol rate for *Constellation*. Checked against a synthesized signal: QPSK at 10 / 20 dB SNR per sample (16 / 26 dB per symbol) gives EVM 15.9 / 5.1 % (theory 15.8 / 5.0), an offset of 1 % of the symbol rate is found to ±1 Hz. Not covered: differential and offset modulations (π/4-DQPSK, OQPSK), phase ambiguity is not resolved (EVM does not depend on it), 16QAM floor is about 1.8 % (interpolation and timing jitter)
- **Channel Activity** (`chanLog`, Analysis): a log of channels built from the labels of the *Signal Type Identifier* (`bands` in, same kind of `bands` out). Each analysis frame the labels are folded into persistent channels — a label joins a channel whose centre is within *tolerance* (or 0.6 × width) and whose width is within a factor of three, otherwise a new channel starts; centre and width are running means, the type is the most frequent label. **Occupancy** is the time a channel was seen divided by the time watched (the frame interval is credited to a channel seen in that frame; its first sighting adds nothing). Channels closer than *merge* (default 2.5 kHz) are shown as one — the sidebands of a tone-modulated FM and other fragments of one signal; 0 turns it off. The readout groups channels by width (narrow < 3 kHz, voice / data 3–16 kHz, wide 16–250 kHz, broadband > 250 kHz); *Save CSV* writes frequency, width, type, occupancy, hits, first and last seen, peak level; *Reset* starts a new watch. Outputs `bands` (occupancy in the label, for the spectrum analyzer), `text`, `count`, and `f` / `occ` of the busiest channel. The detector is `sigid`: where it splits one signal, the registry can only merge by distance
- **IQ Channel Model** (`iqChannel`, IQ, worker): a radio path between a transmitter and a receiver, for testing decoders and receivers without air. In order: sample clock error (*ppm*, cubic Lagrange interpolation, the stream gets slightly shorter or longer) → **multipath** (delay line; profiles *2-ray*, 3GPP *EPA / EVA / ETU* or a *custom* list `delay µs:gain dB, …`; tap power is normalised to 1; delays shorter than a sample period are linearly interpolated, so on a narrow stream they merge into flat fading) with **Rayleigh** or **Rician** (K-factor, first tap) fading — a sum of 24 sinusoids per tap with a given max Doppler spread *fd*, coefficients every sr/(16·fd) samples with linear interpolation, E|h|² = 1 → path gain → **Doppler** shift and drift in Hz/s (a satellite pass is about −50…−200) → **phase noise** (Wiener, oscillator linewidth in Hz) → I/Q imbalance and DC → impulse noise (bursts per second, length, level against the signal) and **AWGN**: *SNR* is taken against the signal power at the channel output (running mean ≈ 0.5 s), or an absolute level in dBFS. Outputs: `out`, `snr` (the SNR that is set, dB; empty with noise off) and `level` (signal level at the output, dBFS). The *seed* makes noise and fading repeatable. Preset: *Measure: Channel Model*. Tests: `node tools/test-kernels.mjs` (noise power, Doppler and drift, two-ray echo, Rayleigh / Rician statistics, ppm, phase-noise variance, impulse rate).
- **Spectrum Baseline** (`specBase`, Analysis; core `modules/baseline-kernels.js`): remembers what the band normally looks like and reports the difference — a watchman for hours of unattended work. The air is cut into cells (*cell width*, `auto` = the bin width on the 1-2-5 grid; a sweep or a retuned source just adds cells, the norm is kept). Each cell keeps its level and spread in dB (running mean and variance over visits) and how often a signal was there (*occ*). The first *learn* visits only teach. Then a cell is **above the norm** if it is higher by *thr* dB and by more than *z* σ (σ not less than *sdMin*); neighbouring cells join into an event, and an event wider than *wide* kHz is **wideband** (a jammer, a raised floor). A carrier that was present in ≥ *occMin* of the visits and is now at the noise level and *drop* dB below the norm is **missing**. An event is reported after *confirm* frames in a row and ends *hold* ms after it is gone. A deviation is absorbed into the norm slowly (1/20 of the normal rate), so a station that stays on becomes normal after about 2 × *adapt* visits. Outputs: `bands` (for the Spectrum Analyzer: orange — new, red — wideband, grey — missing), `rec` (`src:'BASELINE'`, `kind` new / wideband / missing, `status` start / end, `freq`, `bw`, `db`, `excess`, `floor`, `dur` on end — wire it to a Table for a log, or to MQTT / a webhook for alarms), `base` (the norm as a spectrum, to draw under the live one), `count`, `f` and `excess` of the strongest, `learned`. *learn only* updates the norm without reports. **Save norm / Load norm** keep it in a file (about 7 bytes per cell), so a night of learning is not lost. Preset: *Measure: Spectrum Baseline*. Tests: `node tools/test-baseline.mjs` (no false alarms on 300 stable frames, new signal on the 3rd frame, end with a duration, missing carrier, wideband rise, absorption of a permanent station, save / load, a two-part sweep).
- **Cross-Correlator (TDOA / interferometer)** (`iqXcorr`, Analysis, worker) and **IQ Delay** (`iqDelay`, IQ): the software half of a multi-receiver setup (two dongles on one antenna through a splitter, a common noise source, or two receivers of one signal). Two `iq` streams of the same rate are cut into blocks (by arrival order — the offset between the dongles is searched, ± *search range*) and cross-correlated. The integer lag is the peak of the averaged correlation power; the fractional part comes from the slope of the cross-spectrum phase, refined coarse-to-fine (phase differences over 1, 8, 64, 512 bins, each after removing the delay already found), which is insensitive to the random phase of each block caused by the two local oscillators and is close to the theoretical accuracy: 0.0006 samples at high SNR, 0.03 at 0 dB per channel on a wideband source. Outputs: `delay` (samples, b − a: positive when b lags), `dist` (path difference, m), `bearing` (° — asin of the path difference over the *antenna baseline*, with a baseline set), `phase` (what is left after the delay is removed — the carrier phase difference of a narrowband signal, for interferometry; meaningful only with a shared reference clock), `coh` (normalised correlation at the peak, ≈ SNR/(1+SNR) for equal noise) and `drift` (the sample-clock difference between the two receivers, ppm, from the trend of the delay). **Zero** copies the current delay and phase into *delay zero* / *phase zero*: feed both receivers from a common noise source first, so cable lengths and receiver latency drop out. *IQ Delay* delays a stream by a fractional number of samples with a phase shift (windowed sinc, reliable up to ~0.4 of the sample rate) for calibration and tests; the filter is causal, so it has a fixed latency of 16 samples on top of the delay — pass the other stream through an *IQ Delay* with 0 to compare. What this does not do: the sampling of two independent dongles is not synchronised in hardware, so a bearing from the phase needs a shared clock (or a re-calibration every few minutes), and the node does not drive the receivers — it works on whatever two `iq` wires it is given

### Logic Analyzer

**Logic Analyzer** (Analysis): 1…8 channels → digital traces on a common time axis, with a decoder that gives out the **bits and bytes**.

- **Input wires**: *signal* (audio rate — the usual choice, e.g. the `bit` output of *Transmit Chars*, a demodulator's soft output, a square wave) or *number* (one sample per engine block, ~Eng.sr / block, for slow things: lamps, triggers, flip-flops, Compare outputs). A *threshold* with *hysteresis* turns each wire into 0 / 1. The `d1…dN` outputs are the digitised channels. At 44.1 kHz async serial works up to ~9600 baud (at least 4 samples per bit) and I²C / SPI clocks up to ~10 kHz; the readout warns when the baud is too high for the sample rate.
- **History**: drag the traces sideways to scroll back through the last 20 s (the view holds), the wheel changes the window, a double click returns to the live trace; *Hold / Run* still freezes the picture.
- **Display**: the *window* (1 ms … 10 s) is drawn from the list of edges, not samples, so even long windows are cheap. *Trigger* on a rising / falling edge of a chosen channel (the trigger sits at 25 % of the window; the time axis is relative to it), *single shot* with **Arm**, **Hold / Run** freezes the picture. Decoded bytes are drawn as bubbles on the trace (hex and the character, errors in red).
- **Decoders** (*decoder*; channels are set in the advanced parameters):
  - **UART**: baud, 5…16 data bits, parity (none / even / odd), idle level, bit order (LSB first by default). Start bit is checked in the middle, every bit is sampled at its centre; framing and parity errors are marked and not sent to `text`.
  - **SPI**: clock, MOSI, MISO (optional), CS (optional, active low), word size, order (MSB first by default), sampling edge (rising = modes 0 / 3, falling = modes 1 / 2).
  - **I²C**: SCL + SDA; START / repeated START / STOP, 7-bit address with R / W, data bytes, ACK / NAK (a NAK is marked with `!`).
- **Outputs**: `byte` (last byte), `new` (pulse in the block where a byte arrived — wire it to a lamp or a counter), `text` (a line: UART — on a line feed, after 80 characters or after 1 s of silence; SPI — `MOSI … | MISO …` after CS goes high; I²C — `S 3C+W 00 AF P` after STOP), `rec` (a record per byte: `proto`, `byte`, `hex`, `ch`, `time` — seconds from the start, `err`; I²C adds `kind` and `addr`, SPI `line`), `hit` (a pulse on every trigger). `text` goes to a *Text Ticker*, `rec` to a *Rec Log*.

- **USB logic analyzers** (WebUSB; `input` → *USB* in the advanced parameters): cheap 8-channel analyzers on the Cypress FX2 (Saleae clones, boards with sigrok **fx2lafw** firmware). Channels 1…8 are D0…D7, 8-bit samples at 20 kHz … 24 MS/s straight into the same traces, trigger and UART / SPI / I²C decoders (the `ch` wires disappear). Buttons: *USB: connect* (opens the chooser), *USB: load firmware (.fw)*, *USB: start*, *USB: stop*. A board without firmware (it does not answer the version request) needs a fx2lafw image: take it from the `sigrok-firmware-fx2lafw` package (`/usr/share/sigrok-firmware/`) or from PulseView (`fx2lafw-saleae-logic.fw` for Saleae clones, `fx2lafw-cypress-fx2.fw` for a bare CY7C68013A board) — it is GPL-2.0+, so it is not bundled; it is loaded into the board's RAM and lives until the power is cut. The board then restarts; connect again. The page keeps up with a few MS/s: the readout shows the rate and the captured samples and warns when samples are dropped. Chrome / Edge / Opera only; on Linux the device needs a udev rule (or `chmod`) for the browser to open it.

Presets: *Logic Analyzer: UART Decode*, *Logic Analyzer: USB (fx2lafw)*.

## Infrared

The IR layer is split in three, so the same patch works with any hardware:

1. **A frame is a list of pulse lengths** (µs: mark, space, mark, …) and a carrier. On a wire it is a text `F:38000 9000 4500 560 …`, an event: the text is present only in the block where the frame arrived (the same frame twice in a row is two events). Any of these formats is read automatically and can be written back: plain µs numbers (LIRC), Flipper (`ir tx RAW F:… DC:… …` and `.ir` raw data), Tasmota (`IRsend …`, the MQTT body `38000,9000,…`, `IrReceived.RawData` JSON), Pronto hex.
2. **Codecs** (hardware-independent):
   - **IR Encode** (`go` pulse, the *Send* button, or a text on `text`: `NEC 0x04 0x08`, `Sony12 1 21`, `RC5 5 12 t`, `Samsung 7 2`, `NEC rep`) → `raw`. Protocol, address and command are also wire inputs. RC5 / RC6 flip the toggle bit on every send.
   - **IR Decode**: `raw` → `text` (`NEC addr 0x04 cmd 0x08 [04 FB 08 F7]`), `rec` (a record per frame), `addr`, `cmd`, `new`, and `info` with the analysis of an unknown frame: clusters of mark and space lengths and, for pulse-distance protocols, the header, both pause lengths and the bytes (LSB first). A NEC repeat frame repeats the last code.
   - **IR Learn**: stores the first frame (or the next one after *Capture next*) in a text field saved with the patch — a code in any format can be pasted there; *Replay* or a `go` pulse sends it, with a repeat count and a gap.
   - **IR Merge**: up to four frame sources on one adapter input.
3. **Adapters** (hardware):
   - **IR Sound TX**: the carrier as a sine straight to the sound card (**96 kHz or more**: the sample rate must be at least 2.5 × the carrier) → a transistor and an IR LED; *envelope* gives only the envelope for an external 36–40 kHz modulator (works at any rate).
   - **IR Sound RX**: *baseband* — the output of an IR receiver module (TSOP / VS1838) into the line / microphone input; *carrier* — a photodiode with an amplifier (96 kHz or more): the signal is mixed with the carrier and filtered. Adaptive threshold, glitch filter, a frame ends after a pause (*gap*, 20 ms by default; raise it for air conditioners, whose frames have long pauses inside).
   - **IR Serial (WebSerial)**: `sketch` — [`tools/ir/ir-serial.ino`](tools/ir/ir-serial.ino), a bridge for Arduino / ESP32 / ESP8266 / Pico with the IRremote library (`TX <Hz> <µs…>` / `RX <µs…>`); `flipper` — the Flipper Zero console (`ir tx RAW …`, `ir rx raw`); `wire` — your own firmware, plain `F:38000 …` lines both ways.

Presets: *IR: Remote Codes Loopback (No Hardware)*, *IR: Learn and Replay (Sound Card)*, *IR: Arduino / ESP / Flipper (WebSerial)*.

IR as a data link (not a remote): the same adapters carry any frame, and *Transmit Chars* / *Receive Chars* and the *Logic Analyzer* work on the demodulated line.

## Bluetooth LE

Web Bluetooth works in Chrome, Edge and Opera (desktop and Android) on https or localhost; Firefox and iOS do not support it. The device chooser opens only on a click (*Connect* / *Choose device*). Without the filters the chooser lists every device (*list all devices*); by default it is filtered by the service, or by the name prefix.

- **BLE UART**: `line` / `go` (a line per block, split on line feed) and the `text` input, which writes in chunks (20 bytes by default — safe on any device; with a larger MTU raise it). Profiles: *Nordic UART* (service `6e400001-…`), *HM-10 / FFE0*, *custom* (service, characteristic from the device, characteristic to the device). The line end for sending is selectable. The same line protocols as on a serial port work: wire the `line` output to *IR Decode*, or *Parse CSV Line*.
- **BLE GATT**: service and characteristic as a name (`heart_rate`), a 16-bit hex (`180D`) or a 128-bit UUID; *notify* or *read every period*; the format turns the bytes into a number (`value` with scale and offset), `hex` is always given, `rec` has a record per value. The `write` input sends hex or text to the characteristic (relays, LED strips, thermometers' settings).
- **BLE Advertisements**: RSSI (smoothed), TX power, manufacturer data and `present` (0 after the silence set in the parameters) of one chosen device — `watchAdvertisements`, Chrome 85+ (some versions need `chrome://flags/#enable-experimental-web-platform-features`).
- After a disconnect from the device side the node reconnects every 3 s without the chooser (*reconnect*).

Presets: *BLE: Heart Rate Monitor*, *BLE: Find a Beacon by RSSI*, *BLE: UART Terminal*.

### Meshtastic

**Meshtastic (BLE / Serial)** (Sources) talks to a Meshtastic node through its client API — Bluetooth LE (service `6ba1b218-…`) or WebSerial (115200, frames `0x94 0xC3`). The node does the radio and the decryption with its channel keys; the page gets ready messages, positions and telemetry. On connect the node sends its config and the node list.

- **Outputs:** `text` / `from` / `go` / `snr` — incoming chat messages, one per block; `rec` — every packet as a record `{t, kind, id, name, to, ch, snr, rssi, hop, text, lat, lon, alt, battery, voltage, temp, hum, press…}` (kinds: `text`, `position`, `telemetry`, `nodeinfo`, `node` from the config dump, `encrypted` for a channel the node has no key for). Positions of known nodes are added to their other records, so the records go to the *Map* and the *Table* as they are.
- **Input:** `text` is sent to *send to* (`^all` or a node id like `!a1b2c3d4`) on the chosen channel index.
- A node accepts one BLE client at a time: disconnect the phone app first. Not covered: node settings (admin messages).
- The parser is checked against the protobuf layout only (`node tools/test-meshtastic.mjs`), not on real hardware yet.

Preset: *Meshtastic: Chat and Nodes on the Map*.

**Meshtastic Sniffer (LoRa)** (Decoders) is a passive receiver, no node needed: wire *LoRa Decoder*'s `rec` (sync word `2B`) into it. It tries to AES-CTR-decrypt the payload with the channel PSK (base64, comma-separated for several; default `AQ==` — the default public key of the pre-set `LongFast`/`Default` channel, which carries most of the network's open traffic) and, once decrypted, parses it the same way as the live node: text, position, telemetry, nodeinfo, waypoint, traceroute. Frames none of the given PSKs could open (someone else's private channel) are hidden by default (*hide undecrypted*). A channel with its own PSK reads just as well — pass it in.

### MeshCore

**MeshCore Sniffer (LoRa)** (Decoders) is the same idea for [MeshCore](https://github.com/meshcore-dev/meshcore): no node, just *LoRa Decoder*'s `rec` (sync word `12` — the common private/default LoRa sync, shared with other non-Meshtastic LoRa gear too, so content is the only way to tell MeshCore apart). Unlike Meshtastic, most of the packet is plain binary, not protobuf:

- **Advert** (a node announcing itself) is entirely plaintext — public key, name, type (chat / repeater / room / sensor) and an optional position — no key needed. The Ed25519 signature over it is not checked (it only proves authorship, not secrecy; a forged advert is possible but that is a MeshCore protocol property, not a decoder gap).
- **Group text / group data** are AES-128-ECB with a 2-byte HMAC-SHA256 MAC, keyed by the channel PSK (base64; default `izOH6cXN6mrJ5e26oRXNcg==` — the pre-set `Public` channel most clients ship with). Several PSKs, comma-separated, are tried in turn by their 1-byte channel hash.
- Direct messages and anything else keyed by a *recipient's* private key (text, request/response, path, ack, …) cannot be read passively — that needs the node that message was sent to, same as a private Meshtastic channel.

**Outputs:** `text` / `from` / `lat` / `lon` / `new`, and `rec` — `{t, kind, route, hop, from, name, text, lat, lon, channelHash, psk, encrypted…}`. The parser is checked against the wire format only (`node tools/test-meshcore.mjs`), not on real hardware yet. See also the `meshcore` structure in *HEX Structure* for a field-by-field dump of the header and path without decrypting anything.

## MQTT

**MQTT In** and **MQTT Out** (MQTT 3.1.1 over WebSocket, written from scratch, no libraries) connect the workbench to any IoT stack. Each node keeps its own connection; `Connect` / `Disconnect` buttons, auto-reconnect every 3 s, a refused login (wrong user or password) is shown and not retried.

- **MQTT In**: `topic` takes several filters separated by spaces or commas. Outputs: `text` and `topic` (one message per block, a burst is queued), `value` (the payload as a number, or the JSON field named in *JSON field* — `a.b.c`; the last value is kept), `rec` (every message of the block: a JSON object or array → records with `topic` and `t` added; plain text or a number → `{topic, text}` / `{topic, value}`), `new` (a pulse per message).
- **MQTT Out**: `text` is published when it changes (empty text is skipped), `value` when it changes, through the template; the `topic` wire overrides the topic parameter; `ok` is 1 while connected. QoS 1 messages are acknowledged but not re-sent.
- Passwords are stored in the patch as plain text — do not share patches with real credentials.

Presets: *MQTT: Subscribe and Publish*, *IR: Tasmota Blaster over MQTT* (send with *IR Encode*, receive Tasmota `IrReceived.RawData` — *IR Decode* reads that JSON and the `38000,9000,…` body of `IRsend` directly).

## Bytes and files

The `bin` port (brown pin) carries plain bytes: `{d: Uint8Array, name, mime, id}`. A new `id` means new data; nodes compare it to skip repeats. Text ports cannot carry arbitrary bytes, `bin` can.

- **File → Bytes** (Sources): choose a file, it goes out on `bin` when chosen (*send when a file is chosen*), on `go`, on **Send**, or every *repeat* seconds. Outputs `size`, `name`, `new`.
- **Bytes → File** is now the `bin` and `path` inputs of **Files**: the received data is kept in the file store (Files › Received, or the path), optionally also downloaded; the name comes from the data or the path.
- **Bytes → Image** (Decoders): `bin` → `img` (+ `new`, `w`, `h`, `fps`). The format is found by its signature. *mode*: `file` — every `bin` is one picture; `stream` — JPEG frames are cut out of a byte stream by the JPEG structure (an EXIF thumbnail does not break a frame, garbage between frames and a torn frame are skipped and counted), for MJPEG, ESP32-CAM and cameras that write JPEG to a UART; `raw` — pixels without a header: `gray8`, `rgb565` (little-endian) or `rgb888`, width and height set by hand, frames follow each other with no sync (a lost byte shifts the picture — **Reset**). The decoder is asynchronous: while it is busy only the newest frame is kept, the rest is counted as skipped. An animated GIF / WebP gives its first frame (no animation yet), video codecs are not covered. Wire: **Serial Port** → `bin` → **Bytes → Image** → **Frame**; preset *Bytes: Serial Stream → Picture (JPEG / MJPEG / raw)*.
- **Bytes → Text** / **Text → Bytes** (Data): hex, base64, base64url, UTF-8 or Latin-1. Use them to push bytes through anything that moves lines of text.
- **Text: Wrap (prefix/suffix bytes)** (Data): adds arbitrary bytes before and after a text or bytes input. The prefix and suffix are written as a JS string: `\x02`, `\xFF`, `Ж`, `\u{1F600}`, `\r`, `\n`, `\t`, `\0`, `\\`; other characters are UTF-8. Outputs both bytes and text, so no Module Builder is needed.
- **Bytes → Frames** (Protocols) and **Frames → Bytes** (Decoders): for channels that lose, double, reorder or cut data. The file is split into frames `D5 7A | type | xid | seq | total | len | body | CRC-32` (little-endian; the CRC covers type … body). The first frame (META) carries the size, the CRC-32 of the whole file, the name and the MIME type; the rest are DATA frames of *payload per frame* bytes. A frame is one `bin` and one text line (base64 or hex; the receiver tells them apart by the first characters). The receiver accepts frames in any order and any number of times, drops damaged ones, assembles several transfers at once (by `xid`) and checks the size and the file CRC. It reads a stream with arbitrary chunk boundaries and garbage between frames (it looks for the next `D5 7A`), so a raw UART or BLE link works without line breaks. *Carousel* repeats the whole transfer, so a listener that joined late or lost frames gets the rest on the next round — a one-way link needs nothing else. There is no acknowledgement or retransmission request; one frame goes out per engine tick at most (up to the *frames per second* set, which is limited by the tick rate: about 90 at 48 kHz / 512).
- **Transports**: **Serial Port** (`send` in, `bin` out: raw bytes of the port), **Serial Out** (`bin` in), **Text over Network** (WebSocket: `sendBin` in, `bin` out with *binary messages go to the bin output* on), **MQTT In / Out** (the payload as bytes, the topic goes to `name`), **BLE UART** (`bin` in and out; set *bytes per write* to the MTU for speed). For anything else — a sound modem such as TBSK, a radio, a pipe — take the text output of **Bytes → Frames** or the bytes of **Bytes → Text**.
- Preset **Bytes: File over Any Text Channel (Loopback)**. Logic: `modules/bytes-kernels.js`, `node tools/test-bytes.mjs` (CRC check value, image signatures, MJPEG cutting in odd chunks, torn frames, raw pixel formats, loss, reorder, duplicates, damaged frames, garbage, two transfers at once, empty file). Checked in headless Chromium through File → Bytes → Frames → Frames → Bytes → File, over the `bin` wire and over the text wire; WebSerial, WebSocket, MQTT and BLE were not tried on real devices.

## Firmware flashers

Flasher nodes (Output) write a firmware file to a radio in bootloader mode over a serial port (WebSerial, Chrome/Edge). Common to all of them: the firmware comes from the node's own file field or from a `bin` wire (*File → Bytes*); **Port…** picks the port (the browser asks once and the node remembers it), **Flash** opens it and runs the protocol, **Abort** stops at the next step; a rising edge on `go` also starts flashing. Outputs: `progress` (0…1), `busy`, `ok` (pulse), `status`. Hardware not tried: the protocols are ported from [fagci/k1-fw](https://github.com/fagci/k1-fw) and [fagci/rt880-fw](https://github.com/fagci/rt880-fw) and [JuantAldea/RT-890-python-flasher](https://github.com/JuantAldea/RT-890-python-flasher) and [fagci/rt4d-goflasher](https://github.com/fagci/rt4d-goflasher) and checked by `node tools/test-uvk5.mjs`, `node tools/test-rt880.mjs` and `node tools/test-rtboot.mjs` against simulated radios (frames, checksums, retries, lost acknowledgements, NAKs, errors).

- **Flasher: Quansheng UV-K5** (38400 baud): power the radio on with PTT held, press **Flash**. The node waits for five bootloader beacons, answers with the bootloader version and sends 256-byte pages (up to 120 KiB, a `.bin`), each acknowledged by the radio; a lost or refused page is resent up to 3 times. Frames: `AB CD | len | data | CRC-16/XMODEM | DC BA`, data and CRC XORed with a 16-byte table. Logic: `modules/uvk5-kernels.js`, the shared node and port code: `modules/flasher.js`
- **Flasher: Radtel RT-880** (115200 baud): put the radio into the bootloader, press **Flash**. The firmware is a `.bin` or an Intel HEX file (*format → auto* takes HEX if the file starts with «:»; addresses are counted from `0x08002800`, the window is 246 KiB, empty space is `0xFF`, records outside it are skipped, checksums are verified). The node sends `connect` ×3 and `update`, then 246 blocks of 1 KiB (`W | addr | data | checksum`), each acknowledged (ACK 6, NAK 255 — the block is resent up to 3 times), then `end`. If the radio reboots without acknowledging the last block, `end` is still sent and the status says so. *iRadio variant* switches the commands and the checksum offset. Logic: `modules/rt880-kernels.js`
- **Flasher: Radtel RT-890** (115200 baud): power the radio on with both side keys held, press **Flash**. The node probes the bootloader (`52 00 00`, answer `FF`), erases (`39 00 00 55`) and writes 128-byte blocks (`57 | addr | data | sum`, up to 0xEC00 bytes, the tail is padded with zeros), each acknowledged by `06`; a refused or lost block is resent up to 3 times. A firmware that does not fill the memory leaves the radio waiting — the status says so. Logic: `modules/rt890-kernels.js` (shared with the RT-4D profile)
- **Flasher: Radtel RT-4D** (115200 baud): same protocol as the RT-890 with other numbers — checksum offset 72, two erase commands (`39 33 05 10`, `39 33 05 55`), 1 KiB blocks over the whole 246 KiB memory (the image is always padded with zeros to the full size; a larger file is refused). Press **Flash** with the radio in the bootloader

## Packet Link

**Packet Link** (Sources) collects packets from a channel and cuts the stream into records: each one has `hex` and `hexSchema`, so it goes straight into **Table**, **HEX Structure** or **PCAP Writer**. The existing byte nodes (Serial Port, BLE UART, MQTT, WebRTC Data, File → Bytes) wire into its `bin` / `text` inputs, so any channel works.

- **Channels** (*channel*): `websocket` (reconnects), `http stream` (SSE, NDJSON, chunked, pcap; *through the CORS proxy from Settings* for hosts without CORS headers), `serial` (WebSerial), `ble uart` (Web Bluetooth: Nordic UART, HM-10; a notification is a packet), `inputs only`. A browser cannot open TCP or UDP: use a bridge below, or an ESP32 / any device with a WebSocket server. The `send` (bin) and `sendText` inputs write to the channel.
- **Frame** (how the stream becomes packets): `message` (one message or notification is a packet), `hex lines` (the longest run of hex bytes in a line; the text before it goes to `prefix`; `data: …` of SSE is understood), `json lines` (fields of the object go to the record, a `hex` / `data` / `payload` / `raw` / `frame` field is the packet), `text lines`, `slip`, `kiss` (AX.25 TNC; the port is in `port`), `hdlc` (flag 7E, stuffing 7D), `cobs`, `len8` / `len16be` / `len16le` (a length in front), `pcap` (a pcap stream; the time and the link type come from the stream, so `hexSchema` follows it — pcapng is not read from a stream).
- **Outputs**: `rec` (an array per block: `t`, `src`, `kind`, `n`, `len`, `hex`, `hexSchema`, `time`, `text`), `bin` (the last packet), `count`, `bytes`. *HEX schema* sets `hexSchema` (`auto`, `eth`, `wifi`, …); *source label* sets `src`.
- **Bridges** (run on the machine that sees the traffic):

```
tcpdump -i wlan0 -U -w - | websocat -b -s 8767     # channel websocket, ws://host:8767, frame: pcap
dumpcap -i eth0 -P -w - | websocat -b -s 8767      # the same, pcap (not pcapng) from Wireshark's dumpcap
websocat -b -s 8767 udp-l:0.0.0.0:5000             # a datagram is a packet, frame: message
websockify 8767 192.168.1.20:3333                  # a TCP server of a device; frame by its protocol
```

  From an https page the browser blocks `ws://` to other hosts: use `wss://`, localhost, or a local copy of the page over http.
- Logic: `modules/link-kernels.js`, `node tools/test-link.mjs` (every frame, chunks split anywhere, broken input, pcap with a link type, the record). Checked in headless Chromium: SSE → Table and WebSocket + SLIP.

## HEX Structure

A packet as bytes and as fields. The node (Data) shows a hex dump with the bytes of every field in its own colour, a tree of the fields with offsets and values, and a line with the whole packet as hex.

- **Inputs**: `bin` and `text` (hex, any spacing) — the bytes to show and parse; `rec` — a record is built into a packet (fields not in the record keep their values; lengths and checksums are recalculated); a record with a `hex` field (DMR, ADS-B, Wi-Fi sniffer) is parsed instead (*rec input*: auto / build / parse; the structure is taken from the record's `hexSchema`); `go` sends the current packet again.
- **Outputs**: `bin` / `text` (the packet), `rec` (every field by its name: `payload.payload.dport`, `body.ies[0].data.ssid`; an enum also gives `<field>_name`; `_len`, `_ok`, `_schema`; a field with `"as":"name"` is also given a short name that does not depend on the position — Wi-Fi: `ssid`, `ds_channel`, `beacon_interval`, `reason`, `auth_status`, so a Beacon, a Probe request and a Probe response all give `ssid` for a graph / Table; an enum can depend on another field: `enumBy` + `enums`, Wi-Fi `subtype_name` follows `type`), `ok` (lengths and checksums agree), `len`, `new`. Lists can name their items: `"key":"id", "val":"data", "as":"ie"` gives `ie.ssid`, `ie.channel`, `ie.rsn.pairwise`, `ie.p2p`… instead of `body.ies[3]…` (repeats become `ie.vendor`, `ie.vendor_2`); a structure's `"derive"` adds fields computed by condition — Wi-Fi: `kind`, `bssid`, `src`, `dst`, `net`; 802.15.4: `kind`, `src`, `dst`, `net` (PAN).
- **Edit**: click a field to outline its bytes; click its value to type a new one (a number, `0x…`, an enum name, `192.168.0.1`, a MAC or hex for byte fields) — the packet is rebuilt and the length / checksum fields are recalculated; click a byte and type hex digits to overwrite it, **Delete** removes it, **Insert** adds `00`; the line under the tree takes a whole packet. *fix* recalculates lengths and checksums after a raw edit.
- **Structures** (list *structure*): `eth`, `arp`, `ipv4`, `ipv6`, `udp`, `tcp`, `icmp`, `icmpv6`, `radiotap`, `wifi` (802.11, every management / control / data frame: beacon, probe, (re)association, auth, deauth, action — Wi-Fi Direct (P2P) and ESP-NOW —, block ack, LLC/SNAP → IPv4 / ARP / IPv6 / EAPOL, the protected-data header; information elements by name, WPS and P2P attributes), `ieee802154` (MAC, beacon, commands, Zigbee NWK / APS / ZDP / ZCL, 6LoWPAN dispatch of Thread), `adsb` (112-bit Mode S: identification, position), `adsb-short`, `ais` (types 1–5, 11, 18, 21, 24), `pocsag`, `pocsag-cw`, `acars`, `ble-adv`, `gsm-l2`, `gsm-cbs`, `gsm-cbch-block`, `gsmtap`, `m17-lsf`, `m17-pkt`, `p25-tsbk`, `p25-lc`, `p25-hdu`, `nxdn-l3`, `tetra-sync`, `tetra-sysinfo`, `dmr-lc`, `dmr-csbk`, `dmr-pi`, `dmr-slottype`. Nested structures are chosen by a field value (Ethernet type → IPv4, IPv4 protocol → UDP), so an Ethernet frame opens down to the UDP payload.
- **Auto structure**: the *structure* list starts with `auto` — the structure is chosen by the content (a structure with a `detect` expression whose fields parse cleanly and agree with the length: Ethernet, ARP, IPv4, IPv6, 802.11, ADS-B). **HEX View** (Data) is the same dump without a structure, for any bytes.
- **PCAP Reader** (Data): a `.pcap` / `.pcapng` file on `bin` (File → Bytes) → one record per packet (`hex`, `hexSchema` from the link type, time, number) at *packets per second* or all at once; the `bin` output is the last packet. **PCAP Writer**: records with a `hex` field (Wi-Fi Sniffer, DMR / ADS-B decoders) and `bin` packets → a `.pcap` file for Wireshark (Save, or after every packet) → Bytes → File. Link types: Ethernet, 802.11, radiotap, raw IP.
- **Own structure**: *advanced → own structure* takes JSON; *Copy chosen structure to JSON* gives a starting point. A structure is `{endian, bitorder, fields:[…]}` or `{root, lib:{name:structure}}` for several linked ones.

```json
{ "endian": "be", "fields": [
  {"n":"ver",  "t":"bits", "w":4},
  {"n":"ihl",  "t":"bits", "w":4, "calc":"payload.off/4"},
  {"n":"len",  "t":"u16", "calc":"_len"},
  {"n":"proto","t":"u8", "enum":{"6":"TCP","17":"UDP"}},
  {"n":"csum", "t":"u16", "fmt":"hex", "calc":"inet16", "to":"payload"},
  {"n":"src",  "t":"bytes", "fmt":"ipv4"},
  {"n":"opts", "t":"bytes", "len":"ihl>5 ? (ihl-5)*4 : 0"},
  {"n":"payload","t":"rest", "sub":{"by":"proto","map":{"17":"udp"}}} ] }
```

Field types: `u8…u64`, `i8…i64` (any multiple of 8 bits), `f32`, `f64`, `bits` (`w` bits, signed optional), `bytes` (`len`), `str` (`len`, `enc`), `str6` (6-bit characters: ADS-B / AIS; `cs` charset, `trim`), `rest`, `list` (repeats a `sub` structure or an `item` until the data ends or `count`), `pad`. Options: `endian` (`be` / `le`, per structure or field), `bitorder` of the structure (`msb`: the first bit is the top one; `lsb`: 802.11), `scale` / `prec` / `unit` (show a number multiplied, the value stays an integer), `fmt` (`mac`, `ipv4`, `ipv6`, `plmn`, `m17call`, `hex`, `bin`, `ascii`, `bcd`, `dbm`, `time`), `enum`, `flags` (bit → name), `when` (the field exists only if the expression is true), `sub` (a structure name, an inline structure or `{by, map}`). Expressions (`len`, `count`, `when`, `calc`) use earlier fields: `+ - * / % & | ^ << >> == != < <= > >= && || ! ?:`, `field.$off` / `field.$len` (offset from the start of the structure and length in bytes), `_left` (bytes left while parsing), `_len` (size of the structure). `calc` is a checksum (`xor` puts a mask over it) — `crc8`, `crc16-ccitt`, `crc16-ccitt-false`, `crc16-x25`, `crc16-gsm`, `crc16-kermit`, `crc16-arc`, `crc16-modbus`, `crc24-adsb`, `crc16-m17`, `pocsag-bch`, `parity32`, `crc32`, `crc32c`, `inet16`, `xor8`, `sum8` (`from` / `to` set the range, `over:"all"` zeroes the field and takes the whole structure; `pseudo:{proto:17}` adds the IPv4 / IPv6 pseudo-header from the parent's `src` / `dst` — UDP, TCP, ICMPv6; `zeroFF`, `optional` for UDP's zero checksum) — or an expression that is recalculated on build.

**AIS Decoder** has a `raw` output: every message (not only positions) as a record with the message bits in `hex` and `hexSchema` `ais` — HEX Structure shows MMSI, status, speed, position in degrees, names and destination; a `text` input takes `!AIVDM` / `!AIVDO` sentences (multi-part messages are joined).

**POCSAG decoder** records carry `hex` (the address codeword and the data codewords, BCH and parity rebuilt) and `hexSchema` `pocsag`: HEX Structure shows the address, function and every 32-bit word with BCH(31,21) and parity checked (`pocsag-bch`, `parity32`).

**ACARS decoder** records carry `hex` (the block without parity bits, then the BCS) and `hexSchema` `acars`: mode, registration, ack, label, block id, message number, flight and text. `node tools/test-hexdec.mjs` runs the decoders' records against the structures.

**BLE Advertisements** records carry `hex` and `hexSchema` `ble-adv`: Web Bluetooth gives no raw packet, so the AD structures (name, TX power, manufacturer data, service data) are rebuilt from the event; HEX Structure shows them — flags, Apple iBeacon (UUID, major, minor, TX power), Eddystone (UID / URL), company ids. **BLE GATT** records have the value in `hex` (any structure can be chosen by hand).

**GSM** BCCH records carry the decoded L2 message in `hex` and `hexSchema` `gsm-l2`, cell-broadcast records (`GSM-CBS`) the 88-byte page with `gsm-cbs`. `gsm-l2` splits the message down to the bits: System Information 1 / 2 / 2bis / 2ter / 3 / 4 / 13 / 2quater — cell identity, LAI (`MCC-MNC`), LAC, control channel description (CCCH configuration, paging multiframes, T3212), cell options (DTX, radio link timeout), cell selection (hysteresis, RXLEV access minimum in dBm), RACH control parameters (max retransmissions, TX integer, access classes), the cell channel / BCCH frequency lists as ARFCN ranges (`gsm-freqs`: bitmap 0, range 1024 / 512 / 256 / 128, variable bitmap), the optional CBCH description of SI4, and the CSN.1 rest octets as a one-line summary (SI3: selection parameters, GPRS indicator; SI13: PBCCH or RAC; SI2quater: 3G neighbours). The same schema reads the CCCH messages Paging Request 1 / 2 / 3 (TMSI, IMSI / IMEI from the mobile identity) and Immediate Assignment (channel, ARFCN or MAIO / HSN, request reference, timing advance). `gsm-cbs` shows the serial number, message id, data coding scheme, page x of y and the text (GSM 7-bit, UCS-2). **GSMTAP** (UDP port 4729, the capture format of gr-gsm and Wireshark: ARFCN, signal level, frame number, channel type, then the L2 frame) opens down to the message when a `.pcap` goes through PCAP Reader → HEX Structure, also for Linux cooked (`sll`) and loopback (`null`) link types. Checked against the decoder's own parsers (`node tools/test-hexdec.mjs`). **GSM: Receive Bursts** now reads the CCCH of TS0 as well: Paging Request 1 / 2 / 3 (`GSM-PCH` records) and Immediate Assignment, Extended, Reject (`GSM-AGCH`: channel type and sub-channel, TN, TSC, ARFCN or MAIO / HSN, request reference, timing advance; the reject has the wait indication). The blocks are 6–9, 12–15, 16–19 of the 51-multiframe for a CCCH combined with SDCCH/4 and all nine for a non-combined one — the node takes it from CCCH-CONF in SI3 and until then reads only the three common blocks. The identities that paging carries (TMSI, IMSI) belong to particular subscribers, so by default the records hold only a marker (*paging identifiers* = `hash`: a hash with a random salt per session, the same subscriber gives the same marker while the receiver runs) and `hex` has those bytes zeroed; `none` leaves only the count; `show` gives them as they are (for your own network or lab). The readout shows `CCCH ok/blocks pag IA`. The IQ Generator (mode GSM) now transmits paging and assignments, so *GSM* presets show them; `node tools/test-gsm.mjs` runs the whole chain generator → receiver for both CCCH configurations and the three identifier modes. Presets *GSM: Paging, Assignments and HEX (USB SDR)* (spectrum marker on a BCCH carrier → shift → GSM: Receive Bursts → Rec Filter for paging / assignments → Table, and HEX Structure for the last control message) and *GSM: Paging, Assignments and HEX (Generator)* (the same without a radio; the IQ Generator has a *CCCH combined with SDCCH/4* switch). Since then, **GSM: Receive Bursts** also blind-decodes a dedicated channel's SDCCH/SACCH once Immediate Assignment points it there (see [GSM](#gsm)) — `GSM-SDCCH` / `GSM-SACCH` records, with `hex`/`hexSchema` `gsm-l2` for the SI5 case (reuses the SI2 schema) but a plain `hex` (no schema yet) for the MM messages (CM Service Request, Identity, Authentication, Location Updating, TMSI Reallocation); content once ciphering (A5) starts is not reached at all — the FIRE CRC that gates this decode never passes on it.

**M17** call records carry the 30-byte link setup frame in `hex` and `hexSchema` `m17-lsf` (callsigns in base-40, CAN, stream type, encryption, meta, CRC-16 checked); packet records `m17-pkt` (the protocol byte picks IPv4 for protocol 4).

**P25** records carry `hex` and `hexSchema`: TSBK blocks (`p25-tsbk`: 12 bytes with CRC-16 checked; grants, updates, status broadcasts, registration), Link Control words (`p25-lc`, from LDU1 / TDULC) and the header data unit (`p25-hdu`: message indicator, algorithm, key id, talkgroup). The old `hex` of unparsed TSBK / LC is now the whole block.

**NXDN** `call` and `msg` records carry the layer-3 message in `hex` and `hexSchema` `nxdn-l3` (calls and releases: call type, options, source and destination, cipher and key; channel assignments: timer and channel; site information; the unparsed rest as bytes). The old `hex` of an unparsed message is now the whole message.

**TETRA** `sync` and `sysinfo` records carry the PDU bits in `hex` and `hexSchema` `tetra-sync` (colour code, TN / FN / MN, MCC, MNC) and `tetra-sysinfo` (carrier, band, offset, duplex, access parameters, location area, service flags).

**DMR Signalling Builder** (Protocols): the bytes of a link-control word (9 B, `dmr-lc`) or a CSBK / PI header / data header (10 B, `dmr-csbk`, `dmr-pi`) from HEX Structure (`bin` or `text`) → Reed-Solomon RS(12,9) or CRC-16 with the burst mask, BPTC(196,96), the slot type (Golay) with the colour code and the sync word → bursts on `blk` (time slots with CACH, like DMR Data Builder) and the 33-byte burst itself on `bin` / `text`. Types: PI header, Voice LC header, Terminator LC, CSBK, MBC header, Data header.
