Preset *DMR: Signalling from HEX → Burst → Decoder (Loopback)* goes through Symbol Player, shaper, FM modulator and the Digital Voice Decoder and shows the decoded call in a second HEX Structure. In the structures the CRC-16 of CSBK and PI is a `calc` with `xor` (the burst mask).

**Decoders**: DMR records (LC header / end, CSBK, PI) and ADS-B demodulator records carry `hex` (the decoded bytes) and `hexSchema`; wire the decoder's `rec` to HEX Structure to see them field by field. **Wi-Fi Sniffer (ESP32)** (Sources): 802.11 frames from an ESP32 over WebSerial (Chrome / Edge) — flash [`tools/wifi-sniffer-esp32`](tools/wifi-sniffer-esp32/wifi-sniffer-esp32.ino) (promiscuous mode, channel hopping; lines `WIFI,<channel>,<rssi>,<hex>`). Every frame is a record (type, addresses, SSID, channel, security, RSSI, `hex`), the `text` input takes the same lines without hardware. The firmware and the serial node are not tested on hardware (the line parser and the 802.11 structure are tested on sample frames). Use it on your own network or with permission; frames are not decrypted.

**Radio Bridge (ESP32-C5)** (Sources) is one node for everything the chip hears and sends: Wi-Fi (2.4 and 5 GHz; management, control and — by a separate switch — data frames; channel hopping), BLE (passive or active scan) and IEEE 802.15.4 (Zigbee, Thread, Matter-over-Thread). Flash [`tools/radio-bridge-esp32c5`](tools/radio-bridge-esp32c5/radio-bridge-esp32c5.ino) (Arduino-ESP32 ≥ 3.3, NimBLE-Arduino ≥ 2.5; **not tested on hardware**), connect over USB (Chrome / Edge) and press *Connect*. Every frame is one record with the same names for every protocol — `proto`, `kind` (`beacon`, `probe-req`, `adv_ind`, `data`…), `src`, `dst`, `net` (BSSID or PAN), `chan`, `rssi`, plus the packet's own fields by name (`ie.ssid`, `ie.rsn.akm`, `p2p.device_info.name`, `ad.name`, `zb_cluster`…) — and `hex` / `hexSchema`, so it goes straight to *Rec Filter*, *Table*, *Rec → Entities* / *Graph* and *HEX Structure*. A filter on a device is one condition: `r.src==='aa:bb:cc:dd:ee:ff' || r.dst==='aa:bb:cc:dd:ee:ff'`. Transmit: `txbin` (bytes, e.g. from HEX Structure *build*), `txrec` (a record with `hex`, optionally `proto`) or `tx` (text: `WIFI <hex>`, `ZB <hex> csma` or bare hex for the *tx: protocol* choice) → `TX …` to the firmware. The auto-responder to Probe requests is built in: tick *auto-reply*, give the SSID and our BSSID, keep the channel fixed (a hopping radio is already elsewhere when the answer goes out); a request for another SSID is ignored, a broadcast one is answered, at most once a second per address. Any other frame is built by names in HEX Structure (`ie.ssid`, `ie.rates`, `ie.channel`; the element lengths are calculated) and sent through `txbin`. Limits: encrypted payloads stay encrypted (the CCMP / TKIP / AES header is shown), no BLE extended advertising or coded PHY, no 6 GHz; the line protocol is in the firmware header. Logic: `modules/radiobridge-kernels.js`.

**Radio Bridge: capture quality, channel levels and CSI.** The firmware keeps frames in a variable-length ring buffer (48 KB), writes one line per frame, has a bigger USB TX buffer and a deeper Wi-Fi RX queue, and cuts *data* frames to a separate length (*bytes kept per data frame*, default 96 — enough for the 802.11, LLC and IP / TCP headers; the payload is encrypted anyway), so far more frames survive; `INFO` shows `DROPPED` and `RINGFREE`, and every channel window reports its own loss. Tick *channel stats* (firmware `STAT`): the chip counts **every** frame it hears (any type, regardless of the selected classes) and sends one line per channel window (per hop dwell): frames, bytes, max / mean RSSI (−127 — nothing heard), busy % (airtime estimate from length and rate) and lost frames → output `stat` (records `chan-stat`: `id` `ch6`, `chan`, `rssi_max`, `rssi_avg`, `busy`, `fps`, `frames`, `drop`). Tick *CSI* (firmware `CSI ON <ms>`): output `csi` (records `csi`: `src`, `chan`, `rssi`, `sc`, and the lists `csi` — amplitude and `csi_ph` — phase per subcarrier, comma separated). Wire `stat` to a Table with key `id` (the latest window per channel) and its `all` to **Table Chart**; `csi` goes to a plain Table (history) and to a chart (lines or *heat*). Preset *Radio: Wi-Fi channel level · occupancy · CSI*. **Not tested on hardware**: the CSI config fields (`acquire_csi_legacy`, `acquire_csi_ht20`) compile on the C5 SDK, but the CSI itself was not seen on the air; airtime uses the rx_ctrl `rate` plus the bb format / MCS when the SDK has those fields (else an MCS 7 guess for non-legacy frames), so treat busy % as an estimate. If the sketch does not compile, set `WITH_CSI 0`.

**HEX Field Finder** (Data) looks for fields in packets of an unknown format: records with a hex field (decoders, Table, PCAP Reader), `bin` or hex lines pile up; packets of one length (or of one kind and length) are compared byte by byte. It reports candidates with a score 0…1: Unix time (a time equal to the capture time scores highest), latitude / longitude (integers divided by 10^k, 60, 360, 3600…, or 360/2^n, little- or big-endian, signed or not — the values must cluster in a narrow window and change smoothly from packet to packet; a range check alone is not enough, random bytes pass it), float32 / float64 degrees, counters (+1 or +k), identifiers (few distinct values), ASCII text, BCD, a CRC or sum of the tail over the preceding bytes, constants and random runs. Packets of one length are split by the layout of their zero bytes (padding, empty fields). If nearly all changing bytes are random and no checksum is found, the verdict is *looks random: encrypted, compressed or noise*. The `rec` output carries the candidates, `text` a draft structure (JSON) for HEX Structure (advanced → own structure), *Copy schema JSON* puts it on the clipboard. From the command line: `node tools/hex-scan.mjs capture.csv [--col hex|raw|data] [--group-by kind,rate] [--schema]`. The heuristics find what is there; they say nothing about what a field means — that stays with a human. **Except with a reference:** if the quantity the packets carry is known — a thermometer next to the sensor, a voltmeter, a signal generator — wire it to the **`ref`** input (or name a number field of the record in *reference quantity*; *reference name* and *unit* label the result) and every packet is stored with the reference value it arrived with. The finder then correlates the reference with every byte column (1–4 bytes, big / little endian, signed or not), with every window of 3–16 **bits** at any bit offset (most sensors pack 10- and 12-bit values with flags) and with BCD numbers of 2–4 digits, and prints the best non-overlapping ones as `quantity`: `temperature ≈ 0.1·x − 40 °C (r=0.9999, rmse 0.03, 150 pkts, 12 bits from bit 32)`. The scale snaps to a round number (0.1, 0.01, 1/16, 5…) when it is within 2 %, the offset to a multiple of it when it is within the error. Counters are not taken for a quantity even when the reference is a ramp; a constant reference is reported as such (vary it while recording); windows that tie are resolved toward the byte boundary, then toward the narrower window, and a window that starts in the middle of a byte grows over constant leading bits of that byte. The draft structure then has the field with `scale`, `bias` (new in HEX Structure: the shown value is `v·scale + bias`), `prec` and `unit`; a field that is not byte-aligned becomes a `bits` field, the rest of the packet stays bytes and bits. From the command line: `--ref temp --unit "°C"` (a CSV column). Preset: *Hex: Reverse a Sensor — Find the Temperature Field by a Reference*. It needs a reference that actually changes (tens of packets over a few °C or volts), and a correlation is not a proof: a reference that drifts together with an unrelated slow field can fool it, so look at `r`, `rmse` and the number of packets. Logic: `modules/hex-scan-kernels.js`, `node tools/test-hexscan.mjs` (synthetic tracks with a counter, time, latitude / longitude, checksum; angles as 16 bits of a circle; random and half-encrypted packets must not produce coordinates).

Presets *Hex: Parse a Packet (Ethernet / IPv4 / UDP)*, *Hex: PCAP File → Packets → HEX Structure*, *Hex: Table Rows → Packets → Table*, *Wi-Fi: ESP32 Sniffer → Table and HEX Structure*. Logic: `modules/hex-kernels.js`, structures `modules/hex-formats.js`, `node tools/test-hex.mjs` (CRC check values, the Wikipedia IPv4 header, the classic ADS-B frame with CRC-24, build / parse round trips, endianness and bit order, truncated packets, structure checks).

## Map and records

**Records** (`rec` port) carry objects with arbitrary fields — `{lat, lon, id, snr, …}` — from decoders, CSV files and sensors to the map, logs and filters.

- **Fields → Rec** builds a record from its inputs (the field list is editable, ports appear on *Apply fields*); constants like `icon=plane; color=#f80` are added to every record. With the `rec` input it adds/overrides fields in passing records. Emits on change, on a `go` trigger, or every block.
- **Rec → Fields** splits the last record back into ports; *Fields from last rec* fills the list from what actually arrives.
- **CSV → Rec** (text lines or a whole file, header or explicit field names, `,` `;` tab), **Rec Log** (save CSV / GeoJSON / KML / GPX, replay; the map and *Rec: Unique by Key* export the same way), **Rec Filter** (JS condition over `r`).
- **NMEA Parser** (Data): NMEA 0183 text from a GNSS receiver, echo sounder, anemometer, compass or an AIS feed → records and numbers (see [NMEA 0183](#nmea-0183)).
- **My Position**: typed in (lat/lon or Maidenhead locator, ⌖ fills it once from geolocation) or live GPS. Other nodes use it for distances and bearings. *look direction* (used when GPS has no heading), *field of view*, its range and *distance rings* go into the record, so the Map draws your own view sector and rings.
- **Geo from Text**: coordinates from any decoded text — degrees/minutes/seconds, NMEA and APRS (`4903.50N/07201.75W`), ACARS (`N55123E037456`), decimal pairs, 6-char locators (4-char optional).
- **Mark Point**: your position + RSSI/SNR/azimuth/frequency as a record, on a button, a trigger (`go`, e.g. a Joystick button) or every N seconds; **📷 Mark + photo** opens the camera (the phone's camera or the gallery) and puts the photo into the record. The record is a field measurement: `lat lon alt` (from the wires or My Position), `h` — receiving antenna height above ground (the *antenna height* parameter), `rssi` / `snr`, `azimuth`, `freq`, `tx` — the source being measured (Source Locator groups by it), `session` — *field* (moving about) or *quiet* (a calm place for a long listen: filter a Table by `session` to feed one or the other set), `rx_ant`, `rx_gain`, `note`, `photo`, `name` (P1, P2…). Wire `rec` to a Table (store, edit later), to Source Locator and to the Map. The level can be typed in the *level by hand, dBm* parameter (used while the `rssi` input is not wired). Presets *Field Survey: Mark Points with Photos → Table, Locator, Map* and *Trilateration: Type RSSI, Phone GPS → Locator, Map* (phone GPS + typed RSSI → Source Locator → Map).
- **Source Locator**: finds a transmitter from marks (or many at once with *group by*, e.g. `bssid` — one estimate per access point) — signal level (log-distance model, unknown power) and/or bearings; outputs the estimate with an error radius and shows the probability map. Level-based location works on VHF/UHF at short range; on HF (skywave) only bearings make sense. **Whole list from a Table**: the `rows` input takes the Table's list at once (with its filter; marks in the rows need `lat lon` / `grid` and a level or `azimuth`). The set is cached by row — when it changes only the added or deleted rows are applied and only the affected sources (groups) are recomputed; a deleted row disappears from the estimate. Rows from a Table are not trimmed by *keep measurements*; *Clear* empties the node and the list is loaded again. **Height (3D)**: marks may carry `elevation` (° above the horizon; *Mark Point* has an `elevation` input) — every mark with it gives the source altitude `z + d·tan(el)` (weighted: near marks and flat angles count more; *elevation error*), and the estimate gets `alt`, `h` (above ground, from the Horizon map), `alt_err`, `height_src` (`elevation` / `assumed` — ground at the point + *assumed source height*). The `alt` output and `rec` go to Video Overlay (a diamond at the real height), the Map or a Table; `rays` (*rays* parameter) are links from every mark (its `alt`, or ground + `h`) to the estimate — wire to Overlay `rec2` and the Map to see the bearing lines in 3D; *Save estimates to table* → `analysis/sources` (`lat lon alt h err_m alt_err_m height_src`). Logic: `modules/locate3d-kernels.js`, `node tools/test-locate3d.mjs`.
- **FT8** now outputs decoded messages (`msg`) and records: call (`from`), addressee (`to`), locator (remembered per call), SNR (2500 Hz, WSJT-X style estimate), distance and azimuth from your position — a propagation map in one wire.
- **Station Schedule** (Radio): EiBi CSV or your own CSV (`khz, time, days, station, lang, target, itu, lat, lon`). Stations on air now go to the spectrum (`bands` → Spectrum Analyzer), the ones on the tuned frequency to the `now` text, transmitters to the map (country centre when the file has no coordinates; EiBi `/XXX` relay sites are used). Download by URL (optional CORS proxy — eibispace.de has no CORS headers) or load the file; it is kept in the browser. Filters: *only aimed at my area* (EiBi target codes — `Eu`, `EEu`, `NAm`, `As`… — worked out from your position, or type your own list, country codes like `RUS` included), *max distance to transmitter*, and `spanLo`/`spanHi` inputs (wire `freqLo`/`freqHi` from USB SDR) to label only what the receiver sees.

**Map** fields: `lat`, `lon` (or `grid` — Maidenhead locator), `id` (same id → one object with a track), `t`, `label`, `icon` (`dot square triangle diamond star cross plus plane ship car truck bus train tram bike moto heli drone walk antenna tx rx me flag sat balloon` or any emoji/text; vehicles turn with `heading` or along the track), `color`, `size`, `radius` (m, circle), `azimuth` + `range` (km, great-circle bearing line), `rings` (distance rings around the object, km: `1,5,10` or one number, labelled), `fov` (field of view, °) + `fov_range` (km; default `range`, else the largest ring) — a sector around `heading` (or `azimuth`, or the track direction), `heading` (rotates plane/triangle; otherwise taken from the track), `lat2`/`lon2` (line to a second point), `path` (`[[lat,lon],…]` ahead), `track: 0` (no history line), `snr`/`rssi` (colour when no `color`). Everything else is shown as `key: value` on click. Tap on an empty spot — coordinates and locator on the `pick` output.

**Editing on the map** (*mode*): *view* (default) only shows; *edit* — tap a point from `rows` (or one added here) and a form opens with its fields (**＋** adds a field, **Save** applies), the selected point can be dragged; *add* — a tap on an empty spot creates a point named `P1`, `P2`… (*new point name prefix*, *new point icon*). *line*, *polygon*, *ruler*, *circle* — drawing modes: a tap adds a vertex, the panel at the bottom left shows the count, length and area while you draw (**↶** removes the last vertex, **✓** finishes, **✕** cancels); a circle is two taps (centre, a point on the edge). A shape is a record too: `gtype` (`line` / `polygon` / `ruler` / `circle`), `geom` (`[[lat,lon],…]` as JSON text; a circle is `lat`/`lon` + `radius`), `lat`/`lon` — the centre, and the measurements `length_m` (perimeter for a polygon), `area_m2`; `color` is set by *color of new shapes*. A ruler also labels every segment with its length and bearing and the total. Lengths and areas are great-circle / spherical; drag in *edit* moves the whole shape. Shapes go to GeoJSON / KML as LineString / Polygon. **Editing a shape** (*edit*): tap its body (inside a polygon / circle, next to a line) to select it — squares appear on the vertices and small circles in the middle of the edges. Drag a vertex to move it; press a middle circle to insert a vertex and drag it at once; **double tap** a vertex to delete it (a polygon keeps at least 3, a line 2); a circle has a diamond on its edge to change the radius; dragging the centre marker moves the whole shape. Length, area and the centre are recalculated and go to the Table on release. In *edit*, **🗑** in the form deletes the point or shape: a record `{name, _delete: 1}` goes to `edit`, and a Table with *upsert key col* removes the row with that name.

**How to use it**: tick *mode buttons on the map* (Edit group) and a row of buttons appears over the map — ☝ view, ✎ edit, ＋ add a point, ╱ line, ⬠ polygon, 📏 ruler, ◯ circle — so a tile without the settings panel can switch modes too. In ✎ a **long press on an empty spot** puts a new point (`P1`, `P2`…) and opens its form, so adding and editing need no mode switch; ＋ keeps adding point after point; after a shape is finished the map goes back to ✎. The form has the **name** (edit it and **Save** to rename — links to the record follow), **▾** next to *icon* opens a grid of icons (the button at the end of the toolbar chooses the icon of new points; the grid opens next to the button you pressed), **＋** adds a field, 🗑 deletes; the checklist of a point is at the top of the form. *icon size ×* (Display) scales every icon and the checklist badge; the checklist items sit at the top of the form.

**Links between points**: the ⤳ tool — tap one point, then another and a link appears (the same pair again removes it); in ✎ a tap on a line opens its form (label, weight, colour, dashed, 🗑). Links are records `id, from, to, label, weight, color, dash` (the ends are the names of the points, as in Graph) and go to the `link` output — wire it to a **second Table** (a list of its own, *upsert key col* `id`) and wire that Table's `rows` back to the Map's `links` input (links can also arrive in `rows`, like in Graph). Deleting or renaming a point deletes / renames its links in that Table. **Snapping**: a vertex of a line, polygon or ruler that is put (or dragged) within a few pixels of a point lands exactly on it.

Preset **Map Editor: Points, Shapes, Rings, Checklists ↔ Table** shows it all on a small data set (shops with checklists, a car with a view sector, a zone, a route, a circle, your own sector) that it puts into the Table lists `demo/trip` and `demo/trip tasks`.

Every change goes to the `edit` output as a record `{name, lat, lon, …changed fields}` and shows on the map at once. Wire `edit` to a [Table](#table) `rec` input, set the Table's *upsert key col* to `name` (the column the points are named by) and wire its `rows` back to the map: new points become rows, edits and moves update them.

Works offline: the vector base map (Natural Earth 10m: coast, lakes, rivers, country and region borders, ~7 000 cities, English names) is fetched once from `data/basemap.json` and kept in IndexedDB. *Download places* adds ~130 000 towns from GeoNames (shown from zoom 8), or import a GeoNames dump / CSV `name,lat,lon[,population]`. Optional OSM / OpenTopoMap tiles are cached as you view them and stay available offline. Points can be saved in the browser under a name (*save points as*). Rebuild the base map: `node tools/basemap.mjs`.

- **Satellites** (Radio): SGP4 orbits from CelesTrak TLE groups (amateur, weather, NOAA, stations…) and uplink/downlink frequencies from the SatNOGS database — both downloaded once and kept in the browser (or imported from files; set a CORS proxy if a site refuses cross-origin requests). On the map: position, visibility footprint, ground track ahead; click a satellite to select it. For the selected one: az/el/range, next passes (24 h), sky plot, and **Doppler**: `freq` is the downlink as heard (wire it to `tuneFreq` of the USB SDR — the channel follows the pass), `up` is what to transmit. Downlinks of satellites above the horizon are marked on the spectrum (`bands`). Orbits: [satellite.js](https://github.com/shashwatak/satellite-js) 5.0.0 (MIT) in `vendor/`. TLEs age: refresh them every week or two.
- **Horizon** (Radio): the skyline with mountains as an `azimuth → elevation` profile for the observer (*My Position* or wired `lat` / `lon`). Heights come from Terrain-RGB tiles (AWS Terrarium, one per ~40 km at zoom 10, downloaded once and kept in the browser); every azimuth is traced out to *radius* with earth curvature and refraction (*k*, 4/3 by default) from the antenna height. `el` is the horizon at the `az` input (+ *margin*), `max` / `maxaz` is the highest point. The profile is recomputed when the point moves by more than 1 km. Or import your own `azimuth, elevation` file (CSV / TXT, gaps are interpolated) — for buildings and trees, which the terrain data does not have. Open data resolves ~30 m, zoom 10 is ~150 m per pixel: lower the zoom for speed, raise it for nearby ridges. Set a CORS proxy if the tile server refuses cross-origin requests. **Offline**: every tile is kept in the browser database (IndexedDB) on the first download and never fetched again; decoded tiles stay in memory (up to 240), so a window that follows a moving observer decodes only the new ones. *Download area for offline* (advanced) fetches a circle of *download area* km around the observer at the chosen zoom in advance (up to 2500 tiles) and asks the browser for persistent storage, so the data is not cleared when space runs low.
- **Radio Reach** (Radio): how far you receive aircraft from a point with a given antenna, with the mountains in the way. For every azimuth it walks the terrain of the *Horizon* height map (100 m steps, then 1 km beyond the map, where the ground is taken as the observer's ground or sea level — *far*) with the curvature of the Earth and refraction *k*, and finds where an aircraft at the given altitude goes below the line over the highest terrain in front of it — the first loss of the signal. **Several antennas (*ants*, m above ground: `2, 10, 30`) and altitudes (*alts*: `FL100, FL250`, feet or `3000m`) at once**: a polar plot with a curve per altitude for the antenna picked by *antNo*, a readout with min (and its azimuth) / avg / max reach and the covered area for every pair, and a line comparing the antennas at the picked altitude — test antenna heights and compare places (move the point, or feed `lat` / `lon` / `h` from a *Table* row, and log the results). Check: on flat ground the reach of a 10 m antenna at FL100 is 237 km (the radio horizon, 4.12 km·(√10+√2948)). **Received aircraft**: wire the *ADS-B Decoder* `rec` — every aircraft is a dot on the plot, the dashed line is the farthest received per 5° sector, `seen` / `obs` count them, and a received aircraft that the calculation says is hidden is red (`miss`): tune *k* and the antenna height until the red dots go away. **Out**: `min` `avg` `max` (km) and `area` (km²) for the picked pair; `rec` — a summary record per pair (point, ground, antenna, altitude, min / avg / max, area, worst and best azimuth) once per calculation (wire it to a *Table* to keep a log); `poly` — the coverage areas as records with a `path` for the **Map**. *Save to table* appends the summary to the list in *save* (`analysis/reach`). The point must lie inside the radius of a *Horizon* node (the terrain is downloaded there); without Horizon a flat Earth is assumed. Preset *Radio Reach: ADS-B Coverage over Terrain (Generator)*. Logic: `modules/reach-kernels.js`, `node tools/test-reach.mjs`.
- **Ambient Light** (Sources): illuminance in lux from `AmbientLightSensor` (`lux` number, `slux` signal; Chrome / Android, may need the flag `#enable-generic-sensor-extra-classes`)
- **Sensor (Generic Sensor API)** also reads `GravitySensor` (gravity without the motion; in Firefox / Safari it is taken from `devicemotion`) and `RelativeOrientationSensor` (quaternion `qx qy qz qw` without the compass)
- **Compute Pressure** (Sources): CPU load state of the device (`nominal` / `fair` / `serious` / `critical`) as `level` 0…3, `state` and a `rec` record on every change (Chrome / Edge 125+)
- **Network Information** (Sources): `downlink` (Mbit/s), `rtt` (ms), `online` and the connection `type`, a `rec` on every change (Chrome / Edge)
- **Idle Detection** (Sources): `idle` (user away), `locked` (screen locked) and `state`; press *Allow and start*, the threshold is at least 60 s (Chrome / Edge)
- **Broadcast Channel** (Sources): messages between tabs and windows of the same site by a channel name — the `send` / `val` inputs go out, the messages of other tabs (and other nodes with the same name) come on `text` / `rec`
- **WebTransport** (Sources): datagrams over HTTP/3 (QUIC) — `send` out, `text` / `rec` in; for a self-signed server (14 days at most) put its certificate SHA-256 into *certificate SHA-256*
- **Camera**: *torch* (flash light) and *Take photo* — a full-resolution still through `ImageCapture`, saved as a JPEG file; *Record video* (the `rec` input starts it by level) writes the camera track as webm / mp4, with the microphone sound if *record: with sound* is on — see [Camera tools](#camera-tools)
- **Orientation** (Sources): where a phone's camera looks — `az` (0 = north, clockwise), `el` (above the horizon) and `roll`, from `AbsoluteOrientationSensor` (Chrome / Android) or `deviceorientation` (Safari asks for permission; press *Start*). *Camera* picks the rear or the front lens, the screen rotation is accounted for. The compass of a phone is off by 5–15°: correct it in *Video Overlay*.
- **Video Overlay** (Video): a picture (`vid` — the new full-size video output of *Camera* and *Video (file/URL)*; or an `img` raster — *TV Decoder* of an analog FPV / TV signal, SSTV, LRPT) with objects and a HUD over it. It reads no sensors itself: the camera position (`lat` / `lon` / `alt`, m — *My Position*) and direction (`az` / `el` / `roll` — *Orientation*, or telemetry of a drone) come on its inputs, `fov` is a parameter or an input. Objects come on `rec` / `rec2` (*Satellites*, *ADS-B Decoder*, AIS, any record with `lat` / `lon` and an altitude — `alt_km`, `alt_m`, `alt` in metres, ADS-B `alt` in feet — or just `az` / `el` / `range_km`) and are projected from their coordinates on the WGS84 ellipsoid: stub wire models (aircraft, satellite, ship, other), labels, `path` of a satellite, ADS-B extrapolated along the course. Also drawn: the horizon with compass marks, an elevation ladder, the skyline from *Horizon*, the **terrain relief in 3D** (*relief*: a shaded mesh over the heights of *Horizon* out to its *radius* — snow, rock, grass by height, light by slope, haze with distance; *lines* — a wire mesh over a video; *auto* — solid without a video; an aircraft behind a ridge is dimmed; without an `alt` input the camera stands on the ground by the height map; add a *Horizon* node to the patch, it downloads the tiles) and the HUD — crosshair, a heading tape, numbers from inputs `a`…`d`. *find* puts an arrow at the edge to an object outside the frame. Without a video it draws the sky. Presets *AR: Satellites Through the Camera (Phone)*, *AR: ADS-B Aircraft in the Sky*, *AR: Drones Around (Remote ID, Phone)* and *Telemetry: HUD over Video → Record*. The output `vid` is the finished picture (video, objects and HUD) — wire it to *Record Video*. **More**: *Sun and Moon* are drawn at their true positions and sizes (the Moon with its phase and the bright limb towards the Sun); **calibration** — put the crosshair on the Sun, the Moon, a tapped object or a *find* match and press *Align crosshair to target*: the azimuth / elevation corrections are set for you (a phone compass is off by 5–15°; the Sun, the Moon, planets and stars are the reference that needs no network). Also drawn: **planets** (Mercury … Neptune, Keplerian elements, an arc-minute or so), **bright stars** up to a chosen magnitude (46 of them: Sirius, Vega, Polaris, the Big Dipper, the Southern Cross…) and **deep sky** (Andromeda, Pleiades, Orion Nebula, Magellanic Clouds, Omega Centauri, the Galactic Centre) — tap one to select it or align to it; *find* matches their names. **Tap** an object: a card (name, range, azimuth / elevation, altitude, speed, heading, LOS), an arrow when it is off screen, and the `pick` record plus `paz` / `pel` / `prange` outputs. **Line of sight**: every object is tested against the terrain rings of *Horizon* — an aircraft or a satellite behind a ridge is dimmed and marked “no LOS”; `vis` is the record list of what is in line of sight (feed it to a Table or a scanner), `nvis` / `nhid` the counts. **Trails** of moving objects (*trails, s*, the positions kept as they arrive, older parts fade). **Video delay** (*lag*, ms): the pose is taken from that long ago, so the marks do not run ahead of a delayed picture. **Drone**: *tilt* is the camera's angle relative to the heading / pitch input (FPV cameras sit tilted up), *home* (first fix or *Set home here*) draws a flag, the distance, the bearing and an arrow; Remote ID drones get a quadcopter stub and their `height_m` is counted from the terrain. **OSM** (*OSM*, off by default): peaks with heights, towns, major roads and rivers from Overpass for *OSM radius* around the camera (cached in the browser; a feature behind the relief is not drawn; Horizon heights are needed to lay them on the ground). **Airfields and towns** (*airfields and runways*, *towns and villages*, off by default): aerodromes, helipads, runways (drawn to scale, not thinner than 3 px, with the designator «09/27») and towns / villages with the distance, laid on the Horizon terrain. **Points live in Table lists**: everything is written to the lists `geo/airfields`, `geo/runways`, `geo/places` and `geo/peaks` of the [Table](#table) database (only missing rows are added, so your edits stay) and *Video Overlay*, *Flight Sim* and the map read the lists, not a private cache; *own points* (`lists`: one or more Table lists, comma-separated; columns `name`, `lat`, `lon`, `h` — height above ground in m, or `alt` above sea level, `color`) are drawn as coloured diamonds with the distance — signal sources, observation posts, masts. Airfields come from the **OurAirports** database (two CSV files, ~14 MB, downloaded once and kept in the browser; no Overpass needed), or from OSM cells if it cannot be downloaded; towns — at once from the built-in map (and from the GeoNames list if the *Map* node downloaded it), plus villages from OSM Overpass per 0.5° cell (each cell is kept in the browser; a busy server — HTTP 5xx, a «runtime error» answer — is never cached, the next mirror is tried, then a minute's pause). Everything is downloaded apart from the terrain; *Download area for offline* fetches the airport database and a circle of OSM cells of *download area radius* km in advance. **The Sun** is the real one for the place and the time: the terrain mesh is lit by it (slopes towards the Sun are brighter), casts shadows (*shadows*: rays towards the Sun over the heights, to 12 km, a few ms when the mesh is rebuilt), turns dark at night, and without a video the sky goes from night to day blue (stars and planets only when the Sun is down); *haze: visibility* fades distant ridges into the sky (clearer air high on the slopes); the terrain is lit from smoothed vertex normals (no facets); the visible horizon (the compass line) is lower than the level by the dip of the horizon at the altitude (≈1.5° at 2500 m) and the land beyond the mesh radius is filled in the haze colour up to it; the Horizon skyline (orange) is drawn only where it is valid — at the height it was computed for and when the 3D relief is not drawn. *Sun time shift* moves the hour (+6 h — evening shadows), the Sun and its azimuth / elevation are on the status line. Logic: `modules/geofeat-kernels.js`, `modules/geofeat.js`, `node tools/test-geofeat.mjs`. The geometry is in `overlay-kernels.js`, checked by `node tools/test-overlay.mjs` (the Sun and the Moon against the Meeus examples). Record fields for the stub models: `heading`, `roll` and `pitch` (°, a banking and climbing aircraft); `gone: true` with an `id` removes the object at once. The terrain mesh is built once and shifted while the camera moves less than 60 m, so a flying camera does not rebuild it every frame.
- **Flight Sim** (Sources): an arcade aircraft and cameras for the *Video Overlay* — a minimal flight simulator and a viewer for ADS-B traffic. **fly**: your own aircraft on `ail` / `elev` / `rud` (−1…1) and `thr` (a stick −1…1 with the centre at half throttle, a lever 0…1, or *stick up only* where the lower half is idle — the *Joystick* with *hold = left Y* starts at idle), banking turns, a stall at low speed (the nose drops until the speed returns), take-off and landing; a hard touchdown, a fast one or a bank over 25° is a crash (*Reset* or a pulse on `reset`). It is a simple kinematic model (not aerodynamics): the stick sets the bank and the pitch angle, the throttle sets the speed. The start point is the `lat` / `lon` inputs, else *My Position*, else the advanced parameters; *start = nearest runway* puts the aircraft on the nearest OSM runway within 30 km (the same airfield cells as in *Video Overlay*, downloaded once), heading along it, throttle idle — take off with 38+ m/s and the stick pulled back; the aircraft waits (up to 20 s) until *Horizon* knows the ground there, then starts *start height* above it. **watch ADS-B**: the camera follows one aircraft of the `rec` input (*ADS-B Decoder*): the nearest to the tower, or the one matching *follow* (a callsign or an ICAO code), *Next aircraft* (or a pulse on `next`) switches; positions are extrapolated along the course and the bank is estimated from the turn rate. **Cameras** (`cam`, a pulse on the `cam` input switches): *chase* (behind and above, the heading lags a turn), *cockpit*, *tower* (a fixed point, looks at the aircraft and zooms on it) and *orbit* around it. Outputs `lat lon alt az el roll fov` are the camera — wire them to *Video Overlay* and to *Horizon* (the terrain follows the camera); `rec` is your aircraft as a stub model with its bank and pitch (hidden in the cockpit view); `kts agl vsi hdg pwr` go to the HUD inputs `a…d`. The ground is `Horizon.hAt` (without *Horizon* a flat ground at *ground height*). The engine must be running: the time goes by its blocks. Presets *Flight Sim: Fly over Terrain (Joystick)* and *Flight Sim: Watch ADS-B Aircraft (Generator)*. Logic: `modules/flightsim-kernels.js`, `node tools/test-flightsim.mjs`.
- **Record Video** (Output): the mirror of *Video (file/URL)* — frames from `vid` (a video, a canvas, the `vid` output of *Video Overlay* with its HUD) or `img` (*TV Decoder*, *TV Hopper* mosaic, SSTV…) go to a webm / mp4 file through MediaRecorder at a constant frame rate. *Record / stop* and **Snapshot PNG**; *format* (auto — the first the browser can encode: VP9, VP8, H.264), *frame rate*, *bitrate*, *max width*, *save to* — a download or **a file on disk** (streamed, no length limit; Chrome / Edge). Inputs: `gate` — record while it is above 0 (a decoder `lock`, a race flag…), `snap` — a PNG on its rising edge; outputs `on`, `sec`. A webm from MediaRecorder has no duration in its header: browsers and VLC play it, some editors want a remux (`ffmpeg -i in.webm -c copy out.webm`). Presets *Video: File with HUD → Record*, *Analog TV: Record Video and Snapshots (Generator)*

- **Internet Radio** (Sources): search [radio-browser.info](https://www.radio-browser.info) — an open community database with a public API — by name, tag and country; the result is kept in the browser. Stations go out on `rows` as one set — wire it to the Map `rows` and a new search replaces the points (stations without coordinates sit around the country centre); wire the same `rows` to a Table and the list of stations is stored there (*rows wire*: replace or add); click one to play it: its URL goes to *Audio Stream (URL)*, which now starts on a new URL from the wire and gives the sound in stereo (`L` / `R`; a mono stream is the same in both; *Capture Tab/Screen (Audio)* is stereo too; old patches with `audio` are converted on load). An https page cannot play `http://` streams, and only streams that send CORS headers can be captured into the graph.

Presets: *Internet Radio on the Map*, *Satellites: Track and Doppler*, *FT8: Propagation Map*, *Fox Hunt: Locate Transmitter*, *HF: Who Is On Air (Schedule)*, *Map: My Position and Points from CSV*, *HFDL: Receive and Aircraft Map*, *ADS-B: Aircraft Map (Generator)*.

### Rec → Entities rules

One rule per line, `#` starts a comment. The separator is a space-bar-space ` | ` (a bare `|` inside `a|b` means "or").

```
[out[!]:] conditions | fields
```

**Conditions** — space-separated, all must match:

| Form | Matches when |
|---|---|
| `field` | the field has a value |
| `!field` | the field is missing or empty |
| `field=a\|b` | the value equals `a` or `b` |
| `field!=a` | the value differs from `a` |
| `field~regex` | the value matches the regex (case-insensitive) |

**Fields** — comma-separated:

| Form | Result |
|---|---|
| `name=template` | a field built from the template |
| `name` | copy of the record field, if present |
| `*` | all fields of the record |
| `name=` (empty template) | an empty column, e.g. `note=` for your own comments |

**Template** — `{f}` a record field · `{a\|b}` the first non-empty of `a`, `b` · `{f:x}` a number as hex · `{f*}` an array field, one output record per element. If a template needs a field the record lacks, the rule is skipped for that record. `t` is carried over by itself.

**Output** — no prefix: the result goes to `rec` only. `out:` — to the `out` port and to `rec`. `out!:` — to the `out` port only. Press *Apply outputs* to create the ports. `count` is reserved.

Examples (records are made up):

```
# {proto:'ble', src:'aa:bb', name:'Band', rssi:-60}  →  port ble: {id:'aa:bb', type:'ble', label:'Band', rssi:-60}
ble: proto=ble src | id={src}, type=ble, label={name|src}, rssi

# one frame → a node and a link, each to its own port only
node!: proto=wifi src | id={src}, type=station, label={src}
link!: proto=wifi src bssid | id={src}>{bssid}, from={src}, to={bssid}, label={kind}

# {id:'250-01-5', cellArfcns:[10,20]}  →  two records: ARFCN 10, ARFCN 20
arfcn: cellArfcns | id=ARFCN {cellArfcns*}, label=ch {cellArfcns*}

# only beacons and probe responses
ap!: proto=wifi kind=beacon|probe-resp bssid | id={bssid}, type=ap, label={ssid|bssid}

# only broadcast frames
bc!: proto=wifi dst=ff:ff:ff:ff:ff:ff | id={dst}, type=broadcast
```

**Grouping and counting** is not done by the node: it only turns records into records. Put a **Table** with *upsert key col* = `id` (or **Rec: Unique by Key**) after the port — a repeated `id` updates its row and keeps `count`, `first` and `last`. What you put into `id` is the grouping: `{src}` — per device, `ch{chan}` — per channel, `{src}>{dst}` — per pair. Sums and averages need another node.

*insert rules* adds ready sets (GSM cells, Digital voice, Radio, Wi-Fi map) — a good place to start; the *Entities: …* presets use them.

### Dashboard tiles

The ▦ button switches to a tiled dashboard built from the modules of the current patch:

- Pick any module for each pane from its dropdown
- **Tabs in a pane**: **+** next to the dropdown adds a tab, so one pane switches between several modules; the dropdown belongs to the active tab, ⨯ closes it
- Split panes right (⬌) or down (⬍), remove them (✕), drag dividers to resize; panes sit edge to edge, 1px dividers
- **⊡** or a double-click (double-tap) on an empty spot of the pane header maximizes the pane; the same restores the layout
- In narrow panes and on touch the pane buttons fold into **⋯**
- Module displays follow the light theme too (light screen, dark ink); the spectrum analyzer waterfall, persistence, the Map and Video Overlay stay dark
- ⛶ shows only the module's display, without controls, header, frames or selection; the button stays in the pane header on touch too
- **✎** (pane menu, or in a module's header on the canvas) gives a module its own name — it shows in the header, the pane dropdown and the tab (empty name — the default one)
- With no module graph pane on the page, the editing buttons of the toolbar (undo / redo, duplicate, chain, clear, fit, detail) are hidden
- **▶** also asks for the camera and motion sensors at once for modules that need them (Camera, Orientation, Accelerometer, Sensor), so a Video Overlay shows the picture right away
- **Tile pages**: **+** next to ▦ adds another set of tiles; switch pages with the numbered buttons, tap the active one to rename, duplicate, reorder or delete it. A module can appear on several pages
- The layout is saved with the patch and works on touch devices (wide scroll rail for long panes)

### Tile layouts

The **Tile Layouts** preset category loads a patch together with a ready dashboard layout (switch on ▦ after loading): *Modular Rack*, *Compact Pattern Editor*, *Receiver Console*, *Beat Grid*, *Bench Instruments*, *Digital Modes Desk*. Each is a starting point for your own arrangement.

<img width="400" src="docs/screenshots/t-6e6768dc-dark.png" alt="layout 1"> <img width="400" src="docs/screenshots/t-cf25a530-dark.png" alt="layout 2"> <img width="400" src="docs/screenshots/t-ef928755-dark.png" alt="layout 3"> <img width="400" src="docs/screenshots/t-530b8264-dark.png" alt="layout 4"> <img width="400" src="docs/screenshots/t-08174f7a-dark.png" alt="layout 5"> <img width="400" src="docs/screenshots/t-93e4cc36-dark.png" alt="layout 6"> 

### Parameter groups

Modules with many settings (USB SDR, Spectrum Analyzer, Video Overlay, Map, Table, Oscilloscope, Logic Analyzer, IQ Generator, Data Sequencer, Camera, tinySA) sort them into collapsible sections with a header and a count: tap a header to fold or unfold it, the state is saved with the patch. The everyday settings sit in open groups, the rare ones (recording, sweep, markers, waterfall…) in closed ones. A full label that did not fit a row shows on hover.

A module opts in with one line after its definition: `defGroups('id',[{n:'Receiver', p:'sr demod bw'}, {n:'Sweep', p:'sweep swLo swHi', open:false}])` — parameter names per group, groups in the order given, parameters outside any group stay on top. Modules without it look as before.

**Main controls only** (▤ in the pane header, shown for modules that mark their main parameters — USB SDR, Spectrum Analyzer): the pane keeps just those rows and hides the groups, so the display gets the room. A module marks them with `defGroups('id',[…],{pri:'demod bw gainDb'})`. The choice is saved with the layout.

<img src="docs/screenshots/param-groups-dash.png" alt="Parameter groups in a tile">

<img src="docs/screenshots/param-compact-dash.png" alt="Main controls only in a tile">

### On a phone

- **Screen stays on** while the graph runs (Settings → *Keep screen on*, on by default; needs the Screen Wake Lock API).
- **Background audio**: while running, a silent media element is attached to the audio engine, so the browser treats the page as playing media (it is not put to sleep with the screen off) and the lock screen shows play / pause. Without a sound output Chrome may still throttle the page. If the browser suspends the audio context (screen lock, a call, iOS *interrupted*), it is resumed on return; on iOS/Safari the audio session is set to *playback*.
- **Back gesture** closes what is open — the sidebar sheet, a maximized tile, ⛶ canvas-only mode, the settings panel, the module search — instead of leaving the app.
- **Files from other apps**: in the installed app, *Share → DSP* or *Open with → DSP* takes CSV / TSV / TXT / GPX / KML / GeoJSON / lists into a Table (a new one if the patch has none) and loads a patch `.json` (Chromium on Android; file handlers also on desktop).
- **Share out**: Settings → *Patch → Share…* and Table `⋯` → *Share this list…* open the system share menu (a plain download where it is not available).
- **Fullscreen**: the installed app opens without the status bar (`display_override`); reinstall it after an update of the manifest.

### Module graph pane

Pick **◇ Module graph** in a pane's dropdown and that pane becomes a window into the regular node canvas — like the modules window in SunVox, with the modules' own controls in the panes around it.

- Nodes look and work exactly as outside the dashboard: drag, wire, pan, zoom (wheel or two fingers)
- A module opened in another pane stays on the graph as a dashed stub with its ports, so its wires are visible and can be re-patched
- **⊕** or a double-click on an empty spot opens module search (type, ↑/↓, Enter); **⤢** fits the patch into the pane
- One graph pane per tile page; it is saved with the layout

<img src="docs/screenshots/desktop-modgraph-dark.png" alt="Module graph pane with an acid bass and drum sequencer in neighbouring panes">

<img src="docs/screenshots/desktop-modpick-dark.png" alt="Adding a module from the graph pane: search popup">

## Proxy for KiwiSDR and lists

The **CORS proxy** from the settings panel is used for list downloads (Satellites, Station Schedule, SDR Receivers Directory…) and for the **KiwiSDR** and **OpenWebRX** nodes (OpenWebRX: `http://host:port/ws/`). For Kiwi the address `http://host:port/<ts>/SND` is substituted into the proxy URL the same way (`{url}` is replaced by the encoded address, otherwise the address is appended) and the scheme `http(s)` becomes `ws(s)`, so the proxy must pass WebSocket. Without a proxy the node connects to `ws://host:port` directly, which an https page cannot do (mixed content).

nginx, setting `https://example.com/proxy/` (the address is appended):

```nginx
# http {} block
map $http_upgrade $connection_upgrade { default upgrade; '' close; }

# server {} block
location ~ ^/proxy/(?<sch>https?):/+(?<hst>[^/]+)(?<pth>/.*)?$ {
    resolver 1.1.1.1;
    proxy_pass $sch://$hst$pth$is_args$args;
    proxy_http_version 1.1;
    proxy_set_header Host $hst;
    proxy_set_header Upgrade $http_upgrade;
    proxy_set_header Connection $connection_upgrade;
    proxy_ssl_server_name on;
    proxy_read_timeout 3600s;
    add_header Access-Control-Allow-Origin * always;
}
```

This is an open proxy to any host: restrict it (`allow`/`deny`, basic auth, or a whitelist of hosts) before exposing it. Lists need the CORS header, Kiwi only the WebSocket upgrade.

## Proxy setup (nginx)

The **CORS proxy** from the settings panel is used for list downloads (Satellites, Station Schedule, SDR Receivers Directory…) and for the **KiwiSDR** node. For Kiwi the address `http://host:port/<ts>/SND` is substituted into the proxy URL the same way (`{url}` is replaced by the encoded address, otherwise the address is appended) and the scheme `http(s)` becomes `ws(s)`, so the proxy must pass WebSocket. Without a proxy the node connects to `ws://host:port` directly, which an https page cannot do (mixed content).

nginx, setting `https://example.com/proxy/` (the address is appended):

```nginx
# http {} block
map $http_upgrade $connection_upgrade { default upgrade; '' close; }

# server {} block
location ~ ^/proxy/(?<sch>https?):/+(?<hst>[^/]+)(?<pth>/.*)?$ {
    resolver 1.1.1.1 ipv6=off;
    proxy_pass $sch://$hst$pth$is_args$args;
    proxy_http_version 1.1;
    proxy_set_header Host $hst;
    proxy_set_header Upgrade $http_upgrade;
    proxy_set_header Connection $connection_upgrade;
    proxy_ssl_server_name on;
    proxy_read_timeout 3600s;
    add_header Access-Control-Allow-Origin * always;
}
```

This is an open proxy to any host: restrict it (`allow`/`deny`, basic auth, or a whitelist of hosts) before exposing it. Lists need the CORS header, Kiwi only the WebSocket upgrade.
